[math.RT] 15 Jun 2021 Strong positivity for quantum theta bases of quantum cluster algebras Ben Davison Address: School of Mathematics University of Edinburgh Edinburgh EH9 3FD UK Email address: Ben.Davison@ed.ac.uk and Travis Mandel Address: Department of Mathematics University of Oklahoma Norman, OK 73019 USA Email address: tmandel@ou.edu Abstract. We construct “quantum theta bases,” extending the set of quantum cluster monomials, for various versions of skew-symmetric quantum cluster algebras. These bases consist precisely of the indecomposable universally positive elements of the algebras they generate, and the structure constants for their multiplication are Laurent polynomials in the quantum parameter with non-negative integer coefficients, proving the quantum strong cluster positivity conjecture for these algebras. The classical limits recover the theta bases considered by Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich [ GHKK18 ] . Our approach combines the scattering diagram techniques used in loc. cit. with the Donaldson–Thomas theory of quivers. Contents 1 Introduction 2 Quantum scattering diagrams 3 Broken lines and theta bases 4 Quantum cluster algebras 5 Reduction of quantum positivity to the two-wall cases 6 Positivity for Donaldson–Thomas invariants 7 Stability scattering diagrams and the basic two-wall cases A Mutation invariance References 1. Introduction Cluster algebras, now a topic of significant, wide-ranging interest, were originally defined by Fomin and Zelevinsky [ FZ02 ] with the goal of better understanding Lusztig’s dual canonical bases [ Lus90 ] and total positivity [ Lus94 ] . It is therefore no surprise that questions about canonical bases for cluster algebras and their positivity properties are fundamental to the theory, cf. [ FG09 , §4] . These questions were largely answered in the classical setup by Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich [ GHKK18 ] . However, the quantum setup presents new challenges, particularly in the proof of positivity. The present paper uses ideas from Donaldson–Thomas (DT) theory [ KS , Kel11 , Nag13 , DM20 , Bri17 , MR19 ] in concert with scattering diagram technology [ KS06 , Gro10 , GS11 , CPS , FS15 , KS14 , GHK15b , GHKK18 , Man ] to prove positivity in the quantum setting. We construct bases for various flavors of (skew-symmetric) quantum cluster 𝒜 \mathcal{A} - and 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -algebras, extending the set of quantum cluster variables, such that the structure constants for the multiplication are Laurent polynomials in the quantum parameter with non-negative integer coefficients, proving the “quantum strong positivity conjecture.” Moreover we prove that these bases consist precisely of the universally positive indecomposable elements of these algebras (in particular reproving the “quantum positivity conjecture” originally proved in [ Dav18 ] ). Furthermore, we use our constructions and results for quantum scattering diagrams to prove quantum analogues of all the main results of [ GHKK18 ] for skew-symmetric quantum cluster algebras. 1.1. Main results In order to more fully state our main results, we briefly sketch the various constructions of (skew-symmetric) quantum cluster algebras, cf. § 4.2 for details. Let L L be a lattice with a rational skew-symmetric bilinear form ω \omega , and pick D ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 D\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} such that D ​ ω D\omega is ℤ \mathbb{Z} -valued. This data determines a noncommutative algebra ℤ t ω ​ [ L ] {\mathbb{Z}}_{t}^{\omega}[L] over ℤ t ≔ ℤ [ t ± 1 / D ] {\mathbb{Z}}_{t}\coloneqq{\mathbb{Z}}[t^{\pm 1/D}] , generated by elements z v z^{v} for v ∈ L v\in L , with relations determined by z a ​ z b = t ω ⁡ ( a , b ) ​ z a + b z^{a}z^{b}=t^{\omega(a,b)}z^{a+b} for a , b ∈ L a,b\in L . We call elements of ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} positive if their coefficients are in ℤ ≥ 0 \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . A choice of strongly convex rational polyhedral cone σ \upsigma in L ℝ ≔ L ⊗ ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq L\otimes\mathbb{R} determines a monoid L ⊕ ≔ L ∩ σ L^{\oplus}\coloneqq L\cap\upsigma and a formal completion ℤ σ , t ω ​ ⟦ L ⟧ ≔ ℤ t ω ​ [ L ] ⊗ ℤ t ω ​ [ L ⊕ ] ℤ t ω ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ {\mathbb{Z}}_{\upsigma,t}^{\omega}\llbracket L\rrbracket\coloneqq{\mathbb{Z}}_{t}^{\omega}[L]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{t}^{\omega}[L^{\oplus}]}{\mathbb{Z}}_{t}^{\omega}\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket consisting of quantum Laurent series. The algebra 𝒜 q ord \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord} is generated by the usual quantum cluster variables as in [ BZ05 ] , or equivalently, by the (quantum) global monomials defined analogously to the construction of 𝒳 q ord \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\ord} . We denote L 𝒳 ≔ N L_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq N and L 𝒜 ≔ M L_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq M . Let ω 𝒳 ≔ B \omega_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq B and ω 𝒜 ≔ Λ \omega_{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\Uplambda . For convenience, we now begin using 𝒱 \mathcal{V} as a stand-in for either 𝒜 \mathcal{A} or 𝒳 \mathcal{X} so that we can describe both cases at once. Our construction of theta bases for the 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -algebras will involve the construction of a scattering diagram 𝔇 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} in L 𝒱 , ℝ ≔ L 𝒱 ⊗ ℝ L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq L_{\mathcal{V}}\otimes\mathbb{R} . Each generic point 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} in L 𝒱 , ℝ L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} then determines an isomorphism (1) ι 𝒬 : 𝒱 ^ q S ⟶ ∼ ℤ σ S , 𝒱 , t ω 𝒱 ​ ⟦ L 𝒱 ⟧ , \displaystyle\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{S}_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{V}},t}^{\omega_{\mathcal{V}}}\llbracket L_{\mathcal{V}}\rrbracket, cf. § 4.7 . We refer to the set of isomorphisms ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} for generic 𝒬 ∈ L 𝒱 , ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} as the scattering atlas for 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{S}_{q} . So for each f ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S f\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} and each generic 𝒬 ∈ L 𝒱 , ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} , we can use ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} to obtain an expansion ι 𝒬 ​ ( f ) = ∑ v ∈ L 𝒱 a v , 𝒬 ​ z v ∈ ℤ σ S , 𝒱 , t ω 𝒱 ​ ⟦ L 𝒱 ⟧ , \displaystyle\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(f)=\sum_{v\in L_{\mathcal{V}}}a_{v,\mathcal{Q}}z^{v}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{V}},t}^{\omega_{\mathcal{V}}}\llbracket L_{\mathcal{V}}\rrbracket, where each a v , 𝒬 a_{v,\mathcal{Q}} is an element of ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} . We say that a nonzero element f ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S f\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{S}_{q} is universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas if each a v , 𝒬 a_{v,\mathcal{Q}} (for each v v and each generic 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} ) is positive. A universally positive element is called atomic (or indecomposable) if it cannot be written as a sum of two other universally positive elements. In fact, for 𝒱 = 𝒳 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{X} , we can define an isomorphism ι 𝒬 : 𝒳 ^ q S ⟶ ∼ ℤ σ S , 𝒳 , t B ​ ⟦ L 𝒳 ⟧ \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}},t}^{B}\llbracket L_{\mathcal{X}}\rrbracket for any generic 𝒬 ∈ L 𝒜 prin , ℝ ⊃ L 𝒳 , ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathcal{A}^{\prin},\mathbb{R}}\supset L_{\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R}} , cf. § 4.7 . Here, L 𝒜 prin = M prin = M ⊕ N L_{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}}=M^{\prin}=M\oplus N is the analogue of L 𝒜 L_{\mathcal{A}} associated to S prin S^{\prin} , i.e., the seed with principal coefficients associated to S S . We refer to this set of isomorphisms ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} as the principal coefficients scattering atlas. Note that the notions of universally positive and atomic elements make sense for this atlas as well. Theorem 1.1 . Let 𝒱 \mathcal{V} denote either 𝒜 \mathcal{A} or 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . For p 1 , p 2 , p ∈ L 𝒱 p_{1},p_{2},p\in L_{\mathcal{V}} , let α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) ∈ ℤ t \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{t} denote the corresponding structure constant, i.e., (3) ϑ p 1 ​ ϑ p 2 = ∑ p ∈ L 𝒱 α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) ​ ϑ p . \vartheta_{p_{1}}\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\sum_{p\in L_{\mathcal{V}}}\upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p)\vartheta_{p}. Then each α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) ∈ ℤ t \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p)\in\mathbb{Z}_{t} is positive (we thus call the theta basis “strongly positive”). Furthermore, α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p 1 + p 2 ) = t ω ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 ) \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p_{1}+p_{2})=t^{\omega(p_{1},p_{2})} , and α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) = 0 \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p)=0 whenever p ∉ p 1 + p 2 + L 𝒱 ⊕ p\notin p_{1}+p_{2}+L_{\mathcal{V}}^{\oplus} . Using the above quantum theta bases, we produce quantum analogues of all of the main results of [ GHKK18 ] regarding the algebras associated to theta bases, in particular the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} and 𝒳 \mathcal{X} cluster algebras. Here we collect together all of the main features and our results on these bases and algebras for the convenience of the reader. Let K 𝒳 ≔ ℤ ⟨ e i : i ∈ F ⟩ ⊂ N K_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{i}\colon i\in F\rangle\subset N , and let κ 𝒳 : N → K 𝒳 \kappa_{\mathcal{X}}\colon N\rightarrow K_{\mathcal{X}} denote the projection with kernel ℤ ⟨ e i : i ∈ I ∖ F ⟩ \mathbb{Z}\langle e_{i}\colon i\in I\setminus F\rangle . Consider the maps B 1 , B 2 : N → M B_{1},B_{2}\colon N\rightarrow M , B 1 ​ ( n ) ≔ B ⁡ ( n , ⋅ ) B_{1}(n)\coloneqq B(n,\cdot) and B 2 ​ ( n ) ≔ B ⁡ ( ⋅ , n ) B_{2}(n)\coloneqq B(\cdot,n) . E.g., if ω 𝒱 ​ ( u , p ) = 0 \omega_{\mathcal{V}}(u,p)=0 , then τ u ​ ( z p ) = z u ​ z p \uptau_{u}(z^{p})=z^{u}z^{p} . Theorem 1.2 . (1) The classical limits of the quantum theta functions are the classical theta functions of [ GHKK18 , Thm. 0.3] . (2) Let Θ 𝒱 mid ⊂ L 𝒱 \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd}\subset L_{\mathcal{V}} be the set of p p such that ϑ p \vartheta_{p} is a Laurent polynomial (as opposed to merely a formal Laurent series). Then ϑ p ∈ 𝒱 q up \vartheta_{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} for p ∈ Θ 𝒱 mid p\in\Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd} . The set Θ 𝒱 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd} is the same as in the corresponding classical cluster algebra setup of [ GHKK18 , Thm. 0.3] . In particular, Θ 𝒱 mid = Θ 𝒱 , ℝ mid ∩ L 𝒱 \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd}=\Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}}\cap L_{\mathcal{V}} for some cone Θ 𝒱 , ℝ mid ⊂ L 𝒱 , ℝ \Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}}\subset L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} which is convex (but not necessarily strongly convex, rational polyhedral, or closed) and remains convex under the application of any of the piecewise-linear automorphisms T ȷ → 𝒱 T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}} of L 𝒱 , ℝ L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} as defined in ( 66 ) and ( 112 ). (3) The theta functions { ϑ p : p ∈ Θ 𝒱 mid } \{\vartheta_{p}\colon p\in\Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd}\} form a ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -module basis for the ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -subalgebra 𝒱 q mid \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\midd} of 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} which they generate. Every global monomial is a theta function corresponding to some p ∈ Θ 𝒱 mid p\in\Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd} , so we have the inclusions 𝒱 q ord ⊂ 𝒱 q mid ⊂ 𝒱 q up ⊂ 𝒱 ^ q S . \displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\midd}\subset\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S}. (4) Let 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} denote the sub ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -algebra of 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} generated by the theta functions. The theta functions form a topological ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -module basis for 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} , and they form an ordinary ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -module basis if and only if the corresponding classical theta functions form a ℤ {\mathbb{Z}} -module basis for the ℤ {\mathbb{Z}} -algebra 𝒱 can \mathcal{V}^{\can} which they generate. If 𝒱 q \mathcal{V}_{q} has enough global monomials (cf. Definition 4.17 — this is equivalent to the corresponding condition in the classical setup up as in [ GHKK18 , Def. 0.11] ), then 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} is finitely generated over ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} , and each element of 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} is a finite ℤ t \mathbb{Z}_{t} -linear combination of theta functions. In particular, we then have 𝒱 q up ⊂ 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up}\subset\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} . (5) We have the equality 𝒱 q mid = 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\midd}=\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} , i.e., Θ 𝒱 mid = L 𝒱 \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd}=L_{\mathcal{V}} , if and only if the corresponding equality holds in the classical setting. So if we furthermore have that 𝒱 q \mathcal{V}_{q} has enough global monomials, then the full quantum Fock–Goncharov conjecture holds, by which we mean that 𝒱 q mid = 𝒱 q up = 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\midd}=\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up}=\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} . (6) Up to canonical isomorphism ψ ȷ → 𝒱 \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}} , the ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -algebras 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} and 𝒱 q mid \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\midd} depend only on the mutation equivalence class of the initial seed, as do their theta functions (cf. Corollary A.4 for the precise statement). In addition, 𝒱 q can ⊂ 𝒱 ^ q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{\up} , the formal quantum upper cluster algebra defined in § A.3.1 . (7) The mutations μ S , ȷ → 𝒱 \upmu_{S,\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}} respect the K 𝒱 K_{\mathcal{V}} -gradings of 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} and 𝒱 ^ q S ȷ → \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} induced by κ 𝒱 \kappa_{\mathcal{V}} , thus implying that we have a K 𝒱 K_{\mathcal{V}} -grading on 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} . More generally, the adjoint action of any path-ordered product associated to 𝔇 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} (or 𝒟 𝒜 q prin \mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} when 𝒱 = 𝒳 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{X} ) respects the K 𝒱 K_{\mathcal{V}} -grading on 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} . Furthermore, the theta functions are homogeneous with respect to the K 𝒱 K_{\mathcal{V}} -grading on 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} . (8) Suppose that Λ \Uplambda satisfies the compatibility condition ( 4.1 ) for all i ∈ I i\in I (not just i ∈ I ∖ F i\in I\setminus F ). Then the map B 1 B_{1} induces homomorphisms B 1 : 𝒳 ^ q S → 𝒜 ^ q S B_{1}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} and B 1 : 𝒳 q ∗ → 𝒜 q ∗ B_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{q}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{q}^{*} for ∗ = ord , mid , up *=\ord,\midd,\up , or can \can . Furthermore, for any p ∈ L 𝒳 p\in L_{\mathcal{X}} , we have B 1 ​ ( ϑ p ) = ϑ B 1 ​ ( p ) B_{1}(\vartheta_{p})=\vartheta_{B_{1}(p)} . (9) For u ∈ H 𝒱 u\in H_{\mathcal{V}} , p ∈ L 𝒱 p\in L_{\mathcal{V}} , and generic 𝒬 ∈ L 𝒱 , ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} , we have ι 𝒬 ​ ( ϑ u + p ) = τ u ​ ( ι 𝒬 ​ ( ϑ p ) ) \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\vartheta_{u+p})=\uptau_{u}(\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\vartheta_{p})) . In particular, for u ∈ H 𝒱 u\in H_{\mathcal{V}} , ι 𝒬 ​ ( ϑ u ) = z u \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\vartheta_{u})=z^{u} for all generic 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , and so ϑ u ∈ 𝒱 q ord \vartheta_{u}\in\mathcal{V}^{\ord}_{q} . 1.2. The cluster atlas There is a fan 𝒞 \mathcal{C} forming a sub cone-complex of 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} (alternatively, of 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} , the scattering diagram associated to S prin S^{\prin} ), called the cluster complex, whose elements naturally correspond to the seeds S ȷ → S_{\vec{\jmath}} (cf. Proposition 4.9 ). For 𝒬 ȷ → \mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}} a generic point in the chamber corresponding to S ȷ → S_{\vec{\jmath}} , denote ι ȷ → ≔ ι 𝒬 ȷ → \upiota_{\vec{\jmath}}\coloneqq\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}} . This set of charts ι ȷ → \upiota_{\vec{\jmath}} is what we call the cluster atlas. In ( 68 ), we also define a certain linear automorphism ψ ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}} of M M which induces automorphisms of 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] \Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] , also denoted ψ ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}} . Proposition 4.9 (4) says that ψ ȷ → ∘ ι ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}} and μ S , ȷ → 𝒱 \upmu^{\mathcal{V}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}} agree on 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} , so the restriction of the cluster atlas to 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} is the usual set of clusters on the quantum upper cluster algebra. See § 4.7 for more details. In [ FG09 ] , the notion of being universally positive and atomic (called “extremal” there) is defined using the cluster atlas. The scattering atlas or principal coefficients scattering atlas typically includes more charts than the cluster atlas, so universal positivity with respect to the scattering atlas is a stronger condition. Indeed, it is often strictly stronger — [ LLZ14 , Thm 1.2] shows that the atomic elements would often be linearly dependent (at least in the classical setup) if we used the cluster atlas. Atomicity with respect to the scattering atlas was suggested and proven for the classical setting in [ Man17 ] . The quantum global monomials generating 𝒜 q ord \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord} are precisely those ϑ p \vartheta_{p} for p ∈ 𝒞 p\in\mathcal{C} , with the quantum cluster variables corresponding to the primitive generators for the rays of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} , and we have 𝒞 ⊂ Θ 𝒜 , ℝ mid \mathcal{C}\subset\Theta_{\mathcal{A},\mathbb{R}}^{\midd} , cf. Theorem 1.2 (3) above. Applying the statement from Theorem 1.1 that c p , v , 𝒬 ​ ( t − 1 ) = c p , v , 𝒬 ​ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ t ± 1 ] c_{p,v,\mathcal{Q}}(t^{-1})=c_{p,v,\mathcal{Q}}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[t^{\pm 1}] in the special case in which both p p and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} are in 𝒞 \mathcal{C} , we thus deduce the main result of [ Dav18 ] : Corollary 1.3 (Quantum positivity conjecture) . The expression of any quantum cluster variable in any cluster is a bar-invariant Laurent polynomial in t t with non-negative integer coefficients. Taking the classical limit of Corollary 1.3 recovers the main result of [ LS15 ] , i.e. the positivity conjecture for skew-symmetric cluster algebras. In fact, [ Dav18 ] shows something more than Corollary 1.3 — it shows that the coefficients have Lefschetz type (as defined in § 2.1 ). This motivates our main conjecture: Conjecture 1.4 . For any p ∈ L 𝒱 p\in L_{\mathcal{V}} and generic 𝒬 ∈ L 𝒱 , ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} , the coefficients of ι 𝒬 ​ ( ϑ p ) \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\vartheta_{p}) have Lefschetz type. The main theorem of [ Dav18 ] ensures that Conjecture 1.4 holds for 𝒱 = 𝒜 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A} with 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} and p p both in the cluster complex. We show that the coefficients of ι 𝒬 ​ ( ϑ p ) \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\vartheta_{p}) always at least satisfy a weaker condition which we call pre-Lefschetz. Also, using a result of [ MR19 ] , we prove Conjecture 1.4 in the acyclic cases (i.e., for 𝒜 q can \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can} and 𝒳 q can \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\can} associated to seeds whose corresponding quiver has acyclic unfrozen part), cf. § 4.9 . Theorem 1.5 . Conjcture 1.4 holds in acyclic cases. 1.3. Dequantization As an application of our results, we generalize the main result of [ GLS20 ] , which we shall now recall. Note that setting t 1 / D t^{1/D} equal to 1 1 determines a homomorphism lim t → 1 : ℤ t ω ​ [ L ] → ℤ ⁡ [ L ] \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon{\mathbb{Z}}_{t}^{\omega}[L]\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}[L] (for convenience, we write lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} instead of lim t 1 / D → 1 \lim_{t^{1/D}\rightarrow 1} ). This extends to the formal versions, giving a homomorphism lim t → 1 : ℤ σ , t ω ​ ⟦ L ⟧ → ℤ σ ​ ⟦ L ⟧ ≔ ℤ ⁡ [ L ] ⊗ ℤ ⁡ [ L ⊕ ] ℤ ⁡ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ . \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon{\mathbb{Z}}_{\upsigma,t}^{\omega}\llbracket L\rrbracket\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{\upsigma}\llbracket L\rrbracket\coloneqq{\mathbb{Z}}[L]\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}[L^{\oplus}]}{\mathbb{Z}}\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket. The images under this homomorphism are what we refer to as “classical limits” in Theorem 1.2 (1). For ∗ * denoting ord , mid , up \ord,\midd,\up , or can \can , the algebras 𝒱 q ∗ \mathcal{V}^{*}_{q} are ℤ t \mathbb{Z}_{t} -algebras, and torsion-free as ℤ t \mathbb{Z}_{t} -modules. As such it is natural to consider the “classical limits” cl ⁡ ( 𝒱 q ∗ ) ≔ 𝒱 q ∗ / ( t 1 / D − 1 ) ​ 𝒱 q ∗ \cl(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*})\coloneqq\mathcal{V}^{*}_{q}/(t^{1/D}-1)\mathcal{V}^{*}_{q} as dequantizations of the quantum cluster algebras that we are considering in this paper. On the other hand, there are already well-defined classical cluster algebras 𝒱 ∗ ⊂ ℤ σ S , 𝒱 ​ ⟦ L 𝒱 ⟧ \mathcal{V}^{*}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{V}}}\llbracket L_{\mathcal{V}}\rrbracket , and it is natural to ask whether these two dequantizations are the same. To approach this question we consider the restriction of lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} to 𝒱 q ∗ \mathcal{V}_{q}^{*} , which for each choice of ∗ * above has image contained in 𝒱 ∗ \mathcal{V}^{*} . The kernels of these homomorphisms of course always contain the ideal generated by ( t 1 / D − 1 ) (t^{1/D}-1) , but the reverse containment is not so obvious, i.e. it is not clear a priori that the induced map cl ⁡ ( 𝒱 q ∗ ) → 𝒱 ∗ \cl(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{q})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*} is injective. In fact, the main result of [ GLS20 ] is that the kernel of lim t → 1 : 𝒜 q ord → 𝒜 ord \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}:\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\ord} really is equal to the ideal ( t 1 / D − 1 ) ​ 𝒜 q ord (t^{1/D}-1)\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord} , and the natural map cl ⁡ ( 𝒜 q ord ) → 𝒜 ord \cl(\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\ord} is an isomorphism, assuming that 𝒜 ord = 𝒜 up \mathcal{A}^{\ord}=\mathcal{A}^{\up} , and further assuming that 𝒜 ord \mathcal{A}^{\ord} has a ℤ \mathbb{Z} -grading with finite-dimensional homogeneous components. As an application of our main results, we obtain a new proof of their result without the grading condition. More generally, we prove the following: Theorem 1.6 . Let 𝒱 \mathcal{V} denote 𝒜 \mathcal{A} or 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . Then lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} induces an injective morphism cl ⁡ ( 𝒱 q ∗ ) → 𝒱 ∗ \cl(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*} if ∗ = mid , up *=\midd,\up or can \can . The same holds for ∗ = ord *=\ord whenever 𝒱 ord = 𝒱 mid \mathcal{V}^{\ord}=\mathcal{V}^{\midd} (which at least holds when 𝒱 ord = 𝒱 up \mathcal{V}^{\ord}=\mathcal{V}^{\up} ). Moreover, the natural map cl ⁡ ( 𝒱 q ∗ ) → 𝒱 ∗ \cl(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*} is surjective if ∗ * = ord , mid \ord,\midd or can \can (and so it is surjective for ∗ = up *=\up if 𝒱 up = 𝒱 mid \mathcal{V}^{\up}=\mathcal{V}^{\midd} , which at least holds if 𝒱 ord = 𝒱 up \mathcal{V}^{\ord}=\mathcal{V}^{\up} ). Geiss–Leclerc–Schröer had suggested this application of quantum theta functions to the second author while [ GLS20 ] was in progress. Thanks to our positivity result, we are now able to show that their suggestion was correct. See § 4.9 for the proof. Remark 1.7 . The statement that cl ⁡ ( 𝒱 q ∗ ) → 𝒱 ∗ \cl(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*} is an isomorphism means that 𝒱 q ∗ \mathcal{V}_{q}^{*} is a deformation quantization of 𝒱 ∗ \mathcal{V}^{*} . By construction, the quantum cluster 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -variety of [ FG09 , §3] (and the quantum cluster 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -variety that could be analogously defined) is, in the sense of [ Bou20a , Def. 5] , a deformation quantization of the corresponding classical cluster variety. The surjectivity of cl ⁡ ( 𝒱 q up ) → 𝒱 up \cl(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\up} then follows if the cluster variety is affine (at least up to codimension 2 2 ), cf. [ Bou20a , Lem. 7] . 1.4. Additional properties and context for theta functions It is clear that being atomic as in Theorem 1.1 uniquely determines the theta functions, at least once the scattering atlas is given. It would be interesting to have additional characterizations of the theta bases which could be checked using only the cluster algebra structure. E.g., [ LLRZ14 , Conj. 14] suggests that the quantum theta basis { ϑ p : p ∈ ℒ 𝒱 } \{\vartheta_{p}\colon p\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{V}}\} may be the unique minimal topological ℤ t \mathbb{Z}_{t} -module basis amongst the set 𝒮 \mathcal{S} of all pointed, bar-invariant, strongly positive topological ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} -module bases for 𝒱 q can \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can} including all global monomials. Here, by minimal one means that f p − ϑ p ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S f_{p}-\vartheta_{p}\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} has positive coefficients whenever f p f_{p} is the p p -pointed element in another such basis. A standard argument (cf. [ LLRZ14 , §5] ) shows that for 𝒱 = 𝒜 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A} , any (not necessarily minimal) basis in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} is universally positive with respect to the cluster atlas. The conjecture of [ LLRZ14 ] would follow if every basis in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} was universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas. On the other hand, if the theta functions are atomic with respect to the cluster atlas, then it would follow that they are a minimal basis in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , though possibly not the unique minimal basis in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . In fact, [ LLRZ14 , Conj. 14] is stated for quantum greedy bases (which are defined for rank 2 2 quantum cluster algebras) instead of quantum theta bases, but we expect the two to be equal whenever both are defined — the classical analog of this equivalence was proved in [ CGM+17 ] . We also expect our bases to agree with those defined for quantum cluster algebras from surfaces as in [ AK17 ] — quantum Laurent positivity for these was proved in [ All , Thm 5.7] for disks and then in [ CKKO20 , Thm. 1.1] in general. We further expect agreement with a quantum version of the bracelets basis of [ MSW13 ] — this would prove [ Thu14 , Conj. 4.20] . On the other hand, the “triangular bases,” as considered for some quantum cluster algebras in [ Qin17 ] , seem to be different from ours — [ LLRZ14 , Conj. 13] suggests that they might be the maximal bases in 𝒮 \mathcal{S} . We note that our construction of the quantum theta functions was previously given in [ Man ] . However, all results on positivity properties and results stating that these theta functions really are related to the cluster algebras (as opposed to just the formal version 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} without the cluster structure) are new. Since our quantum theta functions agree with those of [ Man ] , we know that they satisfy the following: (1) Fock and Goncharov’s quantum Frobenius map conjecture [ FG09 , Conj. 4.8.6] , cf. [ Man , Thm. 4.3] ; (2) The structure constants of the form α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , 0 ) \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};0) and α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p 3 , 0 ) \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3};0) are sufficient to uniquely determine all the structure constants [ Man , Thm. 2.16] ; (3) All the structure constants and the scattering diagrams are determined by certain refined counts of tropical disks (which may have negative multiplicities). The classical versions of (2) and (3) above are used in [ Man21 ] to show that the classical theta functions are determined by certain descendant log Gromov–Witten numbers — in fact, all the classical structure constants admit Gromov–Witten theoretic descriptions [ KY , GS ] , and as [ KY ] notes, the classical strong positivity follows from the unobstructedness of the relevant moduli of curves. The anticipation of such mirror symmetry type statements was the motivation for the development of the techniques used in [ GHKK18 ] , cf. [ KS06 , GPS10 , GS11 , CPS , KS14 , GHK15b , GHK15a , GHS ] . It would be interesting to find similar geometric descriptions in the quantum setting. Indeed, it is known that the tropical disk counts relevant to rank 2 2 scattering diagrams are related to certain real/open Gromov–Witten invariants [ Mik17 ] or higher-genus Gromov–Witten invariants [ Bou19 , Bou20b ] , and we expect versions of such invariants to describe the quantum theta function multiplication more generally. On the other hand, from the perspective of representation theory, it would be desirable to understand positivity in terms of a monoidal categorification of the (quantum) cluster algebra, cf. [ HL10 , Nak11 , KKKO18 ] . In this perspective, the (quantum) cluster algebra is identified with the Grothendieck ring of a monoidal category, and we expect the theta functions to correspond to simple objects of the category — the atomicity statement of Theorem 1.1 is strong evidence for this. Coefficients of the multiplication should then arise as graded dimensions of vector spaces decomposing the tensor products of these simple objects, cf. [ All19 , §1.5.4] . Our arguments proceed by considering categorifications of the functions appearing on walls in scattering diagrams. We leave for the future the project of categorifying the theta functions themselves (and hence the cluster algebra). 1.5. Positivity for scattering diagrams from DT theory As in [ GHKK18 ] , we deduce our results regarding (quantum) cluster algebras from positivity results for scattering diagrams, which in turn are derived from positivity results in DT theory. We now explain how all of these positivity results fit together. First, note that the scattering diagram 𝔇 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} comes about in a particular way. Namely, we start with a simple, inconsistent scattering diagram 𝔇 in 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} for which the only walls are full hyperplanes v i ω ⟂ v_{i}^{\omega\perp} , and the functions on these walls are 𝔼 ⁡ ( − z v i ) \EE(-z^{v_{i}}) . See § 2.4 for the definition of 𝔼 \EE in terms of plethystic exponentials/quantum dilogarithms. To this initial scattering diagram of incoming walls, we then add outgoing walls to obtain the consistent scattering diagram 𝔇 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} (see Theorem 2.13 ). The important thing to note is that if all new walls carry functions of the form 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) ​ z v 𝔡 ) \EE(-p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t)z^{v_{\mathfrak{d}}}) for p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) ∈ ℤ t p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{t} positive, then the coefficients c p , v , 𝒬 ​ ( t ) c_{p,v,\mathcal{Q}}(t) and α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p) appearing in Theorem 1.1 are positive elements of ℤ t {\mathbb{Z}}_{t} as well. By the lemmas of § 3.2 , the same statement is true if we replace the property “positive” by “bar-invariant,” “pre-Lefschetz,” or “Lefschetz.” The constant polynomial p ⁡ ( t ) = 1 p(t)=1 enjoys all four of these properties, and so the functions on the initial walls satisfy all of these properties. So whichever of “positive,” “bar-invariant,” “pre-Lefschetz,” or “Lefschetz” we have chosen as the property we want to prove that theta functions possess, our job becomes to prove the corresponding preservation theorem for scattering diagrams, stating that when we start adding walls as in Theorem 2.13 in order to make 𝔇 in 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} consistent, we can proceed by only adding walls with functions that are positive/bar-invariant/pre-Lefschetz/Lefschetz. So far we are just recalling the strategy of [ GHKK18 ] for proving positivity of classical theta functions (the other three properties have no analog in the non-quantum setting). The precise form of our preservation theorem (regarding positive, bar-invariant, and pre-Lefschetz incoming functions) is given in Theorem 2.15 . For now we are concentrating on the positivity result, since that is the most fundamental. Similarly to [ GHKK18 , §C.3] , via perturbation arguments and various other tricks, the preservation theorem reduces to the case in which 𝔇 in 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}}_{\In} consists of only two walls, with attached functions 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t m 1 ​ z v 1 ) \EE(-t^{m_{1}}z^{v_{1}}) and 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t m 2 ​ z v 2 ) \EE(-t^{m_{2}}z^{v_{2}}) . In the classical case, there are no powers of t t to worry about, and as in [ GHKK18 , §C.3, Step IV] , one can further reduce to the case in which ω ⁡ ( v 1 , v 2 ) = 1 \omega(v_{1},v_{2})=1 , and so there is just one two-wall base case. It is straightforward to prove by hand that the consistent scattering diagram built out of this base case is positive — one only needs to add a single outgoing wall (by the pentagon identity), and its attached function is positive. In the quantum case, we have to allow ω ⁡ ( v 1 , v 2 ) \omega(v_{1},v_{2}) to take any value in ℤ ≥ 1 \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} , and furthermore the problem turns out to depend fundamentally on the parities of m 1 m_{1} and m 2 m_{2} . So we are confronted by four infinite families of base cases, almost all of which are extremely complicated (with infinitely many outgoing walls, almost always dense in some region of nonzero measure — see for instance Examples 7.9 and 7.10 in § 7.2.2 ). Our approach to proving positivity in all of these base cases is to translate the problem into the DT theory of quiver representations. The links between scattering diagrams, quantum dilogarithm identities and DT theory is well established (see [ Kel11 ] , [ KS14 ] or [ Bri17 ] for background). We show that the positivity problem in the base cases involves the DT invariants of a quiver depending on n = ω ⁡ ( v 1 , v 2 ) n=\omega(v_{1},v_{2}) , and the parities of m 1 m_{1} and m 2 m_{2} . In the case in which m 1 m_{1} and m 2 m_{2} are both even, this turns out to be a problem that has already been solved: positivity amounts to proving positivity of the DT invariants for the n n -Kronecker quiver, which has been established as a very special case of a positivity result of Meinhardt and Reineke regarding acyclic quivers [ MR19 ] using refined DT theory. In all three of the other infinite families, the situation is not so simple: the quiver for which we have to establish positivity of DT invariants involves loops, i.e. it is non-acyclic. To deal with these cases, we prove a generalization of the result of [ MR19 ] , namely the positivity of refined DT invariants for arbitrary quivers. This is the content of our Theorem 6.15 , which we restate here: Theorem 1.8 (Positivity for refined DT invariants) . Let ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} be a generic stability condition. Let S θ ζ ⊂ ℤ ≥ 0 Q 0 S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q_{0}} be the submonoid of dimension vectors of slope θ \uptheta with respect to ζ \upzeta . There is an equality of generating series ∑ v ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 Q 0 χ t ​ ( H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) ) ​ x v ​ t χ Q ​ ( v , v ) = ∏ ∞ → θ − ∞ ∏ 0 ≠ v ∈ S θ ζ 𝔼 ⁡ ( f v ​ ( t ) ​ x v ) \sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q_{0}}}\upchi_{t}(\HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q}))x^{v}t^{\upchi_{Q}(v,v)}=\prod_{\infty\xrightarrow{\uptheta}-\infty}\prod_{0\neq v\in S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}}\EE(f_{v}(t)x^{v}) where the f v ​ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ t ± 1 ] f_{v}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[t^{\pm 1}] have positive coefficients and are even or odd Laurent polynomials, depending on whether χ Q ​ ( v , v ) \upchi_{Q}(v,v) is odd or even, respectively, where χ Q ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \upchi_{Q}(\cdot,\cdot) is the Euler form for Q Q . This theorem turns out to be an easy consequence of the cohomological wall crossing and integrality theorems of [ DM20 ] . In fact, it follows from a very special case since we do not have to consider potentials (equivalently, we set W = 0 W=0 ). We hope that for the audience coming from the theory of cluster algebras, the absence of potentials and the resulting absence of arguments involving vanishing cycles and monodromic motives/mixed Hodge modules from e.g. [ Nag13 , Efi12 , Dav18 ] will make this paper more approachable. Using Theorem 1.8 , we can prove preservation of positivity for all four infinite families of 2-wall base cases (cf. Corollary 7.8 ) and deduce all of our other positivity results. By combining the parity statement of Theorem 1.8 with our positivity result for scattering diagrams and the consistency of Bridgeland’s Hall algebra scattering diagrams [ Bri17 ] , we prove a general parity statement for scattering diagrams, Theorem 2.19 . This then implies the parity statement c p , v , 𝒬 ​ ( − t ) = ± c p , v , 𝒬 ​ ( t ) c_{p,v,\mathcal{Q}}(-t)=\pm c_{p,v,\mathcal{Q}}(t) from Theorem 1.1 , cf. Proposition 3.11 . The strategy for proving preservation of the pre-Lefschetz property is the same as for the preservation of positivity. The main difference in the proof is that the preservation result for the basic two wall case (Proposition 7.12 ) can be proved by translating it into the DT theory of acyclic quivers, allowing us to use the Meinhardt–Reineke theorem to settle it. The pre-Lefschetz property implies positivity, and so this provides a second proof of all of the positivity results in this paper. 1.6. Logical structure of the paper We derive our main results as consequences of results on scattering diagrams and DT theory. On the other hand, we have deferred all of the DT theory until after the proofs of Theorems 1.1 and 1.2 at the end of Section § 4 , in an effort to not over-burden the reader who is primarily interested in cluster algebras. The result is that the logical structure of the paper is somewhat nonlinear, and so for the reader’s benefit we have drawn a map of it below. Since we have pared the paper down to its essentials in this diagram, we have ignored all results and parts of results regarding Lefschetz type/bar invariance/parity while drawing it. Section 4 Theta functions and quantum cluster algebras cor 4.13 , prop 4.15 global monomials are ϑ \vartheta -functions Section 3 Results on theta functions atomicity thm 3.16 Section 5 Reduction to rank 2 Theorem 1.2 Structure of quantum cluster algebras Theorem 1.1 Strong positivity+atomicity for quantum theta bases Theorem 2.15 Preservation of positivity for scattering diagrams positivity thms 3.9 and 3.10 Proposition 7.7 Positivity for stability scattering diagrams cor 7.8 2 wall case Theorem 6.15 Positivity for DT invariants of quivers 1.7. Notation Let 𝕜 \Bbbk be an arbitrary characteristic 0 0 field, or more generally, any commutative ring containing ℚ \mathbb{Q} . Denote 𝕜 t ≔ 𝕜 [ t ± 1 / D ] \Bbbk_{t}\coloneqq\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1/D}] for a fixed positive integer D D (we do not worry about the ambiguity of D D in this notation because one could instead just take 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} to include all fractional powers of t t ). For a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} , we denote (4) ( a ) t ≔ t a − t − a ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] , \displaystyle(a)_{t}\coloneqq t^{a}-t^{-a}\in\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}], and when a ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} we denote (5) [ a ] t ≔ ( a ) t ( 1 ) t = t a − t − a t − t − 1 = t − a + 1 + t − a + 3 + … + t a − 3 + t a − 1 ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] . \displaystyle[a]_{t}\coloneqq\frac{(a)_{t}}{(1)_{t}}=\frac{t^{a}-t^{-a}}{t-t^{-1}}=t^{-a+1}+t^{-a+3}+\ldots+t^{a-3}+t^{a-1}\in\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}]. We will use t t throughout to denote the quantum deformation parameter, avoiding q 1 / 2 q^{1/2} , as the meaning of this parameter is ambiguous 2 2 2 See e.g. [ DMSS15 , Rem 4.25] for a discussion of this point. up to a sign. We say p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is even if p ⁡ ( t ) = p ⁡ ( − t ) p(t)=p(-t) and odd if p ⁡ ( t ) = − p ⁡ ( − t ) p(t)=-p(-t) . We define the parity function on the set of polynomials satisfying p ⁡ ( − t ) = ± p ⁡ ( t ) p(-t)=\pm p(t) by par ⁡ ( p ⁡ ( t ) ) = { 0 if ​ p ​ ( t ) ​ is even 1 if ​ p ​ ( t ) ​ is odd. \parit(p(t))=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }p(t)\textrm{ is even}\\ 1&\textrm{if }p(t)\textrm{ is odd.}\end{cases} Note that par ⁡ ( [ a ] t ) ≡ a + 1 \parit([a]_{t})\equiv a+1 (mod 2 2 ). We say that a polynomial p ⁡ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] p(t)\in\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}] is of Lefschetz type if it is a sum of the polynomials [ n ] t [n]_{t} for n ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} . For n ∈ ℤ n\in\mathbb{Z} we define (6) 𝒫 n ⁡ ( t ) = { t n if ​ n ​ is even t n − 1 ​ [ 2 ] t = t n − 2 + t n if ​ n ​ is odd. \pl_{n}(t)=\begin{cases}t^{n}&\textrm{if }n\textrm{ is even}\\ t^{n-1}[2]_{t}=t^{n-2}+t^{n}&\textrm{if }n\textrm{ is odd.}\end{cases} and we say that p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is pre-Lefschetz , or just pL , if it is a sum of polynomials 𝒫 n ⁡ ( t ) \pl_{n}(t) for n ∈ ℤ n\in\mathbb{Z} . A Lefschetz type polynomial is pL. We say that p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is bar-invariant if it is invariant under the transformation t ↦ t − 1 t\mapsto t^{-1} . 2.1.2. The Lefschetz property The origin of Lefschetz polynomials in geometry is as follows. Let X X be an irreducible variety, and assume that for some complex of constructible sheaves ℱ \mathcal{F} on X X there are isomorphisms H i ⁡ ( X , ℱ ) ≅ H 2 ​ dim ( X ) − i ⁡ ( X , ℱ ) ∗ \HO^{i}(X,\mathcal{F})\cong\HO^{2\dim(X)-i}(X,\mathcal{F})^{*} for all i ∈ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z} . The classical example would be given by setting ℱ \mathcal{F} to be the constant sheaf ℚ X \mathbb{Q}_{X} , where X X is smooth projective — then the above isomorphism is given by Poincaré duality. We normalise the cohomology of X X with coefficients ℱ \mathcal{F} by writing V = H ⁡ ( X , ℱ ) vir ≔ H ⁡ ( X , ℱ ) ​ [ dim ( X ) ] . V=\HO(X,\mathcal{F})_{\vir}\coloneqq\HO(X,\mathcal{F})[\dim(X)]. Then the Poincaré polynomial (7) χ t ​ ( V ) ≔ ∑ i ∈ ℤ dim ( V i ) ​ t i \displaystyle\upchi_{t}(V)\coloneqq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\dim(V^{i})t^{i} is bar-invariant. Assume that, moreover, V V carries an operator ℒ : V → V ⁡ [ − 2 ] \mathscr{L}\colon V\rightarrow V[-2] , i.e. a linear map ℒ \mathscr{L} of cohomological degree 2, such that for every i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 the linear map ℒ i : V − i → V i \mathscr{L}^{i}\colon V^{-i}\rightarrow V^{i} is an isomorphism. Then χ t ​ ( V ) \upchi_{t}(V) is of Lefschetz type. In the classical example mentioned above, this isomorphism is provided by the hard Lefschetz theorem, and the operator ℒ \mathcal{L} is the Lefschetz operator, defined by capping with a hyperplane section. 2.2. Quantum tori 2.2.1. The quantum torus algebra Given any finite-rank lattice L L , we write L ℚ ≔ L ⊗ ℚ L_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq L\otimes\mathbb{Q} and L ℝ ≔ L ⊗ ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq L\otimes\mathbb{R} . We denote the dual pairing between L L and its dual lattice L ∗ ≔ Hom ⁡ ( L , ℤ ) L^{*}\coloneqq\Hom(L,\mathbb{Z}) by ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle . We call a nonzero vector n ∈ L n\in L primitive if it is not a positive integer multiple of any other element of L L . If m = k ​ m ′ m=km^{\prime} for k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} and m ′ ∈ L m^{\prime}\in L primitive, we call k k the index of m m , denoted | m | |m| . Let L L denote a lattice of finite rank r r , equipped with a 1 D ​ ℤ \frac{1}{D}\mathbb{Z} -valued skew-symmetric bilinear form ω \omega for some D ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 D\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} (we take 𝕜 t ≔ 𝕜 [ t ± 1 / D ] \Bbbk_{t}\coloneqq\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1/D}] for this D D ). We consider the quantum torus algebra (8) 𝕜 t [ L ] ≔ 𝕜 t ⟨ z v : v ∈ L ⟩ / ⟨ z a z b = t ω ⁡ ( a , b ) z a + b : a , b ∈ L ⟩ . \displaystyle\Bbbk_{t}[L]\coloneqq\Bbbk_{t}\langle z^{v}\colon v\in L\rangle/\langle z^{a}z^{b}=t^{\omega(a,b)}z^{a+b}\colon a,b\in L\rangle. This is a unital associative algebra, which is noncommutative if ω \omega is nonzero. Note that 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] is L L -graded. We may write 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] as 𝕜 t ω ​ [ L ] \Bbbk^{\omega}_{t}[L] if ω \omega is not clear from context. Now fix a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone σ ⊂ L ℝ \upsigma\subset L_{\mathbb{R}} . Let L ⊕ ≔ σ ∩ L L^{\oplus}\coloneqq\upsigma\cap L , and L + ≔ L ⊕ ∖ { 0 } L^{+}\coloneqq L^{\oplus}\setminus\{0\} . For each k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} , let (9) k L + ≔ { v 1 + … + v k ∈ L : v i ∈ L + for each i = 1 , … , k } . \displaystyle kL^{+}\coloneqq\{v_{1}+\ldots+v_{k}\in L\colon v_{i}\in L^{+}\mbox{ for each }i=1,\ldots,k\}. We consider the formal Laurent series ring 𝕜 σ , t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ ≔ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] ⊗ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ⊕ ] 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ \Bbbk_{\upsigma,t}\llbracket L\rrbracket\coloneqq\Bbbk_{t}[L]\otimes_{\Bbbk_{t}[L^{\oplus}]}\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket , i.e., 𝕜 σ , t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{\upsigma,t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is spanned by formal sums of the form ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ a v ​ z v + w \sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}}a_{v}z^{v+w} for w ∈ L w\in L and coefficients a v ∈ 𝕜 t a_{v}\in\Bbbk_{t} . Where the cone σ \upsigma is clear from the context or not explicitly important, we will omit it from the notation, writing 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ ≔ 𝕜 σ , t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket\coloneqq\Bbbk_{\upsigma,t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . For p ∈ L p\in L , we say f ∈ 𝕜 σ , t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ f\in\Bbbk_{\upsigma,t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is p p -pointed if it has the form f = z p ​ ( 1 + ∑ v ∈ L + a v ​ z v ) \displaystyle f=z^{p}\left(1+\sum_{v\in L^{+}}a_{v}z^{v}\right) with a v ∈ 𝕜 t a_{v}\in\Bbbk_{t} . 2.2.2. Topological structure In general, the theta functions will only provide topological bases, roughly meaning that we may need infinite linear combinations of the theta functions in order to reach every element of the algebras we consider. We carefully review this notion here. For the purposes of the following, we endow 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} with the discrete topology. We make 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] into a topological 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module as follows: let 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} denote the unique maximal monomial ideal of 𝕜 t ​ [ L ⊕ ] \Bbbk_{t}[L^{\oplus}] , i.e., 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} is spanned by monomials z v z^{v} for v ∈ L + v\in L^{+} , and so 𝔪 k \mathfrak{m}^{k} is spanned by monomials z v z^{v} for v ∈ k ​ L + v\in kL^{+} . Let 𝔘 0 \mathfrak{U}_{0} denote the set of 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -submodules U ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] U\subset\Bbbk_{t}[L] which are closed under left (equivalently, right) multiplication by elements of 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} and such that for every f ∈ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] f\in\Bbbk_{t}[L] , there exists some k k such that 𝔪 k ​ f ⊂ U \mathfrak{m}^{k}f\subset U . Let 𝔘 \mathfrak{U} denote the set of cosets of these submodules, i.e., 𝔘 ≔ { a + U : a ∈ 𝕜 t [ L ] , U ∈ 𝔘 0 } \mathfrak{U}\coloneqq\{a+U\colon a\in\Bbbk_{t}[L],\medspace U\in\mathfrak{U}_{0}\} . We consider the topology on 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] with basis of open sets 𝔘 \mathfrak{U} . Note that 𝔘 0 \mathfrak{U}_{0} is a neighborhood basis for the point 0 0 , and the intersection of the sets in 𝔘 0 \mathfrak{U}_{0} is zero, so the topology is Hausdorff. For each S ⊂ L S\subset L , denote U S ≔ ⨁ v ∈ S 𝕜 t ⋅ z v ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] . \displaystyle U_{S}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{v\in S}\Bbbk_{t}\cdot z^{v}\subset\Bbbk_{t}[L]. Then the 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -submodule U S ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] U_{S}\subset\Bbbk_{t}[L] is open if S S satisfies the following two conditions: (1) S S is closed under addition by elements of L ⊕ L^{\oplus} ; (2) For all v ∈ L v\in L there exists some k k such that v + k ​ L + ⊂ S v+kL^{+}\subset S . It will sometimes be useful to let L ± L^{\pm} denote the ℤ \mathbb{Z} -span of L ⊕ L^{\oplus} in L L , and let π : L → L ¯ ≔ L / L ± \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt\colon L\rightarrow\overline{L}\coloneqq L/L^{\pm} denote the projection. Definition 2.1 . Let R R be a commutative ring, which we endow with the discrete topology, and let A A be a topological R R -module with a basis of open neighborhoods of 0 0 consisting of R R -submodules. By a Cauchy sequence in A A , we mean a sequence ( f k ) k (f_{k})_{k} of elements of A A , indexed by k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} , such that for any open neighborhood U U of 0 ∈ A 0\in A , we have f k 1 − f k 2 ∈ U f_{k_{1}}-f_{k_{2}}\in U for all sufficiently large k 1 , k 2 k_{1},k_{2} . Two Cauchy sequences ( f k ) k (f_{k})_{k} and ( g k ) k (g_{k})_{k} are then equivalent if, for each open neighborhood V V of 0 0 , we have f k − g k ∈ V f_{k}-g_{k}\in V for all sufficiently large k k . The assumption that 0 0 has a basis of neighborhoods that are modules ensures that this is an equivalence relation. Note that the Cauchy completion A ^ \widehat{A} of A A , i.e., the set of all equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences in A A , naturally inherits a topological R R -module structure from A A . More precisely, ( f k ) k + ( g k ) k = ( f k + g k ) k (f_{k})_{k}+(g_{k})_{k}=(f_{k}+g_{k})_{k} , and r ⋅ ( g k ) k = ( r ⋅ g k ) k r\cdot(g_{k})_{k}=(r\cdot g_{k})_{k} ; a subset U ^ ⊂ A ^ \widehat{U}\subset\widehat{A} is open if it is the set of all classes of Cauchy sequences equivalent to sequences of elements of some open set U ⊂ A U\subset A . Proposition 2.2 . With the above topology, 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is the Cauchy completion of 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] . Proof. Given an element f = ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ a v ​ z v + w ∈ z w ​ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ f=\sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}}a_{v}z^{v+w}\in z^{w}\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket\subset\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket , a v a_{v} denoting coefficients in 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} , we form the sequence of Laurent polynomials f k = ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ a v , k ​ z v + w ∈ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] f_{k}=\sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}}a_{v,k}z^{v+w}\in\Bbbk_{t}[L] via the rule a v , k = { a v if ​ v ∉ w + k ​ L + 0 otherwise. a_{v,k}=\begin{cases}a_{v}&\textrm{if }v\notin w+kL^{+}\\ 0&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{cases} Let U ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] U\subset\Bbbk_{t}[L] be an open set containing 0. Then there exists a k U ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k_{U}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} such that z w ​ 𝔪 k U ⊂ U z^{w}\mathfrak{m}^{k_{U}}\subset U , from which it follows that f k 1 − f k 2 ∈ U f_{k_{1}}-f_{k_{2}}\in U for all k 1 , k 2 ≥ k U k_{1},k_{2}\geq k_{U} , i.e., ( f k ) k (f_{k})_{k} is indeed Cauchy. Since every element of 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is a finite sum of such elements f f , we see that 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is contained in the Cauchy completion of 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] . For the reverse implication we break the proof into two steps, firstly reducing to the special case in which σ \upsigma is a top dimensional cone. Let ( g k ) k (g_{k})_{k} be a sequence in 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] , and let S ⊂ L S\subset L be defined as the set of v v such that the z v z^{v} -coefficient of g k g_{k} is nonzero for some k k . Let S ¯ ≔ π ⁡ ( S ) \overline{S}\coloneqq\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(S) , let s : S ¯ → S s\colon\overline{S}\rightarrow S be a section of π | S \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt|_{S} , and let U ≔ U L ∖ ( s ⁡ ( S ¯ ) − L ⊕ ) U\coloneqq U_{L\setminus(s(\overline{S})-L^{\oplus})} . Then U U is open. If S ¯ \overline{S} is infinite, then since each g k g_{k} is a Laurent polynomial, for all k k we must be able to find a k ′ > k k^{\prime}>k such that g k − g k ′ ∉ U g_{k}-g_{k^{\prime}}\not\in U . In particular, if ( g k ) k (g_{k})_{k} is Cauchy, then the set S S must be finite. So since 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is closed under finite sums, it suffices to deal with the case | S ¯ | = 1 |\overline{S}|=1 . Then by restricting to U S + L ± U_{S+L^{\pm}} , we can assume that σ \upsigma is a top-dimensional cone. So we make this assumption. For v ∈ L v\in L we define | v | − = min ( k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 : v = a − b with a ∈ L ⊕ and b ∈ k L + ) . \lvert v\lvert_{-}=\min(k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\colon v=a-b\textrm{ with }a\in L^{\oplus}\textrm{ and }b\in kL^{+}). For instance v ∈ L ⊕ v\in L^{\oplus} if and only if | v | − = 0 \lvert v\lvert_{-}=0 . Now let ( g k ) k (g_{k})_{k} be a Cauchy sequence, and assume for a contradiction that there does not exist a w w with g k ∈ z w ​ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ⊕ ] g_{k}\in z^{w}\Bbbk_{t}[L^{\oplus}] for each k k . Then for all N ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} , there exists a v N ∈ L v_{N}\in L with | v N | − > N \lvert v_{N}\lvert_{-}>N such that the z v N z^{v_{N}} coefficient of g k g_{k} is nonzero for some k k — otherwise we could take w = ∑ v ∈ N ​ L + ∖ ( N + 1 ) ​ L + v , w=\sum_{v\in NL^{+}\setminus(N+1)L^{+}}v, and then we would have g k ∈ z − w ​ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ⊕ ] g_{k}\in z^{-w}\Bbbk_{t}[L^{\oplus}] for all k k . After fixing such a choice of v N v_{N} for each N N , let S = ⋂ N ≥ 0 ( L ∖ ( v N − L ⊕ ) ) . S=\bigcap_{N\geq 0}(L\setminus(v_{N}-L^{\oplus})). We claim that U S U_{S} is open. The fact that S + L + ⊂ S S+L^{+}\subset S is clear, so to prove the claim, we fix v ∈ L v\in L and show that there exists a k k such that v + k ​ L + ⊂ S v+kL^{+}\subset S . If N ≥ | v | − N\geq|v|_{-} , then | v N | − > | v | − |v_{N}|_{-}>|v|_{-} , and so v N ∉ v + L + v_{N}\notin v+L^{+} . Since there are only finitely many N ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} with N < | v | − N<|v|_{-} , and since ⋂ k ( v + k ​ L + ) = ∅ \bigcap_{k}(v+kL^{+})=\emptyset , there exists some fixed k k such that v N ∉ v + k ​ L + v_{N}\notin v+kL^{+} for any N N . Then for this k k , we have ( v + k ​ L + ) ∩ ( v N − L ⊕ ) = ∅ (v+kL^{+})\cap(v_{N}-L^{\oplus})=\emptyset for all N N , and so v + k ​ L + ⊂ S v+kL^{+}\subset S , as desired. On the other hand, by construction, there is no N N such that g n − g n ′ ∈ U S g_{n}-g_{n^{\prime}}\in U_{S} for all n , n ′ ≥ N n,n^{\prime}\geq N , contradicting the assumption that ( g k ) k (g_{k})_{k} is Cauchy. ∎ For A A a topological R R -module, we say that S S topologically spans A A over R R if, for every f ∈ A f\in A and each open neighborhood U U of 0 0 , there exists a finite linear combination g = ∑ s ∈ S a s ​ s g=\sum_{s\in S}a_{s}s with a s ∈ R a_{s}\in R such that f − g ∈ U f-g\in U . Equivalently, A A is the closure of the space spanned by S S in the usual sense. One says that S S forms a topological R R -module basis for A A if no proper subset of S S topologically spans A A . In the case in which { 0 } \{0\} is open, this is just the usual notion of a basis. One easily sees the following: Lemma 2.3 . The set { z v } v ∈ L \{z^{v}\}_{v\in L} is a topological 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module basis for 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . More generally, we have the following: Lemma 2.4 . Let { f p } p ∈ L \{f_{p}\}_{p\in L} be a subset of 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket , indexed by L L , such that f p f_{p} is p p -pointed for each p p . Then { f p } p ∈ L \{f_{p}\}_{p\in L} is a topological 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module basis for 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . Proof. Let f = ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ a v ​ z w + v ∈ z w ​ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ . f=\sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}}a_{v}z^{w+v}\in z^{w}\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket\subset\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket. Modulo U w + L + U_{w+L^{+}} , the pointedness implies that f w ≡ z w f_{w}\equiv z^{w} , and so f ≡ g 0 ≔ a 0 ​ f w f\equiv g_{0}\coloneqq a_{0}f_{w} . Now suppose that there exist constants b v ∈ 𝕜 t b_{v}\in\Bbbk_{t} for v ∈ L ⊕ ∖ k ​ L + v\in L^{\oplus}\setminus kL^{+} such that f ≡ g k ≔ ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ ∖ k ​ L + b v ​ f w + v (modulo U w + k ​ L + ). f\equiv g_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}\setminus kL^{+}}b_{v}f_{w+v}\quad\quad\mbox{(modulo $U_{w+kL^{+}}$).} Then there exist additional constants b v ∈ 𝕜 t b_{v}\in\Bbbk_{t} for v ∈ k ​ L + ∖ ( k + 1 ) ​ L + v\in kL^{+}\setminus(k+1)L^{+} such that f − g k \displaystyle f-g_{k} ≡ ∑ v ∈ w + ( k ​ L + ∖ ( k + 1 ) ​ L + ) b v z w + v (modulo U w + ( k + 1 ) ​ L + ) \displaystyle\equiv\sum_{v\in w+(kL^{+}\setminus(k+1)L^{+})}b_{v}z^{w+v}\quad\quad\mbox{(modulo $U_{w+(k+1)L^{+}}$)} ≡ ∑ v ∈ w + ( k ​ L + ∖ ( k + 1 ) ​ L + ) b v f w + v (modulo U w + ( k + 1 ) ​ L + ) , \displaystyle\equiv\sum_{v\in w+(kL^{+}\setminus(k+1)L^{+})}b_{v}f_{w+v}\quad\quad\mbox{(modulo $U_{w+(k+1)L^{+}}$)}, the last equivalence using that f w + v f_{w+v} is ( w + v ) (w+v) -pointed. Thus, f ≡ ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ ∖ ( k + 1 ) ​ L + b v ​ f w + v (modulo U w + ( k + 1 ) ​ L + ) . f\equiv\sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}\setminus(k+1)L^{+}}b_{v}f_{w+v}\quad\quad\mbox{(modulo $U_{w+(k+1)L^{+}}$)}. Proceeding in this way we recursively define b v ∈ 𝕜 t b_{v}\in\Bbbk_{t} for all v ∈ L ⊕ v\in L^{\oplus} . For every open neighborhood U U of 0 0 , we have that U w + k ​ L + ⊂ U U_{w+kL^{+}}\subset U for sufficiently large k k , hence f ≡ ∑ v ∈ L ⊕ ∖ k ​ L + b v ​ f w + v f\equiv\sum_{v\in L^{\oplus}\setminus kL^{+}}b_{v}f_{w+v} modulo U U . Since every element of 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is a finite sum of elements like f f , we see that { f p } p ∈ L \{f_{p}\}_{p\in L} topologically spans. We now show that no proper subset of { f p } p ∈ L \{f_{p}\}_{p\in L} topologically spans 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . Say for a contradiction that there is such a proper subset { f p } p ∈ L ′ \{f_{p}\}_{p\in L^{\prime}} for some L ′ ⊂ L L^{\prime}\subset L . Let p ∈ L ∖ L ′ p\in L\setminus L^{\prime} . We define S = L ∖ ( p − L ⊕ ) S=L\setminus(p-L^{\oplus}) . Then U S U_{S} is open. Assume that we can pick a finite subset S ′ ⊂ L ′ S^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime} such that ( ∑ s ∈ S ′ a s ​ f s ) − z p ∈ U S (\sum_{s\in S^{\prime}}a_{s}f_{s})-z^{p}\in U_{S} for some set of nonzero a s ∈ 𝕜 t a_{s}\in\Bbbk_{t} . If s ∉ p − L ⊕ s\notin p-L^{\oplus} , then f s ∈ U S f_{s}\in U_{S} by definition and s s -pointedness, and so we can assume that S ′ ⊂ p − L ⊕ S^{\prime}\subset p-L^{\oplus} . In fact, S ′ ⊂ p − L + S^{\prime}\subset p-L^{+} since p ∉ L ′ p\notin L^{\prime} . Now pick s ∈ S ′ s\in S^{\prime} minimal with respect to the partial ordering: l ≤ l ′ l\leq l^{\prime} if l ′ ∈ l + L ⊕ l^{\prime}\in l+L^{\oplus} . Then the z s z^{s} coefficient of ( ∑ s ∈ S ′ a s ​ f s ) − z p (\sum_{s\in S^{\prime}}a_{s}f_{s})-z^{p} is a s ≠ 0 a_{s}\neq 0 , and since the z s z^{s} coefficient of every element of U S U_{S} is zero, this gives a contradiction. ∎ We note that the same argument applies in the classical limit. Remark 2.5 . Let { f p } p ∈ L \{f_{p}\}_{p\in L} be as in Lemma 2.4 . By the lemma, for p , p ′ ∈ L p,p^{\prime}\in L we may write f p ​ f p ′ = ∑ v ∈ p + p ′ + L ⊕ a ⁡ ( p , p ′ , v ) ​ f v f_{p}f_{p^{\prime}}=\sum_{v\in p+p^{\prime}+L^{\oplus}}a(p,p^{\prime};v)f_{v} for certain a ⁡ ( p , p ′ , v ) ∈ 𝕜 t a(p,p^{\prime};v)\in\Bbbk_{t} . Then as in the first part of the proof of Proposition 2.2 , a formal power series ∑ v ∈ w + L ⊕ b v ​ ℱ v \sum_{v\in w+L^{\oplus}}b_{v}\mathscr{F}_{v} defines a Cauchy convergent series in 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket by replacing each ℱ v \mathscr{F}_{v} with f v f_{v} , and the induced map A → 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ A\rightarrow\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is an isomorphism of algebras via the proof of Lemma 2.4 . 2.2.3. The quantum torus Lie algebra Let L 0 ⊂ L L_{0}\subset L be a sublattice such that ω ⁡ ( u , v ) ∈ ℤ \omega(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z} whenever u ∈ L 0 u\in L_{0} and v ∈ L v\in L . Here, being L 0 + L_{0}^{+} -graded means that for all a , b ∈ L 0 + a,b\in L_{0}^{+} , we have [ 𝔤 a , 𝔤 b ] ⊂ 𝔤 a + b [\mathfrak{g}_{a},\mathfrak{g}_{b}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{a+b} . Note that 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} and ω \omega satisfy the following compatibility condition: (11) if ​ ω ​ ( a , b ) = 0 , then ​ [ 𝔤 a , 𝔤 b ] = 0 . \displaystyle\mbox{if~}\omega(a,b)=0,\mbox{~then~}[\mathfrak{g}_{a},\mathfrak{g}_{b}]=0.
