Here, we use our assumption that ω ⁡ ( a , b ) ∈ ℤ \omega(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z} , as well as the observation that if a a and c c are positive integers with a | c a|c , then (12) ( c ) t ( a ) t = ∑ k = 1 c / a t − c + ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ a ∈ 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] ⊂ 𝕜 t . \displaystyle\frac{(c)_{t}}{(a)_{t}}=\sum_{k=1}^{c/a}t^{-c+(2k-1)a}\in\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}]\subset\Bbbk_{t}. Let 𝔤 ≥ k ⊂ 𝔤 \mathfrak{g}^{\geq k}\subset\mathfrak{g} denote the Lie algebra ideal spanned by the summands 𝔤 v \mathfrak{g}_{v} with v ∈ k ​ L 0 + v\in kL_{0}^{+} . We define 𝔤 k ≔ 𝔤 / 𝔤 ≥ ( k + 1 ) \mathfrak{g}_{k}\coloneqq\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}^{\geq(k+1)} , and 𝔤 ^ ≔ lim ← k ⁡ 𝔤 k . \displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{k}\mathfrak{g}_{k}. By the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula, we can apply exp \exp to 𝔤 k \mathfrak{g}_{k} and 𝔤 ^ \hat{\mathfrak{g}} to obtain unipotent (respectively, pro-unipotent) algebraic groups G k G_{k} and G ^ = lim ← k ⁡ G k \hat{G}=\varprojlim_{k}G_{k} , respectively. Note that G ^ \hat{G} comes with a projection to G k G_{k} for each k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} , and that we naturally have G ^ ⊂ 1 + 𝔪 ​ 𝕜 ​ [ t ± 1 ] ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ ⊂ 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⊕ ⟧ ⊂ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \hat{G}\subset 1+\mathfrak{m}\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}]\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket\subset\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}]\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket\subset\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L^{\oplus}\rrbracket\subset\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket for 𝔪 \mathfrak{m} the maximal monomial ideal of 𝕜 t ​ [ L ⊕ ] \Bbbk_{t}[L^{\oplus}] . The (adjoint) action of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} on 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] by L L -graded 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -derivations induces an action of 𝔤 ^ \hat{\mathfrak{g}} on 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket by (topologically) 3 3 3 A topological L L -grading on a topological R R -module A A is an L L -grading for an R R -submodule which topologically spans A A . For convenience, we may just refer to this as an L L -grading. L L -graded 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -derivations, hence an (Adjoint) action of G ^ \hat{G} on 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket by 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra automorphisms: for g = exp ⁡ ( b ) ∈ G ^ g=\exp(b)\in\hat{G} and a ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ a\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket , the action is Ad g ⁡ ( a ) = g ​ a ​ g − 1 = exp ⁡ ( [ b , ⋅ ] ) ​ ( a ) \Ad_{g}(a)=gag^{-1}=\exp([b,\cdot])(a) . Lemma 2.6 . For each g ∈ G ^ g\in\hat{G} , Ad g : 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ → 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Ad_{g}\colon\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket\rightarrow\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket is a homeomorphism. Proof. Let 𝔪 ^ \hat{\mathfrak{m}} be the subspace of 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket topologically spanned by the elements z n z^{n} for n ∈ L + n\in L^{+} . Since 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is graded by L 0 + ⊂ L + L_{0}^{+}\subset L^{+} and the action respects the grading, the action of G ^ \hat{G} must preserve 𝔪 ^ \hat{\mathfrak{m}} , i.e., Ad g ⁡ ( 𝔪 ^ ) ⊂ 𝔪 ^ \Ad_{g}(\hat{\mathfrak{m}})\subset\hat{\mathfrak{m}} . Since the same holds for g − 1 g^{-1} , we have Ad g − 1 ⁡ ( 𝔪 ^ ) ⊂ 𝔪 ^ \Ad_{g^{-1}}(\hat{\mathfrak{m}})\subset\hat{\mathfrak{m}} , so 𝔪 ^ ⊂ Ad g ⁡ ( 𝔪 ^ ) \hat{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\Ad_{g}(\hat{\mathfrak{m}}) . Hence, Ad g ⁡ ( 𝔪 ^ ) = 𝔪 ^ \Ad_{g}(\hat{\mathfrak{m}})=\hat{\mathfrak{m}} . Let 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} be the set of open sets obtained by taking closures of images of the sets in 𝔘 0 \mathfrak{U}_{0} under the inclusion 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] → 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}[L]\rightarrow\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . So 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} is a basis of open neighborhoods of 0 ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ 0\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket , and translates of sets in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} form a basis for all open sets in 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . Now, a set U ∋ 0 U\ni 0 is in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} if and only if it is a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -submodule of 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket such that for all k ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} we have 𝔪 ^ k ​ U ⊂ U \widehat{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}U\subset U , and, for all f ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ f\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket , there exists a k 0 ∈ ℕ k_{0}\in\mathbb{N} such that k > k 0 k>k_{0} implies 𝔪 ^ k ​ f ⊂ U \widehat{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}f\subset U . Applying Ad g \Ad_{g} to both sides, the first containment is equivalent to 𝔪 ^ ​ Ad g ⁡ ( U ) ⊂ Ad g ⁡ ( U ) \widehat{\mathfrak{m}}\Ad_{g}(U)\subset\Ad_{g}(U) and so Ad g ⁡ ( U ) \Ad_{g}(U) satisfies the first condition for being in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} . The second containment is equivalent to 𝔪 ^ k ​ Ad g ⁡ ( f ) ⊂ Ad g ⁡ ( U ) \widehat{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}\Ad_{g}(f)\subset\Ad_{g}(U) . Then since Ad g \Ad_{g} is a bijection, this is equivalent to the condition that for all f ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ f\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket , there exists some k 0 ∈ ℕ k_{0}\in\mathbb{N} such that k > k 0 k>k_{0} implies 𝔪 ^ k ​ f ⊂ Ad g ⁡ ( U ) \widehat{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}f\subset\Ad_{g}(U) . But this is equivalent to the second condition for Ad g ⁡ ( U ) \Ad_{g}(U) to be in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} . So Ad g \Ad_{g} takes sets in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} to sets in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} . Additionally, Ad g ⁡ ( U + f ) = Ad g ⁡ ( U ) + Ad g ⁡ ( f ) \Ad_{g}(U+f)=\Ad_{g}(U)+\Ad_{g}(f) and so Ad g \Ad_{g} takes translates (under addition) of sets in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} to other translates of sets in 𝔖 \mathfrak{S} . Applying the same argument to Ad g − 1 = Ad g − 1 \Ad_{g^{-1}}=\Ad_{g}^{-1} yields the claim. ∎ 2.2.4. Classical limits One can take classical limits of everything above. In place of 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] , one considers the usual torus algebra 𝕜 ⁡ [ L ] \Bbbk[L] . Taking t 1 / D ↦ 1 t^{1/D}\mapsto 1 defines a surjective graded algebra homomorphism lim t → 1 : 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] → 𝕜 ⁡ [ L ] . \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon\Bbbk_{t}[L]\rightarrow\Bbbk[L]. Similarly, we can define the (classical) torus Lie algebra, 𝔤 cl ≔ ⨁ v ∈ L 0 + 𝕜 ⋅ z v , \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\cl}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{v\in L_{0}^{+}}\Bbbk\cdot z^{v}, equipped with the bracket [ z a , z b ] ≔ ω ⁡ ( a , b ) ​ z a + b . \displaystyle[z^{a},z^{b}]\coloneqq\omega(a,b)z^{a+b}. Then t ↦ 1 t\mapsto 1 and z ^ v ↦ 1 | v | ​ z v \hat{z}^{v}\mapsto\frac{1}{|v|}z^{v} determines a graded Lie algebra homomorphism lim t → 1 : 𝔤 → 𝔤 cl . \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\cl}. Indeed, lim t → 1 [ z ^ a , z ^ b ] 𝔤 = lim t → 1 ( ( ω ⁡ ( a , b ) ) t ​ ( | a + b | ) t ( | a | ) t ​ ( | b | ) t ​ z ^ a + b ) = ω ⁡ ( a , b ) | a | ​ | b | ​ z a + b = [ z a / | a | , z b / | b | ] 𝔤 cl . \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}[\hat{z}^{a},\hat{z}^{b}]_{\mathfrak{g}}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\left(\frac{(\omega(a,b))_{t}(|a+b|)_{t}}{(|a|)_{t}(|b|)_{t}}\hat{z}^{a+b}\right)=\frac{\omega(a,b)}{|a||b|}z^{a+b}=[z^{a}/|a|,z^{b}/|b|]_{\mathfrak{g}^{\cl}}. We similarly see that these maps lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} respect the action of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} on 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}[L] : lim t → 1 ad z ^ a ⁡ ( z b ) = lim t → 1 ( ( ω ⁡ ( a , b ) ) t ( | a | ) t ​ z a + b ) = ω ⁡ ( a , b ) | a | ​ z a + b = [ z a / | a | , z b ] 𝔤 cl . \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\ad_{\hat{z}^{a}}(z^{b})=\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\left(\frac{(\omega(a,b))_{t}}{(|a|)_{t}}z^{a+b}\right)=\frac{\omega(a,b)}{|a|}z^{a+b}=[z^{a}/|a|,z^{b}]_{\mathfrak{g}^{\cl}}. Since the morphisms lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} respect the L L -gradings, they induce morphisms lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} on the respective completed Lie algebras, giving lim t → 1 : 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ → 𝕜 ⁡ ⟦ L ⟧ \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket\rightarrow\Bbbk\llbracket L\rrbracket , lim t → 1 : 𝔤 ^ → 𝔤 ^ cl \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow\hat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\cl} , and lim t → 1 G ^ → G ^ cl \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\hat{G}\rightarrow\hat{G}^{\cl} , each intertwining the corresponding actions on 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket and 𝕜 ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk\llbracket L\rrbracket . 2.3. Scattering diagrams 2.3.1. Walls and scattering diagrams We assume as above that L L is a lattice equipped with a skew-symmetric rational bilinear form ω \omega , L 0 ⊂ L L_{0}\subset L is a sublattice satisfying ω ⁡ ( u , v ) ∈ ℤ \omega(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z} whenever u ∈ L 0 u\in L_{0} and v ∈ L v\in L , and σ ⊂ L ℝ \upsigma\subset L_{\mathbb{R}} is a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone. We let 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} be any L 0 + L_{0}^{+} -graded Lie algebra satisfying ( 11 ). One defines 𝔤 k \mathfrak{g}_{k} , 𝔤 ^ \hat{\mathfrak{g}} , G k G_{k} , and G ^ \hat{G} analogously to the case in which 𝔤 = 𝔤 L 0 + , ω \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{L_{0}^{+},\omega} . Given a primitive v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} (primitive as an element of L 0 L_{0} ), we define a Lie subalgebra 𝔤 v ∥ ≔ ∏ k ∈ ℤ > 0 𝔤 k ​ v ⊂ 𝔤 ^ , \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{v}^{\parallel}\coloneqq\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\mathfrak{g}_{kv}\subset\hat{\mathfrak{g}}, and a pro-unipotent algebraic subgroup G v ∥ ≔ exp ⁡ ( 𝔤 v ∥ ) ⊂ G ^ G_{v}^{\parallel}\coloneqq\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{v}^{\parallel})\subset\hat{G} . Note that 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} satisfying ( 11 ) implies that 𝔤 v ∥ \mathfrak{g}_{v}^{\parallel} and G v ∥ G_{v}^{\parallel} are Abelian. Consider the map ω 1 : L → L ∗ \omega_{1}\colon L\rightarrow L^{*} , v ↦ ω ⁡ ( v , ⋅ ) v\mapsto\omega(v,\cdot) . We denote v ω ⟂ ≔ ω 1 ​ ( v ) ⟂ = { a ∈ L ℝ : ω ⁡ ( v , a ) = 0 } ⊂ L ℝ . \displaystyle v^{\omega\perp}\coloneqq\omega_{1}(v)^{\perp}=\{a\in L_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\omega(v,a)=0\}\subset L_{\mathbb{R}}. A wall in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is a pair ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) , where 4 4 4 Our definition of walls is consistent with that of [ Man , Def. 2.2] (for m 𝔡 m_{\mathfrak{d}} there taken to be ω ⁡ ( v 𝔡 , ⋅ ) ∈ L ∗ \omega(v_{\mathfrak{d}},\cdot)\in L^{*} in our setup) but slightly different from that of [ GHKK18 , Def. 1.4] . In the convention used by [ GHKK18 ] , the support 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} would live in L ℝ ∗ L^{*}_{\mathbb{R}} and would be parallel to v 𝔡 ⟂ v_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\perp} . We find that our approach is more natural for the quantum setup, especially for defining the scattering diagrams 𝔇 𝒳 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}} of § 4.3 . See [ Man , Rmk. 2.3] or § 2.3.4 for an explanation of how to pass between the different conventions. • f 𝔡 ∈ G v 𝔡 ∥ f_{\mathfrak{d}}\in G_{v_{\mathfrak{d}}}^{\parallel} for some primitive v 𝔡 ∈ L 0 + ∖ ker ⁡ ( ω 1 ) v_{\mathfrak{d}}\in L_{0}^{+}\setminus\ker(\omega_{1}) , and • 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} is a closed, convex (but not necessarily strongly convex), ( r − 1 ) (r-1) -dimensional, rational-polyhedral affine cone in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} parallel to v 𝔡 ω ⟂ v_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\omega\perp} . Here, by a closed, convex, rational polyhedral affine cone in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} , we mean an intersection of sets of the form { v ∈ L ℝ : ⟨ u i , v ⟩ ≥ ⟨ u i , v 0 ⟩ } \{v\in L_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\langle u_{i},v\rangle\geq\langle u_{i},v_{0}\rangle\} for some fixed (but possibly not uniquely determined) v 0 ∈ L ℝ v_{0}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} called the apex of the cone, and some finite collection { u i } \{u_{i}\} of elements of L ∗ L^{*} . Note that the primitive vector v 𝔡 ∈ L 0 + v_{\mathfrak{d}}\in L_{0}^{+} is uniquely determined by f 𝔡 f_{\mathfrak{d}} (assuming f 𝔡 ≠ 1 f_{\mathfrak{d}}\neq 1 ). We call − v 𝔡 -v_{\mathfrak{d}} the direction of the wall. A wall is called incoming if v + ℝ ≥ 0 ​ v 𝔡 ⊂ 𝔡 v+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}v_{\mathfrak{d}}\subset\mathfrak{d} for all v ∈ 𝔡 v\in\mathfrak{d} , and it is called outgoing otherwise. A scattering diagram 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is a set of walls in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} such that for each k > 0 k>0 , there are only finitely many ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D} with f 𝔡 f_{\mathfrak{d}} not projecting to 1 1 in G k G_{k} . For each k ∈ ℤ > 0 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} and scattering diagram 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} , let 𝔇 k ⊂ 𝔇 \mathfrak{D}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{D} denote the finite scattering diagram over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} consisting of the ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D} for which f 𝔡 f_{\mathfrak{d}} is nontrivial in the projection to G k G_{k} . We may also view 𝔇 k \mathfrak{D}_{k} as a scattering diagram over 𝔤 k \mathfrak{g}_{k} by taking the projections of the functions f 𝔡 f_{\mathfrak{d}} . 2.3.2. Path-ordered products We will sometimes denote a wall ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) by just 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} . Denote Supp ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) ≔ ⋃ 𝔡 ∈ 𝔇 𝔡 \Supp(\mathfrak{D})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}}\mathfrak{d} , and (13) Joints ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) ≔ ⋃ 𝔡 ∈ 𝔇 ∂ 𝔡 ∪ ⋃ 𝔡 1 , 𝔡 2 ∈ 𝔇 dim 𝔡 1 ∩ 𝔡 2 = r − 2 𝔡 1 ∩ 𝔡 2 . \displaystyle\Joints(\mathfrak{D})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}}\partial\mathfrak{d}\cup\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{2}\in\mathfrak{D}\\ \dim\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}=r-2\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}. Consider a smooth immersion γ : [ 0 , 1 ] → L ℝ ∖ Joints ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) \upgamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow L_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\Joints(\mathfrak{D}) with endpoints not in Supp ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) \Supp(\mathfrak{D}) which is transverse to each wall of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} it crosses. Let ( 𝔡 i , f 𝔡 i ) (\mathfrak{d}_{i},f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}) , i = 1 , … , s i=1,\ldots,s , denote the walls of 𝔇 k \mathfrak{D}_{k} crossed by γ \upgamma , and say they are crossed at times 0 < t 1 ≤ … ≤ t s < 1 0<t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{s}<1 , respectively (if t i = t i + 1 t_{i}=t_{i+1} , then the fact that each G v ∥ G_{v}^{\parallel} is Abelian will imply that the ordering of these two walls does not affect ( 15 ) and therefore does not matter). Define (14) θ 𝔡 i ≔ f 𝔡 i sgn ⁡ ω ⁡ ( v 𝔡 i , − γ ′ ​ ( t i ) ) ∈ G k . \displaystyle\uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}\coloneqq f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}^{\sign\omega(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}},-\upgamma^{\prime}(t_{i}))}\in G_{k}. Let (15) θ γ , 𝔇 k ≔ θ 𝔡 s ⋯ θ 𝔡 1 ∈ G k , \displaystyle\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}}^{k}\coloneqq\uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\cdots\uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}\in G_{k}, and define the path-ordered product: (16) θ γ , 𝔇 ≔ lim ← k ⁡ θ γ , 𝔇 k ∈ G ^ . \displaystyle\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{k}\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}}^{k}\in\hat{G}. Definition 2.7 . Two scattering diagrams 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} and 𝔇 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime} are equivalent if θ γ , 𝔇 = θ γ , 𝔇 ′ \uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}} for each smooth immersion γ \upgamma as above (assuming transversality with the walls of both 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} and 𝔇 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime} ). A scattering diagram 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is consistent if each θ γ , 𝔇 \uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}} depends only on the endpoints of γ \upgamma , or equivalently, if θ γ , 𝔇 = Id \uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\id whenever γ \upgamma is a closed path. Examples 2.8 . (1) If ( 𝔡 , f 1 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{1}) and ( 𝔡 , f 2 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{2}) are two walls of a scattering diagram with the same support and direction, then replacing these two walls with the single wall ( 𝔡 , f 1 ​ f 2 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{1}f_{2}) results in an equivalent scattering diagram. (2) Replacing a wall ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D} with a pair of walls ( 𝔡 i , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d}_{i},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) , i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 , such that 𝔡 1 ∪ 𝔡 2 = 𝔡 \mathfrak{d}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{d}_{2}=\mathfrak{d} and codim ⁡ ( 𝔡 1 ∩ 𝔡 2 ) = 2 \codim(\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2})=2 produces an equivalent scattering diagram. (3) One says that x ∈ L ℝ x\in L_{\mathbb{R}} is general if it is contained in at most one hyperplane of the form u ⟂ u^{\perp} for u ∈ L ∗ u\in L^{*} . For 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} and x ∈ L ℝ x\in L_{\mathbb{R}} general, denote f x , 𝔇 ≔ ∏ 𝔡 ∋ x f 𝔡 ∈ G ^ , f_{x,\mathfrak{D}}\coloneqq\prod_{\mathfrak{d}\ni x}f_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\hat{G}, where the product is over all walls ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D} with 𝔡 ∋ x \mathfrak{d}\ni x . Note that 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} satisfying ( 11 ) and x x being general ensures that these f 𝔡 f_{\mathfrak{d}} commute, so f x , 𝔇 f_{x,\mathfrak{D}} is well-defined. One easily sees (cf. [ GHKK18 , Lem. 1.9] ) that two scattering diagrams 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} and 𝔇 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime} in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} are equivalent if and only if f x , 𝔇 = f x , 𝔇 ′ f_{x,\mathfrak{D}}=f_{x,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}} for all general x ∈ L ℝ x\in L_{\mathbb{R}} . 2.3.3. Operations on scattering diagrams We introduce a few tools that will prove useful throughout the paper in passing between (consistent) scattering diagrams. As above, we retain our standing assumptions on L , L 0 , ω , σ L,L_{0},\omega,\upsigma and allow 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} to be any L 0 + L_{0}^{+} -graded Lie algebra satisfying ( 11 ). Let π : 𝔤 → 𝔤 ′ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt\colon\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\prime} (or π : 𝔤 ^ → 𝔤 ^ ′ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt\colon\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow\hat{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime} ) be a morphism of L 0 + L_{0}^{+} -graded Lie algebras satisfying ( 11 ). We denote by exp ⁡ π : G ^ → G ^ ′ \exp\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt\colon\hat{G}\rightarrow\hat{G}^{\prime} the induced morphism of pro-unipotent algebraic groups. Let 𝔇 = { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) } j ∈ J \mathfrak{D}=\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\}_{j\in J} be a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . Then we define π ∗ ​ 𝔇 = { ( 𝔡 j , exp ⁡ π ⁡ ( f j ) ) } j ∈ J \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt_{*}\mathfrak{D}=\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},\exp\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(f_{j}))\}_{j\in J} . This gives a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 ′ \mathfrak{g}^{\prime} , as we have not altered the gradings of the functions or the underlying cones for the walls. The scattering diagram π ∗ ​ 𝔇 \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt_{*}\mathfrak{D} is consistent if 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is. Proposition 2.9 . Let 𝔇 = { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) } j ∈ J \mathfrak{D}=\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\}_{j\in J} be a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . We define the opposite scattering diagram 𝔇 op = { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) } j ∈ J \mathfrak{D}^{\opp}=\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\}_{j\in J} , a scattering diagram in L ℝ − L^{-}_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 op \mathfrak{g}^{\opp} (i.e., 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} with its bracket negated), where L ℝ − L^{-}_{\mathbb{R}} is identified with L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} and is endowed with the form − ω -\omega . Then 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is consistent if and only if 𝔇 op \mathfrak{D}^{\opp} is, and the subset J in ⊂ J J_{\In}\subset J parameterizing incoming walls is the same for both scattering diagrams. Proof. The fact that the above pairs really define walls is immediate from the definitions, as is the statement regarding incoming walls, since the underlying cones of the walls, as well as their directions, are identified. Now let γ \upgamma be a smooth immersion as in § 2.3.2 . Then θ γ , 𝔇 op k = \displaystyle\uptheta^{k}_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\opp}}= θ − 1 𝔡 s ⋅ op θ − 1 𝔡 s − 1 ⋯ op θ − 1 𝔡 1 \displaystyle\uptheta^{-1}_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\cdot_{\opp}\uptheta^{-1}_{\mathfrak{d}_{s-1}}\cdots_{\opp}\uptheta^{-1}_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}} = \displaystyle= θ − 1 𝔡 1 ⋯ θ − 1 𝔡 s \displaystyle\uptheta^{-1}_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}\cdots\uptheta^{-1}_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}} = \displaystyle= ( θ 𝔡 s ⋯ θ 𝔡 1 ) − 1 \displaystyle(\uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\cdots\uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}})^{-1} and the statement regarding consistency follows. ∎ Corollary 2.10 . Let 𝔇 = { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) } j ∈ J \mathfrak{D}=\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\}_{j\in J} be a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . Then 𝔇 − ≔ { ( 𝔡 j , f j − 1 ) } j ∈ J \mathfrak{D}^{-}\coloneqq\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j}^{-1})\}_{j\in J} is a scattering diagram in L ℝ − L^{-}_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} , with the same incoming walls as 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , which is consistent if and only if 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is. Proof. There is an isomorphism s : \displaystyle s\colon 𝔤 op → 𝔤 \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\opp}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g} a ↦ − a \displaystyle a\mapsto-a and 𝔇 − = s ∗ ​ ( 𝔇 op ) \mathfrak{D}^{-}=s_{*}(\mathfrak{D}^{\opp}) . ∎ It will prove extremely useful to be able to take direct images of scattering diagrams along morphisms of lattices as well as graded Lie algebras, and so we generalize the definition of π ∗ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt_{*} given above. We say that a scattering diagram is saturated if for every wall ( 𝔡 , f ) (\mathfrak{d},f) in 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , the cone 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} is closed under addition of elements in ker ⁡ ( ω 1 ) \ker(\omega_{1}) . It is easy to check that every consistent scattering diagram is equivalent to a saturated scattering diagram. Lemma 2.11 . Consider L , L 0 , ω L,L_{0},\omega as above, and assume moreover that we have a lattice L ~ \widetilde{L} with a sublattice L ~ 0 \widetilde{L}_{0} and a ℚ \mathbb{Q} -valued skew-symmetric form ω ~ \widetilde{\omega} on L ~ \widetilde{L} . Let π : L ~ → L \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt\colon\widetilde{L}\rightarrow L be a map of lattices such that ω ~ = π ∗ ​ ω \widetilde{\omega}=\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt^{*}\omega and π ℝ : L ~ ℝ → L ℝ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow L_{\mathbb{R}} is surjective. Let σ ~ ⊂ L ~ ℝ \widetilde{\upsigma}\subset\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} be a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone such that π ⁡ ( σ ~ ) ⊂ σ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\widetilde{\upsigma})\subset\upsigma . Set L ~ 0 + = ( L ~ 0 ∩ σ ~ ) ∖ { 0 } \widetilde{L}_{0}^{+}=(\widetilde{L}_{0}\cap\widetilde{\upsigma})\setminus\{0\} . Let 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} and 𝔤 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}} be L + L^{+} and L ~ + \tilde{L}^{+} -graded Lie algebras satisfying ( 11 ) with respect to ω \omega and ω ~ \widetilde{\omega} respectively. We assume that we are given a morphism of Lie algebras ϖ : 𝔤 ~ → 𝔤 \varpi\colon\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g} , such that ϖ ⁡ ( 𝔤 ~ v ) ⊂ 𝔤 π ⁡ ( v ) \varpi(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{v})\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\uppi\hskip 0.40974pt(v)} for all v ∈ L ~ 0 + v\in\widetilde{L}_{0}^{+} (where we write 𝔤 0 = 0 \mathfrak{g}_{0}=0 ). We denote by exp ⁡ ϖ \exp\varpi the induced morphism of the pro-unipotent algebraic groups associated to the completions of 𝔤 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}} and 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . If 𝔇 ~ ≔ { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) } j ∈ J \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}\coloneqq\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\}_{j\in J} is a scattering diagram in L ~ ℝ \widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}} , then (17) 𝔇 ≔ ( π , ϖ ) ∗ ​ ( 𝔇 ~ ) ≔ { ( π ⁡ ( 𝔡 j ) , exp ⁡ ϖ ⁡ ( f j ) ) } j ∈ J \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq(\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt,\varpi)_{*}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}})\coloneqq\{(\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{j}),\exp\varpi(f_{j}))\}_{j\in J} is a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . If 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} is consistent and saturated, then so is 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} . If ( 𝔡 j , f j ) (\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j}) is incoming then so is the corresponding wall in 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , and if 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} is saturated, then the reverse implication also holds. Proof. Firstly, since π ⁡ ( k ​ L ~ 0 + ) ⊂ k ​ L 0 + \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(k\widetilde{L}_{0}^{+})\subset kL_{0}^{+} , there is a morphism of quotient groups exp ⁡ ϖ : G ~ k → G k \exp\varpi\colon\widetilde{G}_{k}\rightarrow G_{k} (this is also why the induced map on the completions is well-defined) and the finiteness condition for 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} to be a scattering diagram is satisfied. Let 𝔡 j \mathfrak{d}_{j} be a wall of 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} . Since π ℝ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt_{\mathbb{R}} is surjective, the codimension of the span of π ⁡ ( 𝔡 k ) \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{k}) is at most one. On the other hand, π ⁡ ( v 𝔡 j ) ∉ ker ⁡ ( ω 1 ) \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}})\notin\ker(\omega_{1}) since v 𝔡 j ∉ ker ⁡ ( ω ~ 1 ) v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\notin\ker(\widetilde{\omega}_{1}) , and so π ⁡ ( 𝔡 j ) ⊂ π ​ ( v 𝔡 j ) ω ⟂ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{j})\subset\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}})^{\omega\perp} has codimension at least one, i.e. it has the required codimension: one. Now, let γ ⊂ L ℝ \upgamma\subset L_{\mathbb{R}} be a path for which θ γ , 𝔇 \uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}} is defined. Saturation of the walls of 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} implies that they are invariant under translation by elements of ker ⁡ ( π ) ⊗ ℝ \ker(\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt)\otimes\mathbb{R} , and so any smooth lift γ ~ ′ \widetilde{\upgamma}^{\prime} of γ ′ \upgamma^{\prime} to L ~ ℝ \widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} will satisfy exp ⁡ ϖ ⁡ ( θ γ ~ ′ , 𝔇 ~ ) = θ γ ′ , 𝔇 \exp\varpi(\uptheta_{\widetilde{\upgamma}^{\prime},\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}})=\uptheta_{\upgamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{D}} . It follows that consistency of 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} implies consistency of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , as desired. Let ( 𝔡 j , f j ) (\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j}) be a wall in 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} . Then there is a (possibly non-unique) α j ∈ L ~ ℝ \upalpha_{j}\in\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} such that 𝔡 j + α j \mathfrak{d}_{j}+\upalpha_{j} has the origin as an apex. Then the cone π ⁡ ( 𝔡 j ) + π ⁡ ( α j ) ⊂ L ℝ \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{j})+\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\upalpha_{j})\subset L_{\mathbb{R}} has the origin as an apex as well. If ( 𝔡 j , f j ) (\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j}) is incoming, then v 𝔡 j ∈ 𝔡 j + α j v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\in\mathfrak{d}_{j}+\upalpha_{j} , and so π ⁡ ( v 𝔡 j ) ∈ π ⁡ ( 𝔡 j + α j ) = π ⁡ ( 𝔡 j ) + π ⁡ ( α j ) \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}})\in\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{j}+\upalpha_{j})=\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{j})+\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\upalpha_{j}) , meaning that the corresponding wall in 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is incoming. Conversely, suppose that the wall in 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} indexed by j j is incoming, so π ⁡ ( v 𝔡 j ) ∈ π ⁡ ( 𝔡 j ) + π ⁡ ( α j ) \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}})\in\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathfrak{d}_{j})+\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\upalpha_{j}) . Then there exists a v ~ ∈ 𝔡 j + α j \tilde{v}\in\mathfrak{d}_{j}+\upalpha_{j} such that π ⁡ ( v ~ ) − π ⁡ ( v 𝔡 j ) = 0 \uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(\tilde{v})-\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}})=0 , and so v ~ − v 𝔡 j ∈ ker ⁡ ( ω ~ 1 ) \tilde{v}-v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\in\ker(\widetilde{\omega}_{1}) . Now if 𝔇 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}} is saturated, then v 𝔡 j ∈ 𝔡 j + α j v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\in\mathfrak{d}_{j}+\upalpha_{j} , so 𝔡 j \mathfrak{d}_{j} is incoming, as desired. ∎ Finally, as well as being able to take the above direct image of scattering diagrams, it will be useful to be able to take inverse images, at least along inclusions of lattices. Lemma 2.12 . Let L , L 0 , ω , σ L,L_{0},\omega,\upsigma be as above. Let i : L ~ ↪ L i\colon\widetilde{L}\hookrightarrow L be a saturated sublattice such that the projection of σ \upsigma along L ℝ → L ℝ / L ~ ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow L_{\mathbb{R}}/\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} is strongly convex, and define σ ~ ≔ σ ∩ L ~ ℝ \widetilde{\upsigma}\coloneqq\upsigma\cap\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} , L ~ 0 ≔ L 0 ∩ L ~ \widetilde{L}_{0}\coloneqq L_{0}\cap\widetilde{L} , L ~ 0 + ≔ ( L ~ 0 ∩ σ ~ ) ∖ { 0 } \widetilde{L}_{0}^{+}\coloneqq(\widetilde{L}_{0}\cap\widetilde{\upsigma})\setminus\{0\} , 𝔤 ~ ≔ ⨁ v ∈ L ~ 0 + 𝔤 v \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{v\in\widetilde{L}_{0}^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{v} , and ω ~ = ω | L ~ \widetilde{\omega}=\omega\lvert_{\widetilde{L}} . We denote by p : ⨁ v ∈ L 0 + 𝔤 v → ⨁ v ∈ L ~ 0 + 𝔤 v . p\colon\bigoplus_{v\in L_{0}^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{v}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\widetilde{L}_{0}^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{v}. the retraction of Lie algebras obtained by taking the quotient by the factors 𝔤 v \mathfrak{g}_{v} for v ∉ L ~ 0 + v\notin\widetilde{L}_{0}^{+} . Let 𝔇 ≔ { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) } j ∈ J \mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\}_{j\in J} be a scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . Denote by J ′ ⊂ J J^{\prime}\subset J those walls such that v 𝔡 j ∈ L ~ 0 v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\in\widetilde{L}_{0} and codim L ~ ℝ ⁡ ( L ~ ℝ ∩ 𝔡 j ) = 1 \codim_{\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{j})=1 (so in particular, v 𝔡 j ∉ ker ⁡ ( ω ~ ) v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\notin\ker(\widetilde{\omega}) ). We assume that codim L ~ ℝ ⁡ ( L ~ ℝ ∩ ∂ 𝔡 j ) ≥ 2 \codim_{\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap\partial\mathfrak{d}_{j})\geq 2 for each j ∈ J ′ j\in J^{\prime} — up to equivalence, this always holds for consistent 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} . Then i ∗ ​ 𝔇 ≔ { ( 𝔡 j ∩ L ~ ℝ , exp ⁡ ( p ) ​ ( f j ) ) } j ∈ J ′ i^{*}\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\{(\mathfrak{d}_{j}\cap\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}},\exp(p)(f_{j}))\}_{j\in J^{\prime}} is a scattering diagram in L ~ ℝ \widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 ~ \widetilde{\mathfrak{g}} which is consistent if 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is. The incoming walls of i ∗ ​ 𝔇 i^{*}\mathfrak{D} are given by those j ∈ J ′ j\in J^{\prime} such that ( 𝔡 j , f j ) (\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j}) is an incoming wall of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} . Proof. The proof of finiteness is as in the proof of Lemma 2.11 , and the fact that the cones in i ∗ ​ 𝔇 i^{*}\mathfrak{D} have the right dimension follows from the assumptions, so i ∗ ​ 𝔇 i^{*}\mathfrak{D} is indeed a scattering diagram. The claim regarding incoming walls is immediate from the definition. Let γ \upgamma be a smooth closed path [ 0 , 1 ] → L ~ ℝ ∖ Joints ⁡ ( i ∗ ​ 𝔇 ) [0,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\Joints(i^{*}\mathfrak{D}) with base-points not in Supp ⁡ ( i ∗ ​ 𝔇 ) \Supp(i^{*}\mathfrak{D}) which is transverse to each wall of i ∗ ​ 𝔇 i^{*}\mathfrak{D} it crosses, which we may moreover consider as a path in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} . Fix a k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 . First, we may perturb γ \upgamma so that it avoids any walls 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} satisfying codim ⁡ ( L ~ ℝ ∩ 𝔡 ) ≥ 2 \codim(\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap\mathfrak{d})\geq 2 , without affecting the associated path ordered product θ γ , 𝔇 k \uptheta^{k}_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}} . We then perturb γ \upgamma in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} to a smooth closed path γ ′ \upgamma^{\prime} which is transverse to all walls of 𝔇 k \mathfrak{D}_{k} , has base-point in the complement of Supp ⁡ ( 𝔇 k ) \Supp(\mathfrak{D}_{k}) , crosses the same walls of { ( 𝔡 j , f j ) : j ∈ J ′ } ∩ 𝔇 k \{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},f_{j})\colon j\in J^{\prime}\}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{k} as before in the same order as before, and crosses no walls 𝔡 j \mathfrak{d}_{j} with v 𝔡 j ∈ ker ⁡ ( ω ~ 1 ) v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}\in\ker(\widetilde{\omega}_{1}) . Then θ γ , i ∗ ​ 𝔇 k = exp ⁡ ( p ) ​ ( θ γ ′ , 𝔇 k ) = exp ⁡ ( p ) ​ ( 1 ) = 1 \uptheta_{\upgamma,i^{*}\mathfrak{D}}^{k}=\exp(p)(\uptheta_{\upgamma^{\prime},\mathfrak{D}}^{k})=\exp(p)(1)=1 . The consistency claim follows. We now consider the claim that, for consistent 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} considered up to equivalence, the condition codim L ~ ℝ ⁡ ( L ~ ℝ ∩ ∂ 𝔡 j ) ≥ 2 \codim_{\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap\partial\mathfrak{d}_{j})\geq 2 is always satisfied for j ∈ J ′ j\in J^{\prime} . Suppose 𝔡 j \mathfrak{d}_{j} is a wall failing this condition. Then consistency around the corresponding joint of 𝔡 j \mathfrak{d}_{j} forces there to be additional such walls. But for any two such walls 𝔡 j , 𝔡 j ′ \mathfrak{d}_{j},\mathfrak{d}_{j^{\prime}} , we have ω ~ ​ ( v 𝔡 j , v 𝔡 j ′ ) = 0 \widetilde{\omega}(v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}},v_{\mathfrak{d}_{j^{\prime}}})=0 , hence [ f j , f j ′ ] = 0 [f_{j},f_{j^{\prime}}]=0 by ( 11 ). The only possibility then is that the collection of such walls can be glued (as in Example 2.8 (2)) to remove the existence of such joints. ∎ 2.3.4. Existence and uniqueness of consistent scattering diagrams The following theorem is a fundamental result on scattering diagrams. The two-dimensional case over 𝔤 cl \mathfrak{g}^{\cl} was proved in [ KS06 ] , while the generalization to higher dimensions (and more general affine spaces than just L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} ) was proved in [ GS11 ] . An extension to more general Lie algebras, including the quantum torus Lie algebra 𝔤 L 0 + , ω \mathfrak{g}_{L_{0}^{+},\omega} , is given by [ KS14 , Prop. 3.2.6, 3.3.2] , (cf. [ GHKK18 , Thm. 1.21] for a review of this argument in the cluster setup). Theorem 2.13 . Let 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} be a finite scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} whose only walls are incoming. Then there is a unique-up-to-equivalence scattering diagram 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , also denoted Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) , such that 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is consistent, 𝔇 ⊃ 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}\supset\mathfrak{D}_{\In} , and 𝔇 ∖ 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\In} consists only of outgoing walls. As noted in Footnote 4 , our definition of a wall is slightly different from that used in [ KS14 , GHKK18 ] , and our quantum torus Lie algebras are different from the Lie algebras used in [ GS11 ] , so we briefly explain how to recover Theorem 2.13 in our setup. First, we note that the uniqueness statement follows from exactly the same argument used in the proof of [ CM20 , Thm. 3.5] (just replace the Λ ℝ ∨ \Uplambda_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee} there with Λ ℝ \Uplambda_{\mathbb{R}} , which in our notation is L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} — also, 𝔤 k \mathfrak{g}_{k} there is 𝔤 k − 1 \mathfrak{g}_{k-1} here). On the other hand, the existence follows from taking the asymptotic scattering diagram of the perturbed scattering diagram 𝔇 k ∞ \mathfrak{D}_{k}^{\infty} constructed in [ Man , §3.2.2] . We note that the assumption in [ CM20 , Thm. 3.5] that walls of 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} are full hyperplanes is not needed for the uniqueness proof, and since incoming walls can always be extended to full affine hyperplanes by adding outgoing walls and then applying the equivalence of Example 2.8 (2), we can assume this property holds when applying the existence proof. Alternatively, one may use the operations of § 2.3.3 to recover the existence result in our setup directly from that of [ KS14 ] : We apply what we call the principal coefficients trick, which involves essentially the same procedure used for defining cluster algebras with principal coefficients, cf. § 4.1 . Let v 1 , … , v r v_{1},\ldots,v_{r} be a basis for L L , and consider the lattice L ∘ ≔ ℤ ⁡ ⟨ v 1 , … , v r , f 1 , … , f r ⟩ L^{\circ}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{r},f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle for f 1 , … , f r f_{1},\ldots,f_{r} some new formal generators. Denote by i ℝ : L ℝ ↪ L ℝ ∘ i_{\mathbb{R}}\colon L_{\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow L^{\circ}_{\mathbb{R}} , the natural inclusion. We extend ω \omega to a skew-symmetric form ω ∘ \omega^{\circ} on L ∘ L^{\circ} by defining ω ∘ ​ ( v i , f j ) = δ i ​ j \omega^{\circ}(v_{i},f_{j})=\updelta_{ij} and ω ∘ ​ ( f i , f j ) = 0 \omega^{\circ}(f_{i},f_{j})=0 for all i , j i,j . We set σ ∘ = i ℝ ​ ( σ ) \upsigma^{\circ}=i_{\mathbb{R}}(\upsigma) . The matrix for ω ∘ \omega^{\circ} in this basis is ( A B C D ) \left(\begin{matrix}A&B\\ C&D\end{matrix}\right) , where A , B , C , D A,B,C,D are r × r r\times r matrices, A A being the matrix for ω \omega , B B the identity matrix, C C negative the identity, and D D the 0 0 -matrix. Such a matrix has determinant 1 1 , so the map ω 1 ∘ : L ∘ → ( L ∘ ) ∗ \omega_{1}^{\circ}\colon L^{\circ}\rightarrow(L^{\circ})^{*} , v ↦ ω ∘ ​ ( v , ⋅ ) v\mapsto\omega^{\circ}(v,\cdot) , is an isomorphism. Let 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} be as in Theorem 2.13 . As noted above, we may assume that the initial walls are full affine hyperplanes, i.e. all the walls are of the form ( v j ω ⟂ , f 𝔡 j ) (v_{j}^{\omega\perp},f_{\mathfrak{d}_{j}}) for v j ∈ L + v_{j}\in L^{+} indexed by j ∈ J j\in J . We define 𝔇 in ∘ \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\circ} to be the scattering diagram in L ℝ ∘ L^{\circ}_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} with walls given by ( i ​ ( v j ) ω ∘ ⟂ , f 𝔡 v j ) (i(v_{j})^{\omega^{\circ}\mskip-3.0mu\perp},f_{\mathfrak{d}_{v_{j}}}) for j ∈ J j\in J . The scattering diagram 𝔇 in ∘ \mathfrak{D}^{\circ}_{\In} has only incoming walls. Let Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ∘ ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\circ}) be a consistent scattering diagram in L ℝ ∘ L^{\circ}_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} such that Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ∘ ) ∖ 𝔇 in ∘ \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\circ})\setminus\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}_{\In} only contains outgoing walls. Then by Lemma 2.12 the scattering diagram i ∗ ​ Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ∘ ) i^{*}\scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\circ}) is consistent, with incoming walls equal to i ∗ ​ 𝔇 in ∘ = 𝔇 in . i^{*}\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}_{\In}=\mathfrak{D}_{\In}. So now for the existence proof, it suffices to deal with the cases where ω \omega is unimodular, i.e., ω 1 : L → L ∗ \omega_{1}\colon L\rightarrow L^{*} is an isomorphism. For such cases, the two viewpoints on scattering diagrams can easily be seen to be equivalent as follows: given a wall parallel to v 𝔡 ⟂ v_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\perp} and having direction − ω 1 ​ ( v 𝔡 ) -\omega_{1}(v_{\mathfrak{d}}) as in the [ GHKK18 ] perspective, applying ω 1 − 1 \omega_{1}^{-1} to the support and direction of the wall yields a wall in our perspective. Paths and the corresponding path-ordered products are similarly related using ω 1 \omega_{1} and ω 1 − 1 \omega_{1}^{-1} to pass between the two perspectives. Thus, existence follows from the usual formulation of the result. 2.4. The quantum and plethystic exponentials 2.4.1. The plethystic exponential We generalize the above via the plethystic exponential . Let R = ℤ ( ( t ) ) ⟦ x 1 , … x n ⟧ R=\mathbb{Z}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu)\llbracket x_{1},\ldots x_{n}\rrbracket and let ℐ ⊂ R \mathscr{I}\subset R be the maximal ideal generated by x 1 , … , x n x_{1},\ldots,x_{n} . We write K = ℤ ≥ 0 n K=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n} and for v ∈ K v\in K we define x v = ∏ x i v i x^{v}=\prod x_{i}^{v_{i}} as usual. The plethystic exponential is a group isomorphism from ℐ \mathscr{I} (viewed as a group under addition) to 1 + ℐ 1+\mathscr{I} , defined by Exp − t , x 1 , … , x n ⁡ ( ∑ 0 ≠ v ∈ K r ∈ ℤ a v ​ r ​ x v ​ ( − t ) r ) = ∏ 0 ≠ v ∈ K r ∈ ℤ ( 1 − x v ​ ( − t ) r ) − a v ​ r . \displaystyle\Exp_{-t,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq v\in K\\ r\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}a_{vr}x^{v}(-t)^{r}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq v\in K\\ r\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}(1-x^{v}(-t)^{r})^{-a_{vr}}. We denote by (24) Log − t , x 1 , … , x n : 1 + ℐ → ℐ \Log_{-t,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}\colon 1+\mathscr{I}\rightarrow\mathscr{I} the inverse isomorphism of groups. Given f ⁡ ( x 1 , … ​ x n , t ) ∈ ℐ f(x_{1},\ldots x_{n},t)\in\mathscr{I} we define 𝔼 ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( x 1 , … ​ x n , t ) ) ≔ Exp − t , x 1 , … , x n ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( x 1 , … ​ x n , t ) ​ ( t + t 3 + t 5 + … ) ) . \EE(f(x_{1},\ldots x_{n},t))\coloneqq\Exp_{-t,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}}\left(f(x_{1},\ldots x_{n},t)(t+t^{3}+t^{5}+\ldots)\right). Note that ( 27 ) fails if we allow odd j j . Similarly, it is straightforward to compute that for any a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} , we have (28) 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t 2 ​ a ​ z v ) = 𝔼 ⁡ ( t 2 ​ a ​ z v ) − 1 = Ψ t ​ ( t 2 ​ a ​ z v ) , \displaystyle\EE(-t^{2a}z^{v})=\EE(t^{2a}z^{v})^{-1}=\Psi_{t}(t^{2a}z^{v}), and (29) 𝔼 ⁡ ( t 2 ​ a + 1 ​ z v ) = ∏ k = 1 ∞ 1 1 − t 2 ​ a + 2 ​ k ​ z v . \displaystyle\EE(t^{2a+1}z^{v})=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-t^{2a+2k}z^{v}}. Lemma 2.14 . Let p ⁡ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] p(t)\in\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}] . Then log ⁡ ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v ) ) \log(\EE(-p(t)z^{v})) is bar-invariant if and only if p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is. Proof. First, assume that p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is bar-invariant. We can write p ⁡ ( t ) = α ⁡ ( t ) + β ⁡ ( t ) p(t)=\upalpha(t)+\upbeta(t) where α ⁡ ( t ) \upalpha(t) is odd and β ⁡ ( t ) \upbeta(t) is even, and both are bar-invariant. Since log ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ ( − α ⁡ ( t ) − β ⁡ ( t ) ) ] = \displaystyle\log\left[\EE(-\upalpha(t)-\upbeta(t))\right]= log ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ ( − α ⁡ ( t ) ) ​ 𝔼 ⁡ ( − β ⁡ ( t ) ) ] \displaystyle\log\left[\EE(-\upalpha(t))\EE(-\upbeta(t))\right] = \displaystyle= log ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ ( − α ⁡ ( t ) ) ] + log ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ ( − β ⁡ ( t ) ) ] , \displaystyle\log\left[\EE(-\upalpha(t))\right]+\log\left[\EE(-\upbeta(t))\right], it suffices to prove the claim for both α ⁡ ( t ) \upalpha(t) and β ⁡ ( t ) \upbeta(t) separately. Write α ⁡ ( t ) = ∑ i α i ​ t i \upalpha(t)=\sum_{i}\upalpha_{i}t^{i} . Then by ( 30 ) we have log ⁡ [ 𝔼 ⁡ ( α ⁡ ( t ) ) ] = \displaystyle\log[\EE(\upalpha(t))]= log ( ∏ r = 0 ∞ ∏ i ∈ ℤ | α i ≠ 0 Ψ t 2 r ( t 2 r ​ i z 2 r ​ v ) α i ) \displaystyle\log\left(\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}\lvert\upalpha_{i}\neq 0}\Psi_{t^{2^{r}}}(t^{2^{r}i}z^{2^{r}v})^{\upalpha_{i}}\right) = \displaystyle= − ∑ r = 0 ∞ ∑ i ∈ ℤ | α i ≠ 0 α i Li ( − t 2 r ​ i z 2 r ​ v ; t 2 r ) \displaystyle-\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}\lvert\upalpha_{i}\neq 0}\upalpha_{i}\Li(-t^{2^{r}i}z^{2^{r}v};t^{2^{r}}) and the statement follows from Li ⁡ ( − t 2 r ​ i ​ z 2 r ​ v , t 2 r ) t ↦ t − 1 = Li ⁡ ( − t − 2 r ​ i ​ z 2 r ​ v , t 2 r ) \Li(-t^{2^{r}i}z^{2^{r}v};t^{2^{r}})_{t\mapsto t^{-1}}=\Li(-t^{-2^{r}i}z^{2^{r}v};t^{2^{r}}) and α i = α − i \upalpha_{i}=\upalpha_{-i} . The proof for β ⁡ ( t ) \upbeta(t) is similar, but simpler. The reverse implication follows from the fact that the z v z^{v} coefficient of log ⁡ ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v ) ) \log(\EE(-p(t)z^{v})) is precisely p ⁡ ( t ) / ( 1 ) t p(t)/(1)_{t} . ∎ 2.4.3. Characteristic function interpretation of the plethystic exponential The operations Exp \Exp and 𝔼 \EE have a natural interpretation in terms of generating functions of graded vector spaces, which we now explain. Let K = ℤ ≥ 0 n K=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n} be as above. Let 𝒞 \mathcal{C} denote the Abelian category of ( K ⊕ ℤ ) (K\oplus\mathbb{Z}) -graded vector spaces. We consider the ℤ \mathbb{Z} -grading as a cohomological grading, and for V ∈ ob ⁡ ( 𝒞 ) V\in\ob(\mathcal{C}) and v ∈ K v\in K we denote by V v V_{v} the cohomologically graded vector space obtained by taking the v v th graded piece with respect to the K K -grading. We denote by Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} the full subcategory of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} containing those objects such that for every i ∈ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z} and v ∈ K v\in K , the vector space V v i V_{v}^{i} is finite-dimensional, and for every v ∈ K v\in K there is an N v ∈ ℤ N_{v}\in\mathbb{Z} such that V v i = 0 V_{v}^{i}=0 for all i ≤ N v i\leq N_{v} . We define the characteristic function of an object of Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} by (31) χ K , t ​ ( V ) = ∑ v ∈ K ∑ i ∈ ℤ dim ( V v i ) ​ x v ​ t i ∈ R . \upchi_{K,t}(V)=\sum_{v\in K}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\dim(V_{v}^{i})x^{v}t^{i}\in R. Given an object V ∈ Vect K + V\in\Vect^{+}_{K} we can form the free supercommutative algebra 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ) \Sym(V) generated by V V , i.e. the quotient of the free tensor algebra of V V by the two-sided ideal spanned by elements of the form a ⊗ b − ( − 1 ) α ​ β ​ b ⊗ a \displaystyle a\otimes b-(-1)^{\upalpha\upbeta}b\otimes a for a a and b b homogeneous elements with respect to the cohomological grading, of degrees α \upalpha and β \upbeta . Decomposing V = V odd ⊕ V even V=V^{\odd}\oplus V^{\even} , with V odd V^{\odd} the summand of V V concentrated in odd cohomological degree, and V even V^{\even} the summand concentrated in even cohomological degree, we have 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ) ≅ Sym ⁡ ( V even ) ⊗ ⋀ ( V odd ) \Sym(V)\cong\CSym(V^{\even})\otimes\bigwedge(V^{\odd}) where Sym ⁡ ( V even ) \CSym(V^{\even}) is the free commutative algebra generated by V even V^{\even} and ⋀ ( V odd ) \bigwedge(V^{\odd}) is the free exterior algebra generated by V odd V^{\odd} . The object 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ) \Sym(V) will again be an object of Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} if V 0 = 0 V_{0}=0 . This condition also implies that χ K , t ​ ( V ) ∈ ℐ \upchi_{K,t}(V)\in\mathscr{I} . The plethystic exponential is uniquely determined by the facts that it is a group homomorphism and satisfies (32) Exp − t , x 1 , … ​ x n ⁡ ( χ K , t ​ ( V ) ) = χ K , t ​ ( 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ) ) \Exp_{-t,x_{1},\ldots x_{n}}(\upchi_{K,t}(V))=\upchi_{K,t}(\Sym(V)) for V ∈ ob ⁡ ( Vect K + ) V\in\ob(\Vect_{K}^{+}) satisfying the condition that V 0 = 0 V_{0}=0 . We write B ≔ H ⁡ ( pt / ℂ ∗ , ℚ ) ​ [ − 1 ] = ℚ ⁡ [ u ] B\coloneqq\HO(\pt/\mathbb{C}^{*},\mathbb{Q})[-1]=\mathbb{Q}[u] where u r u^{r} has cohomological degree 2 ​ r + 1 2r+1 . Then if a ∈ V a\in V has degree ( v , i ) (v,i) , i.e. cohomological degree i i and K K -degree v v , the element a ⊗ u r ∈ V ⊗ B a\otimes u^{r}\in V\otimes B has degree ( v , i + 2 ​ r + 1 ) (v,i+2r+1) . The tensor product V ⊗ B V\otimes B is again an object of Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} . Assuming that V 0 = 0 V_{0}=0 , we may write (33) 𝔼 ⁡ ( χ K , t ​ ( V ) ) = χ K , t ​ ( 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ⊗ H ⁡ ( pt / ℂ ∗ , ℚ ) ​ [ − 1 ] ) ) . \EE(\upchi_{K,t}(V))=\upchi_{K,t}\big(\Sym\big(V\otimes\HO(\pt/\mathbb{C}^{*},\mathbb{Q})[-1]\big)\big). Following [ KS11 , Sec. 6.1] we define the quantum tropical vertex group G qtr G^{\qtrop} to be the closure of the subgroup of G ^ \hat{G} generated by the elements 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v ) \EE(-t^{a}z^{v}) , and we define the quantum tropical vertex algebra 𝔤 qtr ⊂ 𝔤 ^ \mathfrak{g}^{\qtrop}\subset\hat{\mathfrak{g}} to be the image of G qtr G^{\qtrop} under log \log . By ( 28 ) and ( 30 ), 𝔤 qtr \mathfrak{g}^{\qtrop} is a Lie subalgebra of 𝔤 ^ \hat{\mathfrak{g}} , considered as a Lie algebra over ℤ \mathbb{Z} . Precisely, it is the closure of the Lie algebra generated by quantum dilogarithms, which in turn is a Lie subalgebra of our quantum torus Lie algebra 𝔤 ^ L 0 + , ω \hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{L_{0}^{+},\omega} . 2.5. Main result for scattering diagrams The following theorem, for which the proof will be completed in § 5 –§ 7 , is the quantum analog of [ GHKK18 , Thm. 1.13] and is the key to all our other results. Theorem 2.15 . Let 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} be a finite scattering diagram in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 = 𝔤 L 0 + , ω \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{L_{0}^{+},\omega} of the form (34) 𝔇 in = { ( v i ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ( − p i ( t ) z v i ) ) : v i ∈ L 0 + , i = 1 , … , s } , \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\In}=\left\{(v_{i}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-p_{i}(t)z^{v_{i}}))\colon v_{i}\in L_{0}^{+},i=1,\ldots,s\right\}, where p i ​ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ t ± 1 ] p_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[t^{\pm 1}] for all i i . Then there is some 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} representing the equivalence class of Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) such that the incoming walls of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} are precisely the walls ( 34 ), and all walls of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} are of the form (35) ( 𝔡 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) ​ z v 𝔡 ) ) \displaystyle(\mathfrak{d},\EE(-p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t)z^{v_{\mathfrak{d}}})) for various cones 𝔡 ⊂ L ℝ \mathfrak{d}\subset L_{\mathbb{R}} , vectors v 𝔡 ∈ L 0 + v_{\mathfrak{d}}\in L_{0}^{+} , and Laurent polynomials p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) with positive integer coefficients. If each p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) is of pL type, then we can choose the p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) to be of pL type as well. If each p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) is bar-invariant in addition to having positive integer coefficients, then we can take each p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) to be bar-invariant as well. In particular, by ( 28 ) and ( 30 ), we could alternatively find 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} representing Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) such that the set of incoming walls of this 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} forms a scattering diagram equivalent to 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} , and such that all the scattering functions of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} have the form Ψ t ​ ( t 2 ​ a ​ z v ) \Psi_{t}(t^{2a}z^{v}) or Ψ t 2 r ​ ( t 2 r ​ ( 2 ​ a + 1 ) ​ z 2 r ​ v ) \Psi_{t^{2^{r}}}(t^{2^{r}(2a+1)}z^{2^{r}v}) for various a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} , r ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} , and v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} . Proof. For the statements regarding p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) being positive or pL, we will in § 5 inductively reduce to the case of just two initial walls. Then Corollary 7.8 and Proposition 7.12 will prove the positivity and pL statements, respectively, for these two-wall cases. For bar-invariance, recall from Lemma 2.14 that p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is bar-invariant if and only if log ⁡ ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v ) ) \log(\EE(-p(t)z^{v})) is bar-invariant. Note that we can view 𝔤 = 𝔤 L 0 + , ω \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{L_{0}^{+},\omega} as a Lie algebra over 𝕜 \Bbbk rather than 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] \Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}] , and then we can consider the Lie subalgebra 𝔤 bar \mathfrak{g}^{\barr} (over 𝕜 \Bbbk ) consisting of the bar-invariant elements. So the bar-invariance of the Laurent polynomials p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) implies that we can view 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} as a scattering diagram over 𝔤 bar \mathfrak{g}^{\barr} , and then applying Theorem 2.13 over 𝔤 bar \mathfrak{g}^{\barr} yields 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} defined over 𝔤 bar \mathfrak{g}^{\barr} as well. Then each p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) must be bar-invariant, as desired. ∎ Remark 2.16 . In [ Bou20b , §8] , the polynomials p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) of ( 35 ) for certain scattering diagrams are interpreted as (higher-genus) log BPS invariants. Theorem 2.15 thus recovers the integrality result of [ Bou20b , Thm. 33] , and furthermore, proves positivity for these log BPS invariants, as well as bar-invariance and pL type (we note that positivity for acyclic cases was proved in [ Bou20b , Prop. 41] ). Theorem 2.19 also yields a parity result for these log BPS invariants. Theorem 2.15 combined with ( 23 ) will imply that the coefficients of the monomials attached to our broken lines in § 3 are Laurent polynomials in t t with positive integer coefficients. This is the key to proving Theorems 3.9 and 3.10 , hence Theorem 1.1 . Conjecture 2.17 . For 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} as in ( 34 ), if each p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) is of Lefschetz type, then we can choose 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} representing Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) so that all walls are as in ( 35 ) with each p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) of Lefschetz type. For cases of interest for studying theta bases of quantum cluster algebras associated to acyclic quivers, we can in fact prove Conjecture 2.17 : Theorem 2.18 . Assume that 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} is as in ( 34 ), and furthermore that each p i ​ ( t ) = 1 p_{i}(t)=1 , and also that the s × s s\times s matrix ( ω ⁡ ( v i , v j ) ) i , j (\omega(v_{i},v_{j}))_{i,j} is the signed adjacency matrix of an acyclic quiver Q Q . Then Conjecture 2.17 holds for Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) . This follows by combining results of [ Bri17 ] and [ MR19 ] . Since both of these papers are written in terms of DT theory, we defer the proof to § 7.2 , where the result will appear as part of Proposition 7.7 . Then in § 7.2.3 , we will combine Proposition 7.7 with Theorem 2.15 to prove the following. Theorem 2.19 . Broken lines and theta bases 3.1. Definitions and basic properties Fix a consistent scattering diagram 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . Let p ∈ L p\in L , 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ ∖ Supp ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\Supp(\mathfrak{D}) . A broken line γ \upgamma with respect to 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) is the data of a continuous map γ : ( − ∞ , 0 ] → L ℝ ∖ Joints ( 𝔇 ) \upgamma\colon(-\infty,0]\rightarrow L_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\Joints(\mathfrak{D}) , values − ∞ < τ 0 ≤ τ 1 ≤ … ≤ τ ℓ = 0 -\infty<\uptau_{0}\leq\uptau_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\uptau_{\ell}=0 , and for each i = 0 , … , ℓ i=0,\ldots,\ell , an associated monomial c i ​ z v i ∈ 𝕜 t ​ [ L ] c_{i}z^{v_{i}}\in\Bbbk_{t}[L] , with c i ∈ 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] c_{i}\in\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}] and v i ∈ L v_{i}\in L , such that: (i) γ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 𝒬 \upgamma(0)=\mathcal{Q} . (ii) For i = 1 ​ … , ℓ i=1\ldots,\ell , γ ′ ​ ( τ ) = − v i \upgamma^{\prime}(\uptau)=-v_{i} for all τ ∈ ( τ i − 1 , τ i ) \uptau\in(\uptau_{i-1},\uptau_{i}) . Similarly, γ ′ ​ ( τ ) = − v 0 \upgamma^{\prime}(\uptau)=-v_{0} for all τ ∈ ( − ∞ , τ 0 ) \uptau\in(-\infty,\uptau_{0}) . (iii) c 0 = 1 c_{0}=1 and v 0 = p v_{0}=p . (iv) For i = 0 , … , ℓ − 1 i=0,\ldots,\ell-1 , γ ⁡ ( τ i ) ∈ Supp ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) \upgamma(\uptau_{i})\in\Supp(\mathfrak{D}) . Let (38) f i ≔ ∏ ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 𝔡 ∋ γ ⁡ ( τ i ) f 𝔡 sgn ⁡ ω ⁡ ( v 𝔡 , v i ) ∈ G ^ . \displaystyle f_{i}\coloneqq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D}\\ \mathfrak{d}\ni\upgamma(\uptau_{i})\end{subarray}}f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\sign\omega(v_{\mathfrak{d}},v_{i})}\in\hat{G}. I.e., f i f_{i} is the ϵ → 0 \epsilon\rightarrow 0 limit of the element θ γ | ( τ i − ϵ , τ i + ϵ ) , 𝔇 \uptheta_{\upgamma|_{(\uptau_{i}-\epsilon,\uptau_{i}+\epsilon)},\mathfrak{D}} defined in ( 16 ) (using a smoothing of γ \upgamma ). Then c i + 1 ​ z v i + 1 c_{i+1}z^{v_{i+1}} is a homogeneous (with respect to the L L -grading) term of Ad f i ⁡ ( c i ​ z v i ) \Ad_{f_{i}}(c_{i}z^{v_{i}}) , not equal to c i ​ z v i c_{i}z^{v_{i}} (this term c i ​ z v i c_{i}z^{v_{i}} is instead obtained by forgetting the time τ i \uptau_{i} from the data). If every wall of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} has apex at the origin, then we say that 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} is generic if 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is not in v ⟂ v^{\perp} for any v ∈ L ∗ v\in L^{*} . For more general 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , we call 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} generic as long as it is not in some bad 5 5 5 Specifically, 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ ∖ Supp ⁡ ( 𝔇 ) \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\Supp(\mathfrak{D}) is generic unless it is an endpoint for some “degenerate broken line” which passes through a joint, cf. [ CPS , Rmk. 4.5] . measure-zero subset of L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} (we shall have no need for broken lines associated to these more general 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} ). For each p ∈ L p\in L and each generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , we define an element ϑ p , 𝒬 ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket which we view as the local expression of a theta function in a chart corresponding to 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . For any p ∈ L p\in L , we define (39) ϑ p , 𝒬 ≔ ∑ Ends ⁡ ( γ ) = ( p , 𝒬 ) c γ ​ z v γ ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \displaystyle\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\sum_{\Ends(\upgamma)=(p,\mathcal{Q})}c_{\upgamma}z^{v_{\upgamma}}\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket (the fact that ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is well-defined is part of Proposition 3.1 ). Here, the sum is over all broken lines γ \upgamma with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) , and c γ ​ z v γ c_{\upgamma}z^{v_{\upgamma}} denotes the monomial attached to the final straight segment of γ \upgamma . In particular, we always have ϑ 0 , 𝒬 = 1 \vartheta_{0,\mathcal{Q}}=1 . We define A 𝒬 can A_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\can} to be the 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra generated by the set of theta functions Θ 𝒬 ≔ { ϑ p , 𝒬 : p ∈ L } \Theta_{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\{\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\colon p\in L\} . The following is essentially [ Man , Prop. 2.13] , but we include a more detailed proof here for completeness. We note that exactly the same argument can be used in the classical setting. Proposition 3.1 . For fixed generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} and any p ∈ L p\in L , ( 39 ) gives a well-defined element ϑ p , 𝒬 ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket of the form (40) ϑ p , 𝒬 = z p ​ ( 1 + ∑ v ∈ L 0 + a v ​ z v ) . \displaystyle\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}=z^{p}\left(1+\sum_{v\in L_{0}^{+}}a_{v}z^{v}\right). Furthermore, the theta functions Θ 𝒬 ≔ { ϑ p , 𝒬 : p ∈ L } \Theta_{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\{\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\colon p\in L\} form a topological 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module basis for A 𝒬 ≔ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ A_{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . Hence, Θ 𝒬 \Theta_{\mathcal{Q}} also forms a topological basis for the subalgebra A 𝒬 can ⊂ A 𝒬 A^{\can}_{\mathcal{Q}}\subset A_{\mathcal{Q}} . Proof. Given n ∈ L + ⊂ L n\in L^{+}\subset L , let δ ⁡ ( n ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 \updelta(n)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} denote the largest number k k such that n ∈ k ​ L + n\in kL^{+} as defined in ( 9 ). We extend this to δ : L ⊕ → ℤ ≥ 0 \updelta\colon L^{\oplus}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} by taking δ ⁡ ( 0 ) ≔ 0 \updelta(0)\coloneqq 0 . Note that δ ⁡ ( n 1 + n 2 ) ≥ δ ⁡ ( n 1 ) + δ ⁡ ( n 2 ) \updelta(n_{1}+n_{2})\geq\updelta(n_{1})+\updelta(n_{2}) for any n 1 , n 2 ∈ L ⊕ n_{1},n_{2}\in L^{\oplus} . Since 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} is L 0 + L_{0}^{+} -graded and acts on 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket by L L -graded 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -derivations, we see that for any v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} , f ∈ G ^ v ∥ f\in\hat{G}_{v}^{\parallel} , c ∈ 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] c\in\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}] , and p ∈ L p\in L , the element Ad f ⁡ ( c ​ z p ) ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Ad_{f}(cz^{p})\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket must have the form Ad f ⁡ ( c ​ z p ) = c ​ z p ​ ( 1 + ∑ k ∈ ℤ > 0 c k ​ z k ​ v ) \displaystyle\Ad_{f}(cz^{p})=cz^{p}\left(1+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}c_{k}z^{kv}\right) for some collection of coefficients c k ∈ 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] c_{k}\in\Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}] . From this it is clear that if γ \upgamma is a broken line with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) , then v γ ∈ p + L ⊕ v_{\upgamma}\in p+L^{\oplus} . Furthermore, we see that v γ = p v_{\upgamma}=p if and only if γ \upgamma is the unique straight broken line with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) , and in this case we have c γ ​ z γ = z p c_{\upgamma}z^{\upgamma}=z^{p} . In particular, once we show that ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is well-defined, it will immediately follow that it has the form given in ( 40 ). Now to show that θ p , 𝒬 \uptheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is well-defined, we need to show that for each fixed v ∈ p + L + v\in p+L^{+} , there are only finitely many broken lines γ \upgamma with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) and with v γ = v v_{\upgamma}=v . Suppose ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 ∖ 𝔇 δ ⁡ ( v − p ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\updelta(v-p)} . Then if ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) contributes to a bend of a broken line γ \upgamma with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) , we have δ ⁡ ( v γ − p ) > δ ⁡ ( v − p ) \updelta(v_{\upgamma}-p)>\updelta(v-p) . Thus, it suffices to restrict to the finite scattering diagram 𝔇 δ ⁡ ( v − p ) \mathfrak{D}_{\updelta(v-p)} . By starting at τ = 0 \uptau=0 and flowing backwards, we see that a broken line γ \upgamma with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) and v γ = v v_{\upgamma}=v is uniquely determined by its kinks, i.e., by the data of where it bends and the degrees δ \updelta which it bends by. But since each kink increases δ ⁡ ( v γ − p ) \updelta(v_{\upgamma}-p) , such γ \upgamma can have at most δ ⁡ ( v − p ) \updelta(v-p) many kinks, and since 𝔇 δ ⁡ ( v − p ) \mathfrak{D}_{\updelta(v-p)} is finite, there are only finitely many places where γ \upgamma can cross a wall of 𝔇 δ ⁡ ( v − p ) \mathfrak{D}_{\updelta(v-p)} . This shows that there are only finitely many such γ \upgamma , as desired, and so ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is well-defined. Finally, since ( 40 ) says that ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is p p -pointed, Lemma 2.4 tells us that Θ 𝒬 \Theta_{\mathcal{Q}} is indeed a topological basis for 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . ∎ The theta functions also satisfy the following important compatibility condition, due to [ CPS ] in the classical limit and [ Man , Thm 2.14] in the quantum setup. Properties preserved by adjoint actions The following lemma implies that if 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} has the form of ( 35 ) with each p 𝔡 ​ ( t ) p_{\mathfrak{d}}(t) being bar-invariant and having positive integer coefficients, then the theta functions defined with respect to 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} will be bar-invariant as well. Lemma 3.4 . Let v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} , p ∈ L p\in L , and ϵ ≔ sgn ⁡ ω ⁡ ( v , p ) \epsilon\coloneqq\sign\omega(v,p) . Suppose p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) is a bar-invariant positive Laurent polynomial in t t . Then Ad 𝔼 ⁡ ( − ϵ ​ p ​ ( t ) ​ z v ) ⁡ ( z p ) \Ad_{\EE\left(-\epsilon p(t)z^{v}\right)}(z^{p}) is bar-invariant. Proof. This can be seen using ( 28 ) or ( 30 ) to relate 𝔼 ⁡ ( − ϵ ​ p ​ ( t ) ​ z v ) \EE(-\epsilon p(t)z^{v}) to a product of functions of the form Ψ t a ​ ( t − b ​ z c ​ v ) ​ Ψ t a ​ ( t b ​ z c ​ v ) \Psi_{t^{a}}(t^{-b}z^{cv})\Psi_{t^{a}}(t^{b}z^{cv}) for various a , b , c a,b,c , and then applying ( 23 ) to compute how these act on z p z^{p} . Alternatively, by Lemma 2.14 , bar-invariance of p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) implies bar-invariance of log ⁡ ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v ) ) \log(\EE\left(-p(t)z^{v}\right)) , and then the claim follows from noting that the adjoint action of bar-invariant elements of 𝔤 ^ L 0 + , ω \hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{L_{0}^{+},\omega} on 𝕜 σ , t ω ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{\upsigma,t}^{\omega}\llbracket L\rrbracket preserves bar-invariance, and so exp ⁡ ( [ log ⁡ ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v ) ) , ⋅ ] ) \exp([\log(\EE(-p(t)z^{v})),\cdot]) does as well. ∎ The next lemma implies that if Conjecture 2.17 holds, then the coefficients of the theta functions are in fact of Lefschetz type as predicted in Conjecture 1.4 , cf. Conjecture 3.13 . In particular, combining Theorem 2.18 with Lemma 3.5 will yield Theorem 1.5 , cf. § 4.9 . Lemma 3.5 . Let v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} , p ∈ L p\in L , and let ϵ ≔ sgn ⁡ ω ⁡ ( v , p ) \epsilon\coloneqq\sign\omega(v,p) . Then Ad 𝔼 ⁡ ( − ϵ ​ [ a ] t ​ z v ) ⁡ ( z p ) = ∑ r ≥ 0 p r ​ ( t ) ​ z p + r ​ v \Ad_{\EE\left(-\epsilon[a]_{t}z^{v}\right)}(z^{p})=\sum_{r\geq 0}p_{r}(t)z^{p+rv} where the Laurent polynomials p r ​ ( t ) p_{r}(t) are of Lefschetz type. Proof. Let K = ℤ ≥ 0 K=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} and let V V be an integrable K K -graded 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 \mathfrak{sl}_{2} -representation. Let h h be a nonzero element in the Cartan subalgebra of 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 \mathfrak{sl}_{2} . Main results for theta functions For the entirety of § 3.3 we assume that 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} is as in ( 34 ) with each p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) a Laurent polynomial in t t with positive integer coefficients, and 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is a consistent scattering diagram representing Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) and having one of the forms described in Theorem 2.15 . 3.3.1. Positivity Equation ( 23 ), together with the definition of a broken line, implies the following: Lemma 3.8 . Let 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} be a consistent scattering diagram representing Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) as in Theorem 2.15 such that all scattering functions have the form Ψ t ​ ( t 2 ​ a ​ z v ) \Psi_{t}(t^{2a}z^{v}) or Ψ t 2 r ​ ( t 2 r ​ ( 2 ​ a + 1 ) ​ z 2 r ​ v ) \Psi_{t^{2^{r}}}(t^{2^{r}(2a+1)}z^{2^{r}v}) for various a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} , r ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . Let γ \upgamma be a broken line with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) , and let c ​ z v cz^{v} be the monomial attached to some straight segment of γ \upgamma , other than the initial straight segment (whose attached monomial is z p z^{p} ). Then v ∈ p + L + ⊂ L v\in p+L^{+}\subset L , and c c is a Laurent polynomial in t t with positive integer coefficients. The following is an immediate corollary of Lemma 3.8 together with the definition of ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} . Theorem 3.9 (Universal positivity) . For any p ∈ L p\in L and generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , the function ϑ p , 𝒬 ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket has the form ϑ p , 𝒬 = z p + ∑ v ∈ L + c v ​ z p + v \displaystyle\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}=z^{p}+\sum_{v\in L^{+}}c_{v}z^{p+v} where each c v c_{v} is a Laurent polynomial in t t with positive integer coefficients. Similarly, the following is a corollary of Lemma 3.8 and ( 41 ). Theorem 3.10 (Strong positivity) . For any p 1 , p 2 ∈ L p_{1},p_{2}\in L , ϑ p 1 ​ ϑ p 2 = ϑ p 1 + p 2 + ∑ v ∈ L + α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p 1 + p 2 + v ) ​ ϑ p 1 + p 2 + v , \vartheta_{p_{1}}\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\vartheta_{p_{1}+p_{2}}+\sum_{v\in L^{+}}\upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p_{1}+p_{2}+v)\vartheta_{p_{1}+p_{2}+v}, where each α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p 1 + p 2 + v ) ∈ 𝕜 t \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p_{1}+p_{2}+v)\in\Bbbk_{t} is positive. More generally, all structure constants α ⁡ ( p 1 , … , p s , p ) ∈ 𝕜 t \upalpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{s};p)\in\Bbbk_{t} are positive. 3.3.2. Parity Using Theorem 2.19 and applying Lemma 3.7 inductively, we obtain the following result concerning the parities of the coefficients of the theta functions. Proposition 3.11 . Then in the expression ϑ p , 𝒬 = z p + ∑ v ∈ L + c v ​ ( t ) ​ z p + v , \displaystyle\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}=z^{p}+\sum_{v\in L^{+}}c_{v}(t)z^{p+v}, each c v ​ ( t ) c_{v}(t) is even or odd, with parity given by that of ω ⁡ ( v , p ) + λ ⁡ ( a , a ) \omega(v,p)+\lambda(a,a) , where a a is determined by v = ∑ i = 1 s a i ​ v i v=\sum_{i=1}^{s}a_{i}v_{i} , and λ \lambda is as in ( 36 ).
