Bar-invariance, pre-Lefschetz, and Lefschetz type The following is an immediate consequence of Lemmas 3.4 and 3.6 . Proposition 3.12 . Suppose 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} is as in ( 34 ) with each p i ​ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ t ± 1 ] p_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[t^{\pm 1}] being pL (respectively, bar-invariant), and that 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is the resulting consistent scattering diagram with pL (respectively, bar-invariant) scattering functions as in Theorem 2.15 . If c ​ z v cz^{v} is the monomial attached to a straight segment of a broken line γ \upgamma defined with respect to 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , then c c is pL (respectively, bar-invariant). Consequently, for any p ∈ L p\in L and generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , the coefficients c v c_{v} as in Theorem 3.9 are pL (respectively, bar-invariant). As previously noted, if the first part of Conjecture 2.17 is true, then by Lemma 3.5 , we could deduce the following conjecture in the same way as Theorems 3.10 and 3.9 : Conjecture 3.13 . Assume that 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} is as in ( 34 ) and satisfies the stronger assumption that each of the Laurent polynomials p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) is of Lefschetz type. Then for p ∈ L p\in L and generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , the coefficients c v c_{v} in Theorem 3.9 are of Lefschetz type. Conjecture 1.4 is then a restatement of special cases of Conjecture 3.13 . For cases corresponding to acyclic quivers, we can in fact prove Conjecture 3.13 by combining Theorem 2.18 with Lemma 3.5 , yielding the following: Theorem 3.14 . Assume that 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} is as in ( 34 ), and satisfies the stronger assumption that each of the polynomials p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) is equal to 1 1 , and also that the s × s s\times s matrix ( ω ⁡ ( v i , v j ) ) i , j (\omega(v_{i},v_{j}))_{i,j} is the signed adjacency matrix of an acyclic quiver and is nondegenerate. Then Conjecture 3.13 holds. 3.3.4. An application of positivity One of the most useful consequences of positivity is that it ensures that broken lines and structure constants do not vanish when applying lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} . We illustrate this in the proof of the following lemma, which will be useful when proving Theorem 1.6 in § 4.9 . Lemma 3.15 . Consider subsets Θ ⊂ Θ ′ ⊂ L \Theta\subset\Theta^{\prime}\subset L , and let A Θ A_{\Theta} denote the subalgeba generated by { ϑ p } p ∈ Θ \{\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Theta} (e.g., A Θ = A can A_{\Theta}=A^{\can} if Θ = L \Theta=L ). If the classical limits of the theta functions { lim t → 1 ϑ p } p ∈ Θ ′ \{\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Theta^{\prime}} form a 𝕜 \Bbbk -module basis for lim t → 1 ( A Θ ) \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}(A_{\Theta}) , then the theta functions { ϑ p } p ∈ Θ ′ \{\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Theta^{\prime}} form a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module basis for A Θ A_{\Theta} (not just a topological basis). Proof. By definition, A Θ A_{\Theta} is spanned over 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} by products of theta functions, so it suffices to check that each product ∏ i = 1 s ϑ p i \prod_{i=1}^{s}\vartheta_{p_{i}} of theta functions (with p i ∈ Θ p_{i}\in\Theta for each i i ) can be expressed as a finite 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -linear combination of theta functions ϑ p \vartheta_{p} for p ∈ Θ ′ p\in\Theta^{\prime} . Note that strong positivity in the quantum setting (i.e., Theorem 3.10 ) implies that α ⁡ ( p 1 , … , p s , p ) \upalpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{s};p) is nonzero if and only if its classical limit is nonzero. Hence, for p 1 , … , p s ∈ Θ p_{1},\ldots,p_{s}\in\Theta , we have that α ⁡ ( p 1 , … , p s , p ) \upalpha(p_{1},\ldots,p_{s};p) is zero except at a finite collection of p ∈ Θ ′ p\in\Theta^{\prime} , as desired, simply because the same is true in the classical limit. ∎ 3.3.5. Atomicity Recall that for each generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , we have an isomorphism ι 𝒬 : A ⟶ ∼ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon A\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . Given f ∈ A f\in A , let f 𝒬 ≔ ι 𝒬 ​ ( f ) ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ f_{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(f)\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket . One says that a nonzero element f ∈ A f\in A is universally positive (with respect to the scattering atlas) if for each generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , the coefficients of f 𝒬 ∈ 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ f_{\mathcal{Q}}\in\Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket are Laurent polynomials in t t with non-negative integer coefficients. We say that a universally positive function f f is atomic (or indecomposable) if it cannot be written as a sum of two other universally positive functions. We obtain the following by the same argument used to prove the atomicity of the classical theta basis in [ Man17 , Thm. 1] . Theorem 3.16 . The quantum theta functions are the atomic elements of A A . Proof. Theorem 3.9 tells us that the quantum theta functions are universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas. To show atomicity, it thus suffices to show that for any f ∈ A f\in A universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas, the expansion f = ∑ p ∈ L a p ​ ϑ p f=\sum_{p\in L}a_{p}\vartheta_{p} has positive integer coefficients. For any p ∈ L p\in L , one can choose a positive integer k k which is large enough to ensure that that p ∉ p ′ + k ​ L 0 + p\notin p^{\prime}+kL_{0}^{+} for any p ′ p^{\prime} with a p ′ ≠ 0 a_{p^{\prime}}\neq 0 . Then for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} sharing a chamber of 𝔇 k \mathfrak{D}_{k} with p p , the only possible broken line with respect to 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , with ends ( p ′ , 𝒬 ) (p^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}) for a p ′ ≠ 0 a_{p^{\prime}}\neq 0 , and with v γ = p v_{\upgamma}=p is the straight broken line with attached monomial z p z^{p} . It follows that for 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} sufficiently close to p p , a p a_{p} is equal to c p c_{p} in the formal Laurent series expansion ι 𝒬 ​ ( f ) = ∑ p ∈ L c p ​ z p \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(f)=\sum_{p\in L}c_{p}z^{p} . This is indeed positive by the universal positivity assumption on f f . ∎ 3.3.6. Homogeneity of theta functions The classical theta functions are known to be eigenfunctions for various torus actions, cf. [ GHKK18 , Prop. 7.7 and Prop. 7.19] for the 𝒜 prin \mathcal{A}^{\prin} and 𝒜 \mathcal{A} cases, respectively, and also [ GHK15b , Thm. 5.2] for what can be interpreted as a special case of a torus action on the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -space. In other words, the theta functions are homogeneous with respect to certain gradings. This property is important since, for example, the weight spaces of these torus actions on 𝒜 prin \mathcal{A}^{\prin} and 𝒜 \mathcal{A} are related to spaces of global sections of line bundles on 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and on various (partial) compactifications of 𝒳 \mathcal{X} — cf. [ GHK15a , §4] and [ Man19 ] , respectively. Applications of this to representation theory are given in [ GHKK18 , §0.4] and in [ Mag , Mag20 ] . These results on the homogeneity of theta functions for various gradings are all special cases of the following general observation (which holds in the quantum and classical settings): Proposition 3.17 (Homogeneity of theta functions) . Let κ : L → K \kappa\colon L\rightarrow K be a morphism of lattices such that the directions of all walls of 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} (hence all walls of Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) ) lie in ker ⁡ ( κ ) \ker(\kappa) . Then Ad α \Ad_{\upalpha} with α = θ γ , 𝔇 \upalpha=\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}} defined as in ( 16 ) respects the K K -grading on the algebras A 𝒬 A_{\mathcal{Q}} , and so κ \kappa canonically determines a K K -grading on A can A^{\can} and on any A Θ A_{\Theta} as in Lemma 3.15 . The theta functions are homogeneous with respect to this grading, and deg ⁡ ( ϑ p ) = κ ⁡ ( p ) \deg(\vartheta_{p})=\kappa(p) . 3.3.7. Fibration over a torus The following will yield Theorem 1.2 (9). In the classical setting, it essentially says that Spec \Spec of an algebra of theta functions can be viewed as a flat family over an algebraic torus such that each fiber has a theta basis which is canonical up to a global re-scaling. Proposition 3.18 . Let H ≔ { u ∈ L : ω ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 ​ for all ​ v ∈ L + } H\coloneqq\{u\in L\colon\omega(u,v)=0\mbox{ for all }v\in L^{+}\} . Let τ u \uptau_{u} denote the 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module automorphism of 𝕜 t ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}\llbracket L\rrbracket taking z v z^{v} to z v + u z^{v+u} for each v ∈ L v\in L . Then for u ∈ H u\in H , p ∈ L p\in L , and any generic 𝒬 ∈ L ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathbb{R}} , we have ϑ p + u , 𝒬 = τ u ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ) \vartheta_{p+u,\mathcal{Q}}=\uptau_{u}(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}) . Proof. Note that H ℝ H_{\mathbb{R}} is parallel to all walls of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , so we can project to L ℝ / H ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}}/H_{\mathbb{R}} without changing the set of walls crossed by a broken line. Also, u ∈ H u\in H implies that g ​ z u = z u ​ g gz^{u}=z^{u}g for all g ∈ G ^ g\in\hat{G} , hence Ad g ⁡ ( τ u ​ z v ) = Ad g ⁡ ( t − ω ⁡ ( u , v ) ​ z u ​ z v ) = t − ω ⁡ ( u , v ) ​ z u ​ Ad g ⁡ ( z v ) = τ u ​ ( Ad g ⁡ ( z v ) ) . \displaystyle\Ad_{g}(\uptau_{u}z^{v})=\Ad_{g}(t^{-\omega(u,v)}z^{u}z^{v})=t^{-\omega(u,v)}z^{u}\Ad_{g}(z^{v})=\uptau_{u}(\Ad_{g}(z^{v})). It follows that the projections to L ℝ / H ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}}/H_{\mathbb{R}} of the broken lines with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) are precisely the same as for the projections of the broken lines with ends ( p + u , 𝒬 ) (p+u,\mathcal{Q}) , except that the monomials attached to the straight segments of the latter broken lines are obtained by applying τ u \uptau_{u} to the corresponding monomials in the former set of broken lines. The proposition then follows. ∎ 4. Quantum cluster algebras 4.1. Seeds We review the definitions of seeds and cluster algebras from the perspective of [ FG09 ] . A (skew-symmetric) seed is a collection of data (46) S = { N , I , E ≔ { e i } i ∈ I , F , B } , \displaystyle S=\{N,I,E\coloneqq\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I},F,B\}, where N N is a finitely generated free Abelian group, I I is a finite index-set, E E is a basis for N N indexed by I I , F F is a subset of I I , and B ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) B(\cdot,\cdot) is a skew-symmetric ℚ \mathbb{Q} -valued bilinear form on N N such that B ⁡ ( e i , e j ) ∈ ℤ B(e_{i},e_{j})\in\mathbb{Z} whenever i i and j j are not both in F F . When i ∈ F i\in F , we say e i e_{i} is a frozen vector . Let N uf N_{\uf} denote the span of { e i } i ∈ I ∖ F \{e_{i}\}_{i\in I\setminus F} in N N . If the seed S S is not clear from the context, we may write the data with subscripts S S to clarify, e.g., S = { N S , I S , E S = { e S , i } , F S , B S } S=\{N_{S},I_{S},E_{S}=\{e_{S,i}\},F_{S},B_{S}\} . Let M = N ∗ ≔ Hom ℤ ⁡ ( N , ℤ ) M=N^{*}\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(N,\mathbb{Z}) . We define elements e i ∗ ∈ M e^{*}_{i}\in M by e i ∗ ​ ( e j ) = δ i ​ j e^{*}_{i}(e_{j})=\updelta_{ij} , i.e. the e i ∗ e^{*}_{i} form a dual basis to the e i e_{i} . We define maps B 1 B_{1} and B 2 B_{2} from N N to M M by B 1 : n ↦ B ⁡ ( n , ⋅ ) B_{1}\colon n\mapsto B(n,\cdot) and B 2 : n ↦ B ⁡ ( ⋅ , n ) B_{2}\colon n\mapsto B(\cdot,n) . Given a seed S S and a skew-symmetric ℚ \mathbb{Q} -valued bilinear form Λ \Uplambda on M M , we say (following the quantization of cluster algebras from [ BZ05 ] ) that ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) forms a compatible pair if 6 6 6 Our B B is actually what [ FG09 ] would call ϵ \epsilon , and this is the transpose of what is typically called B B in the Fomin-Zelevinsky [ FZ02 ] perspective used by [ BZ05 ] . Λ ⁡ ( ⋅ , B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) = e i for each i ∈ I ∖ F . \displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c l}\Uplambda(\cdot,B_{1}(e_{i}))=e_{i}&\mbox{ for each $i\in I\setminus F$.}\end{array} Note that the existence of such a Λ \Uplambda is equivalent to the condition that B 1 | N uf B_{1}|_{N_{\uf}} is injective (called the “Injectivity Assumption” in [ GHKK18 ] ). Also note that Λ ⁡ ( u , v ) ∈ ℤ \Uplambda(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z} for any u ∈ B 1 ​ ( N uf ) u\in B_{1}(N_{\uf}) , v ∈ M v\in M . Given a seed S S as above, one associates another seed S prin S^{\prin} defined as follows: • N S prin ≔ N ⊕ M N_{S^{\prin}}\coloneqq N\oplus M , often written as just N prin N^{\prin} . • I S prin I_{S^{\prin}} is the disjoint union of two copies of I I . We will call them I 1 I_{1} and I 2 I_{2} to distinguish between them. • E S prin ≔ { ( e i , 0 ) : i ∈ I 1 } ∪ { ( 0 , e i ∗ ) : i ∈ I 2 } E_{S^{\prin}}\coloneqq\{(e_{i},0)\colon i\in I_{1}\}\cup\{(0,e_{i}^{*})\colon i\in I_{2}\} . • F S prin ≔ F 1 ∪ I 2 F_{S^{\prin}}\coloneqq F_{1}\cup I_{2} , where F 1 F_{1} is F F viewed as a subset of I 1 I_{1} . • B S prin ​ ( ( n 1 , m 1 ) , ( n 2 , m 2 ) ) ≔ B ⁡ ( n 1 , n 2 ) + m 2 ​ ( n 1 ) − m 1 ​ ( n 2 ) B_{S^{\prin}}((n_{1},m_{1}),(n_{2},m_{2}))\coloneqq B(n_{1},n_{2})+m_{2}(n_{1})-m_{1}(n_{2}) . We denote the analog of B 1 B_{1} for B S prin B_{S^{\prin}} by B 1 prin : N prin → M prin ≔ ( N prin ) ∗ = M ⊕ N B^{\prin}_{1}\colon N^{\prin}\rightarrow M^{\prin}\coloneqq(N^{\prin})^{*}=M\oplus N , identifying N = N ∗ ⁣ ∗ N=N^{**} . I.e., (48) B 1 prin : ( n , m ) ↦ B S prin ​ ( ( n , m ) , ⋅ ) = ( B 1 ​ ( n ) − m , n ) . \displaystyle B^{\prin}_{1}\colon(n,m)\mapsto B_{S^{\prin}}((n,m),\cdot)=(B_{1}(n)-m,n). Note that the form B S prin B_{S^{\prin}} is unimodular (i.e., has determinant 1 1 ) on N S prin N_{S^{\prin}} by the argument given for ω ∘ \omega^{\circ} in § 2.3.4 . Thus, B 1 prin B^{\prin}_{1} is an isomorphism, and so one can always find a skew-symmetric form Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} on M prin M^{\prin} such that ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) forms a compatible pair. For example, we may take Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} to be the skew-symmetric bilinear form on M prin M^{\prin} determined by (49) Λ prin ​ ( B 1 prin ​ ( a ) , B 1 prin ​ ( b ) ) = B S prin ​ ( a , b ) . \displaystyle\Uplambda^{\prin}(B^{\prin}_{1}(a),B^{\prin}_{1}(b))=B_{S^{\prin}}(a,b). Alternatively, if ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) is a compatible pair, we can obtain a compatible Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} for S prin S^{\prin} by taking Λ prin = ρ ∗ ​ Λ \Uplambda^{\prin}=\rho^{*}\Uplambda where ρ \rho is the map M ⊕ N → M M\oplus N\rightarrow M , ( m , n ) ↦ m (m,n)\mapsto m . That is, we can choose Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} to be given by (50) Λ prin ​ ( ( m 1 , n 1 ) , ( m 2 , n 2 ) ) ≔ Λ ⁡ ( m 1 , m 2 ) \displaystyle\Uplambda^{\prin}((m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2}))\coloneqq\Uplambda(m_{1},m_{2}) for all ( m 1 , n 1 ) , ( m 2 , n 2 ) ∈ M prin (m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2})\in M^{\prin} . 4.2. Construction of quantum cluster algebras Given a compatible pair ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) , we define the quantum torus algebra 𝒜 q S ≔ 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] \mathcal{A}_{q}^{S}\coloneqq\Bbbk^{\Uplambda}_{t}[M] as in ( 8 ). For each i ∈ I i\in I , we denote A S , i ≔ z e S , i ∗ ∈ 𝒜 q S \displaystyle A_{S,i}\coloneqq z^{e_{S,i}^{*}}\in\mathcal{A}_{q}^{S} where { e S , i ∗ } i ∈ I \{e_{S,i}^{*}\}_{i\in I} is the basis for M M dual to E S E_{S} . Let (51) C S + ≔ { m ∈ M ℝ : ⟨ e i , m ⟩ ≥ 0 ​ ∀ i ∈ I ∖ F } . \displaystyle C_{S}^{+}\coloneqq\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\langle e_{i},m\rangle\geq 0~\forall i\in I\setminus F\}. Let 𝒜 ̊ q S \mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} denote the skew-field of fractions of 𝒜 q S \mathcal{A}_{q}^{S} — cf. [ BZ05 , §11] for a brief review of skew-fields of fractions including a proof of the fact that quantum torus algebras satisfy the (left) Ore condition and thus have well-defined (right) skew-fields of fractions, into which they embed. Similarly, we obtain from S S (without needing the existence of a compatible Λ \Uplambda ) another quantum torus algebra 𝒳 q S ≔ 𝕜 t B ​ [ N ] . \mathcal{X}_{q}^{S}\coloneqq\Bbbk^{B}_{t}[N]. We write 𝒳 ̊ q S \mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} for the skew-field of fractions of 𝒳 q S \mathcal{X}_{q}^{S} . Given a seed S = ( N , I , E = { e i } i ∈ I , F , B ) S=(N,I,E=\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I},F,B) , the mutation of S S with respect to j ∈ I ∖ F j\in I\setminus F is the seed μ j ​ ( S ) ≔ ( N , I , E ′ = { e i ′ } i ∈ I , F , B ) \upmu_{j}(S)\coloneqq(N,I,E^{\prime}=\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in I},F,B) , where the vectors e i ′ e_{i}^{\prime} are defined by e i ′ ≔ μ j ​ ( e i ) ≔ { e i + max ⁡ ( 0 , B ⁡ ( e i , e j ) ) ​ e j if i ≠ j − e j if i = j . \displaystyle e^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\upmu_{j}(e_{i})\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}e_{i}+\max(0,B(e_{i},e_{j}))e_{j}&\mbox{if $i\neq j$~}\\ -e_{j}&\mbox{if $i=j$.}\end{array}\right. We can use the new seed to define another copy of the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} version of the quantum torus algebra, 𝒳 q μ j ​ ( S ) = 𝕜 t B ​ [ N μ j ​ ( S ) ] ≅ 𝒳 q S \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\upmu_{j}(S)}=\Bbbk_{t}^{B}[N_{\upmu_{j}(S)}]\cong\mathcal{X}_{q}^{S} — this last isomorphism is a by-product of the fact that the construction of the quantum torus algebra is independent of E S E_{S} . For the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} version, recall that ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) being a compatible pair implies that B 1 : N uf , ℝ → B 1 ​ ( N uf , ℝ ) ⊂ M ℝ B_{1}\colon N_{\uf,\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow B_{1}(N_{\uf,\mathbb{R}})\subset M_{\mathbb{R}} is an isomorphism onto its image, and by ( 4.1 ), m ↦ Λ ⁡ ( ⋅ , m ) m\mapsto\Uplambda(\cdot,m) is the inverse isomorphism. Since mutation preserves N uf N_{\uf} and B B , it follows that for each j ∈ I ∖ F j\in I\setminus F , ( μ j ​ ( S ) , Λ ) (\upmu_{j}(S),\Uplambda) is again a compatible pair. It therefore makes sense to define 𝒜 q μ j ​ ( S ) = 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\upmu_{j}(S)}=\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] , using the same Λ \Uplambda as in the definition of 𝒜 q S \mathcal{A}_{q}^{S} . In order to consider formal versions of the quantum cluster algebras, we define certain completions of 𝕜 t B ​ [ N ] \Bbbk_{t}^{B}[N] and 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] \Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] . Taking σ S , 𝒳 \upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}} to be the cone generated by { e S , i : i ∈ I ∖ F } \{e_{S,i}\colon i\in I\setminus F\} , we consider the completion 𝒳 ^ q S ≔ 𝕜 σ S , 𝒳 , t B ​ ⟦ N ⟧ . \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\coloneqq\Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}},t}^{B}\llbracket N\rrbracket. We similarly define the formal completion 𝒜 ^ q S ≔ 𝕜 σ S , 𝒜 , t Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ , \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S}\coloneqq\Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}},t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket, where σ S , 𝒜 \upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}} is the cone spanned by { B 1 ​ ( e S , i ) : i ∈ I ∖ F } \{B_{1}(e_{S,i})\colon i\in I\setminus F\} (the existence of Λ \Uplambda satisfying ( 4.1 ) ensures that the vectors in this set are linearly independent, thus determine a strongly convex cone). Similarly in the principal coefficients case, we define 𝒜 ^ q prin , S ≔ 𝒜 ^ q S prin \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S}\coloneqq\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S^{\prin}} . For j ∈ I ∖ F j\in I\setminus F , the quantum dilogarithm Ψ t ​ ( z e j ) \Psi_{t}(z^{e_{j}}) is an element of the completion 𝒳 ^ q S = 𝕜 σ S , 𝒳 , t B ​ ⟦ N ⟧ \widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q}=\Bbbk^{B}_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}},t}\llbracket N\rrbracket . One may easily verify, e.g., using ( 23 ), that for p ∈ N p\in N satisfying B ⁡ ( e j , p ) ≤ 0 B(e_{j},p)\leq 0 (with e j = e S , j e_{j}=e_{S,j} ), we have (54) μ S , j 𝒳 ​ ( z p ) ≔ Ad Ψ t ​ ( z e j ) − 1 ⁡ ( z p ) = ∑ n = 0 B ⁡ ( p , e j ) ( B ⁡ ( p , e j ) n ) t ​ z p + n ​ e j . \displaystyle\upmu_{S,j}^{\mathcal{X}}(z^{p})\coloneqq\Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{e_{j}})^{-1}}(z^{p})=\sum_{n=0}^{B(p,e_{j})}\binom{B(p,e_{j})}{n}_{t}z^{p+ne_{j}}. It follows that the automorphism Ad Ψ t ​ ( z e j ) − 1 : 𝒳 ^ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝒳 ^ q S \Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{e_{j}})^{-1}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q} induces a well-defined algebra isomorphism μ S , j 𝒳 : 𝒳 ̊ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝒳 ̊ q μ j ​ ( S ) , \upmu_{S,j}^{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}^{\upmu_{j}(S)}_{q}, with inverse defined similarly and induced by Ad Ψ t ​ ( z e j ) \Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{e_{j}})} . We define μ S , j 𝒜 : 𝒜 ̊ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝒜 ̊ q μ j ​ ( S ) \upmu_{S,j}^{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{\upmu_{j}(S)}_{q} similarly using the algebra automorphism Ad Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( e j ) ) − 1 \Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{B_{1}(e_{j})})^{-1}} of 𝒜 ^ q S = 𝕜 σ S , 𝒜 , t Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S}=\Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}},t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket . By ( 23 ) again, we deduce that (55) μ S , j 𝒜 ​ ( A S , i ) = { A S , i if ​ i ≠ j A S , i + z e i ∗ + B 1 ​ ( e i ) if ​ i = j . \upmu_{S,j}^{\mathcal{A}}(A_{S,i})=\begin{cases}A_{S,i}&\textrm{if }i\neq j\\ A_{S,i}+z^{e_{i}^{*}+B_{1}(e_{i})}&\textrm{if }i=j.\end{cases} One refers to μ S , j 𝒜 \upmu_{S,j}^{\mathcal{A}} and μ S , j 𝒳 \upmu_{S,j}^{\mathcal{X}} as the cluster 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -mutations and cluster 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -mutations, respectively. Now consider an s s -tuple ȷ → = ( j 1 , … , j s ) ⊂ ( I ∖ F ) s \vec{\jmath}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{s})\subset(I\setminus F)^{s} . For k = 1 , … , s , s + 1 k=1,\ldots,s,s+1 we let ȷ → k \vec{\jmath}_{k} denote the tuple ( j 1 , … , j k − 1 ) (j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1}) . The tuple ȷ → \vec{\jmath} determines a new seed (56) S ȷ → ≔ μ j s ∘ ⋯ ∘ μ j 1 ( S ) , \displaystyle S_{\vec{\jmath}}\coloneqq\upmu_{j_{s}}\circ\cdots\circ\upmu_{j_{1}}(S), as well as an isomorphism μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 ≔ μ S ȷ → s , j s 𝒜 ∘ ⋯ ∘ μ S ȷ → 1 , j 1 𝒜 : 𝒜 ̊ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝒜 ̊ μ ȷ → ​ ( S ) q , \upmu_{S,\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\upmu_{{S_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}},}j_{s}}^{\mathcal{A}}\circ\cdots\circ\upmu_{{S_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}},}j_{1}}^{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{\upmu_{\vec{\jmath}}(S)}_{q}, and similarly, an isomorphism μ S , ȷ → 𝒳 : 𝒳 ̊ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝒳 ̊ q μ ȷ → ​ ( S ) \upmu_{{S},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{X}}\colon\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathring{\mathcal{X}}^{\upmu_{\vec{\jmath}}(S)}_{q} . If ȷ → \vec{\jmath} is empty, these are the identity isomorphisms. In particular, S ȷ → 1 = S S_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}}=S , and S ȷ → s + 1 = S ȷ → S_{\vec{\jmath}_{s+1}}=S_{\vec{\jmath}} . Define 𝒜 q ȷ → ≔ ( μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 ) − 1 ​ ( 𝒜 q S ȷ → ) ⊂ 𝒜 ̊ q S , \displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q}\coloneqq(\upmu_{{S},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}})\subset\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S}, and similarly, 𝒳 q ȷ → ≔ ( μ S , ȷ → 𝒳 ) − 1 ​ ( 𝒳 q S ȷ → ) ⊂ 𝒳 ̊ q S . \displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q}\coloneqq(\upmu_{{S},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{X}})^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}})\subset\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}. For each i ∈ I i\in I , we obtain an element A ȷ → , i ≔ ( μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 ) − 1 ​ ( A S ȷ → , i ) ∈ 𝒜 q ȷ → A_{\vec{\jmath},i}\coloneqq(\upmu_{{S},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}(A_{S_{\vec{\jmath}},i})\in\mathcal{A}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q} . The elements ⋃ ȷ → { A ȷ → , i : i ∈ I } ∪ { A ȷ → , i − 1 : i ∈ F } ⊂ 𝒜 q ȷ → \bigcup_{\vec{\jmath}}\{A_{\vec{\jmath},i}\colon i\in I\}\cup\{A_{\vec{\jmath},i}^{-1}\colon i\in F\}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q} are called the quantum cluster variables . By a quantum cluster monomial , we mean an element of the form ( μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 ) − 1 ​ ( z m ) ∈ 𝒜 q ȷ → (\upmu_{{S},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}(z^{m})\in\mathcal{A}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q} for m ∈ C S ȷ → + m\in C_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+} . The ordinary quantum cluster algebra 𝒜 q ord \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord} is defined to be the sub 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra of 𝒜 ̊ q S \mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} generated by the quantum cluster variables. The upper quantum cluster algebra 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} is defined by 𝒜 q up ≔ ⋂ ȷ → 𝒜 q ȷ → ⊂ 𝒜 ̊ q S \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up}\coloneqq\bigcap_{\vec{\jmath}}\mathcal{A}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} where the intersection is over all finite tuples of elements of I ∖ F I\setminus F . The algebras 𝒜 q ȷ → \mathcal{A}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q} are called clusters of 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} (note that two different tuples ȷ → \vec{\jmath} may yield the same cluster). The “quantum Laurent phenomenon” [ BZ05 , Cor. 5.2] says that the quantum cluster variables can be expressed as quantum Laurent polynomials in every cluster, i.e., (57) 𝒜 q ord ⊂ 𝒜 q up . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up}. The upper quantum cluster 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -algebra 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q} is similarly defined by 𝒳 q up ≔ ⋂ ȷ → 𝒳 q ȷ → ⊂ 𝒳 ̊ q S . \displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q}\coloneqq\bigcap_{\vec{\jmath}}\mathcal{X}^{\vec{\jmath}}_{q}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}. We say an element f ∈ 𝒳 q up f\in\mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q} is a (quantum) global monomial if there is some ȷ → \vec{\jmath} such that μ S , ȷ → 𝒳 ​ ( f ) \upmu_{{S},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{X}}(f) is a monomial z n ∈ 𝒳 q S ȷ → z^{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} for some n ∈ N S ȷ → n\in N_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} . Then the ordinary quantum cluster 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -algebra 𝒳 q ord \mathcal{X}^{\ord}_{q} is defined to be the subalgebra of 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} generated by the quantum global monomials. We may define global monomials in 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} analogously to the definition in 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} . In light of ( 57 ), one sees that the quantum cluster monomials are global monomials. On the other hand, let m ∈ M ∖ C S 𝐣 + m\in M\setminus C^{+}_{S_{\mathbf{j}}} . Then in 𝒜 q μ ȷ → ​ ( S ) \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\upmu_{\vec{\jmath}}(S)} , we have z m = t a ​ z m ′ ​ A S ȷ → , j − k z^{m}=t^{a}z^{m^{\prime}}A_{S_{\vec{\jmath}},j}^{-k} for some j ∈ I ∖ F j\in I\setminus F , m ′ m^{\prime} in the span of { e S ȷ → , i ∗ } i ∈ I ∖ { j } \{e_{S_{\vec{\jmath}},i}^{*}\}_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}} , a ∈ 1 D ​ ℤ a\in\frac{1}{D}\mathbb{Z} , and k ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} . By ( 55 ) it follows that μ S ȷ → , j 𝒜 ​ ( z m ) \upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S_{\vec{\jmath}},j}(z^{m}) is a genuine rational function, and not a Laurent polynomial, and so z m z^{m} is not a global monomial. In conclusion, the quantum cluster monomials are precisely the global monomials in 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} . Given a seed S S and a compatible Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} for the corresponding seed with principal coefficients S prin S^{\prin} , we write 𝒜 q prin , up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\up} to denote the upper quantum cluster algebra with principal coefficients, i.e., the upper quantum cluster algebra associated to the compatible pair ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) . Similarly, 𝒜 q prin , ord \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\ord} denotes the ordinary quantum cluster algebra with principal coefficients. The following lemma relates 𝒜 q prin , up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\up} to 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} and 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q} . Lemma 4.1 . Let S S be a seed, and let ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) be a compatible pair. If Λ prin | B 1 prin ​ ( ( N , 0 ) ) \Uplambda^{\prin}|_{B^{\prin}_{1}((N,0))} is given as in ( 49 ), then the map ξ : N → M ⊕ N \upxi\colon N\rightarrow M\oplus N , n ↦ ( B 1 ​ ( n ) , n ) n\mapsto(B_{1}(n),n) induces injective homomorphisms ξ : 𝒳 q S ↪ 𝒜 q prin , S \upxi\colon{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} , extending to ξ : 𝒳 ̊ q S ↪ 𝒜 ̊ q prin , S \upxi\colon\mathring{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} and ξ : 𝒳 ^ q S ↪ 𝒜 ^ q prin , S \upxi\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} , which intertwine all sequences of mutations, thus also inducing an injection ξ : 𝒳 q up ↪ 𝒜 q prin , up \upxi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\up} . On the other hand, suppose that ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) is a compatible pair and that Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} is given as in ( 50 ). Then the map ρ : M ⊕ N → M \rho\colon M\oplus N\rightarrow M , ( m , n ) ↦ m (m,n)\mapsto m induces surjective homomorphisms ρ : 𝒜 q prin , S → 𝒜 q S \rho\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} and ρ : 𝒜 ^ q prin , S → 𝒜 ^ q S \rho\colon\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} intertwining sequences of mutations, thus also inducing ρ : 𝒜 q prin , up → 𝒜 q up \rho\colon\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\up}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} . Proof. First we must check that ξ \upxi and ρ \rho induce well-defined maps of the quantum torus algebras. For ξ \upxi , this means checking that (58) Λ prin ​ ( ξ ⁡ ( n 1 ) , ξ ⁡ ( n 2 ) ) = B ⁡ ( n 1 , n 2 ) . \displaystyle\Uplambda^{\prin}(\upxi(n_{1}),\upxi(n_{2}))=B(n_{1},n_{2}). By the definition of ξ \upxi , the left-hand side of ( 58 ) is Λ prin ​ ( ( B 1 ​ ( n 1 ) , n 1 ) , ( B 1 ​ ( n 2 ) , n 2 ) ) \Uplambda^{\prin}((B_{1}(n_{1}),n_{1}),(B_{1}(n_{2}),n_{2})) , which by ( 48 ) equals Λ prin ​ ( B 1 prin ​ ( n 1 , 0 ) , B 1 prin ​ ( n 2 , 0 ) ) \Uplambda^{\prin}(B^{\prin}_{1}(n_{1},0),B^{\prin}_{1}(n_{2},0)) . The equality ( 58 ) is now equivalent to the condition that Λ prin | B 1 , prin ​ ( ( , , , ) ) \Uplambda^{\prin}|_{B_{1,\prin}((N,0))} is given as in ( 49 ). It is clear that ξ \upxi is injective and thus extends to the skew-fields of fractions. Since ξ ⁡ ( e i ) = B 1 prin ​ ( e i ) \upxi(e_{i})=B_{1}^{\prin}(e_{i}) , we see that ξ ⁡ ( σ S , 𝒳 ) = σ S prin , 𝒜 \upxi(\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}})=\upsigma_{S^{\prin},\mathcal{A}} , and so ξ \upxi extends to the completions as well. Similarly, the condition for ρ \rho to induce a well-defined map of quantum torus algebras is just that Λ prin ​ ( ( m 1 , n 1 ) , ( m 2 , n 2 ) ) = Λ ⁡ ( ρ ⁡ ( m 1 , n 1 ) , ρ ⁡ ( m 2 , n 2 ) ) \Uplambda^{\prin}((m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2}))=\Uplambda(\rho(m_{1},n_{1}),\rho(m_{2},n_{2})) , and this is the condition we imposed. Since ρ ⁡ ( B 1 prin ​ ( e i ) ) = B 1 ​ ( e i ) \rho(B_{1}^{\prin}(e_{i}))=B_{1}(e_{i}) , we have ρ ⁡ ( σ S prin , 𝒜 ) = σ S , 𝒜 \rho(\upsigma_{S^{\prin},\mathcal{A}})=\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}} , and so ρ \rho extends to 𝒜 ^ q prin , S → 𝒜 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} . It now suffices to check that ξ \upxi and ρ \rho commute with mutations. Since the mutations are automorphisms of the corresponding formal quantum cluster algebras, it suffices to check that ξ \upxi and ρ \rho commute with the mutations associated to the initial seed (because the same formulas define ξ \upxi and ρ \rho in the coordinate system associated to any other seed). For ξ \upxi , using ( 48 ) we have that ξ ⁡ ( e i ) = ( B 1 ​ ( e i ) , e i ) = π 1 prin ​ ( e i , 0 ) \upxi(e_{i})=(B_{1}(e_{i}),e_{i})=\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt^{\prin}_{1}(e_{i},0) , so ξ \upxi maps Ψ t ​ ( z e i ) \Psi_{t}(z^{e_{i}}) to Ψ t ​ ( z π 1 prin ​ ( e i ) ) \Psi_{t}(z^{\uppi\hskip 0.40974pt^{\prin}_{1}(e_{i})}) , and the commutativity of ξ \upxi with the mutations follows. Similarly, for ρ \rho , we have ρ ⁡ ( π 1 prin ​ ( e i ) ) = ρ ⁡ ( B 1 ​ ( e i ) , e i ) = B 1 ​ ( e i ) \rho(\uppi\hskip 0.50003pt^{\prin}_{1}(e_{i}))=\rho(B_{1}(e_{i}),e_{i})=B_{1}(e_{i}) , so ρ \rho maps Ψ t ​ ( z π 1 prin ​ ( e i ) ) \Psi_{t}(z^{\uppi\hskip 0.40974pt^{\prin}_{1}(e_{i})}) to Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) \Psi_{t}(z^{B_{1}(e_{i})}) , as desired. ∎ Lemma 4.2 . Let ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) be a compatible pair, and suppose Λ ⁡ ( ⋅ , B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) = e i \Uplambda(\cdot,B_{1}(e_{i}))=e_{i} for all i ∈ I i\in I , not just i ∈ I ∖ F i\in I\setminus F . Then B 1 : N → M B_{1}\colon N\rightarrow M induces homomorphisms B 1 : 𝒳 q up → 𝒜 q up B_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} and B 1 : 𝒳 ^ q S → 𝒜 ^ q S B_{1}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} . Proof. The condition is equivalent to requiring that Λ ⁡ ( B 1 ​ ( u ) , B 1 ​ ( v ) ) = B ⁡ ( u , v ) \Uplambda(B_{1}(u),B_{1}(v))=B(u,v) for all u , v ∈ N u,v\in N , and it follows that B 1 B_{1} induces a well-defined map of quantum torus algebras. This extends to the formal quantum tori since B 1 ​ ( σ S , 𝒳 ) = σ S , 𝒜 B_{1}(\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}})=\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}} . Compatibility with mutations now follows from the definitions of μ j 𝒳 \upmu_{j}^{\mathcal{X}} and μ j 𝒜 \upmu_{j}^{\mathcal{A}} in terms of conjugation by Ψ t ​ ( z e j ) \Psi_{t}(z^{e_{j}}) and Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( e j ) ) \Psi_{t}(z^{B_{1}(e_{j})}) , respectively, and the observation that B 1 ​ ( Ψ t ​ ( z e j ) ) = Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( e j ) ) B_{1}(\Psi_{t}(z^{e_{j}}))=\Psi_{t}(z^{B_{1}(e_{j})}) . The claim for the upper quantum cluster algebras follows. ∎ 4.3. The scattering diagrams associated to a seed Given a compatible pair ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) , we define a scattering diagram 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} as follows. Take 𝔤 ≔ 𝔤 M 0 + , Λ \mathfrak{g}\coloneqq\mathfrak{g}_{M_{0}^{+},\Uplambda} for M 0 ≔ B 1 ​ ( N uf ) ⊂ M M_{0}\coloneqq B_{1}(N_{\uf})\subset M and M 0 + ≔ ( M 0 ∩ σ S , 𝒜 ) ∖ { 0 } M_{0}^{+}\coloneqq(M_{0}\cap\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}})\setminus\{0\} . Note that we indeed have B 1 ​ ( e i ) Λ ⟂ = e i ⟂ ⊂ M ℝ B_{1}(e_{i})^{\Uplambda\perp}=e_{i}^{\perp}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}} by ( 4.1 ). We then take 𝔇 𝒜 q ≔ Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in 𝒜 q ) \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}}\coloneqq\scat(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}}_{\In}) as in Theorem 2.13 . We define 𝔇 in 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} and 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} in the same way as 𝔇 in 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} and 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} above, but using a compatible pair ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) in place of ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) . Similarly, given a seed S S and taking N + ≔ ( N ∩ σ S , 𝒳 ) ∖ { 0 } N^{+}\coloneqq(N\cap\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}})\setminus\{0\} , we construct the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -scattering diagram in N ℝ N_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 N + , B \mathfrak{g}_{N^{+},B} as follows: 𝔇 in 𝒳 q ≔ { ( B 2 ( e i ) ⟂ , Ψ t ( z e i ) ) : i ∈ I ∖ F , B 2 ( e i ) ≠ 0 } and 𝔇 𝒳 q ≔ Scat ( 𝔇 in 𝒳 q ) . \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}}_{\In}\coloneqq\{(B_{2}(e_{i})^{\perp},\Psi_{t}(z^{e_{i}}))\colon i\in I\setminus F,~B_{2}(e_{i})\neq 0\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}}\coloneqq\scat(\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}}_{\In}). Note that we indeed have B 2 ​ ( e i ) ⟂ = e i B ⟂ B_{2}(e_{i})^{\perp}=e_{i}^{B\perp} . Also, the condition that B 2 ​ ( e i ) ≠ 0 B_{2}(e_{i})\neq 0 ensures that e i ∉ ker ⁡ ( B ) e_{i}\notin\ker(B) , as required for our definition of a wall. We can always treat e i ∈ ker ⁡ ( B ) e_{i}\in\ker(B) as a frozen vector without changing the construction of the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -spaces or 𝔇 in 𝒳 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}}_{\In} , so for simplicity, one may assume that no unfrozen vectors in ker ⁡ ( B ) \ker(B) exist. We make the following observations: Lemma 4.3 . All hypotheses (hence conclusions) from Theorem 2.15 apply to the scattering diagrams 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} , 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} , and 𝔇 𝒳 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}} . Similarly, all results of § 3.3 (except possibly Theorem 3.14 ) apply to the theta functions constructed from these scattering diagrams. For the rest of this section, we assume that all consistent scattering diagrams are in one of the forms described in the conclusions of Theorem 2.15 . Remark 4.4 (Non skew-symmetric quantum cluster algebras) . Throughout this article, we assume that all seeds are skew-symmetric, i.e., that the bilinear form B B is skew-symmetric. More generally though, B B might be merely “skew-symmetrizable,” i.e., of the form B ⁡ ( e i , e j ) = d j ​ β ​ ( e i , e j ) B(e_{i},e_{j})=d_{j}\upbeta(e_{i},e_{j}) for some skew-symmetric ℚ \mathbb{Q} -valued form β \upbeta and some numbers d j ∈ ℚ > 0 d_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0} such that B ⁡ ( e i , e j ) ∈ ℤ B(e_{i},e_{j})\in\mathbb{Z} whenever i i and j j are not both in F F . Then following [ BZ05 ] for the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -side and [ FG09 ] for the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -side, the constructions of the quantum cluster algebras change in the following ways. The maps B 1 B_{1} and B 2 B_{2} are defined in the same way using B B , but the quantum torus algebra 𝒳 q S \mathcal{X}_{q}^{S} is defined using β \upbeta , i.e., 𝒳 q S ≔ 𝕜 t β ​ [ N ] \mathcal{X}_{q}^{S}\coloneqq\Bbbk_{t}^{\upbeta}[N] . The compatibility condition ( 4.1 ) is changed to Λ ⁡ ( ⋅ , B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) = 1 d i ​ e i \Uplambda(\cdot,B_{1}(e_{i}))=\frac{1}{d_{i}}e_{i} . The mutations μ j 𝒜 \upmu_{j}^{\mathcal{A}} and μ j 𝒳 \upmu_{j}^{\mathcal{X}} are defined using Ad Ψ t 1 / d i ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) − 1 \Ad_{\Psi_{t^{1/d_{i}}}(z^{B_{1}(e_{i})})^{-1}} and Ad Ψ t 1 / d i ​ ( z e i ) − 1 \Ad_{\Psi_{t^{1/d_{i}}}(z^{e_{i}})^{-1}} , respectively. Then as suggested in [ Man , §4.2] , the appropriate initial scattering diagrams seem to be 𝔇 in 𝒜 q ≔ { ( e i ⟂ , Ψ t 1 / d i ​ ( z e i ) ) : i ∈ I ∖ F } \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}}_{\In}\coloneqq\{(e_{i}^{\perp},\Psi_{t^{1/d_{i}}}(z^{e_{i}}))\colon i\in I\setminus F\} and 𝔇 in 𝒳 q ≔ { ( B 2 ( e i ) ⟂ , Ψ t 1 / d i ( z B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) ) : i ∈ I ∖ F , B 2 ( e i ) ≠ 0 } \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}}_{\In}\coloneqq\{(B_{2}(e_{i})^{\perp},\Psi_{t^{1/d_{i}}}(z^{B_{1}(e_{i})}))\colon i\in I\setminus F,B_{2}(e_{i})\neq 0\} , because crossing these walls recovers the quantum mutations associated to the initial seed as defined in [ BZ05 ] and [ FG09 ] , respectively (defining these scattering diagrams in a way compatible with lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} requires some careful modifications to the definition of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} ). However, it is suspected that positivity for quantum theta functions will fail in this general setup — in rank 2 2 , the quantum theta functions are expected to agree with the quantum greedy bases, and positivity for some greedy basis elements (outside the cluster complex) is known to fail, cf. [ LLRZ14 , end of §3] . 4.4. Relating theta functions for different spaces In this subsection we explain how the theta functions for the algebras 𝒜 ^ q \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q} , 𝒜 ^ q prin \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin} , and 𝒳 ^ q \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q} are related to each other. We then use this to relate the quantum theta functions to the classical theta functions as defined in [ GHKK18 ] . We will use the maps ξ : N → M ⊕ N \upxi\colon N\rightarrow M\oplus N and ρ : M ⊕ N → M \rho\colon M\oplus N\rightarrow M from Lemma 4.1 , and by abuse of notation we extend these to maps between the respective lattices tensored with ℝ \mathbb{R} . Suppose that ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) is a compatible pair and that Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} is given by ρ ∗ ​ Λ \rho^{*}\Uplambda as in ( 50 ), so we can consider ρ \rho as in Lemma 4.1 . Then ker ⁡ ( ρ ) = ( 0 , N ℝ ) \ker(\rho)=(0,N_{\mathbb{R}}) is parallel to the supports of the walls in 𝔇 in 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} , hence also parallel to the supports of the walls in 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} . Furthermore, as observed in the proof of Lemma 4.1 , ρ \rho maps Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 prin ​ ( e i ) ) \Psi_{t}(z^{B^{\prin}_{1}(e_{i})}) to Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) \Psi_{t}(z^{B_{1}(e_{i})}) . Thus, using the uniqueness of the consistent scattering diagram in Theorem 2.13 , we have (up to equivalence) that (59) 𝔇 𝒜 q = { ( ρ ⁡ ( 𝔡 ) , ρ ⁡ ( f ) ) : ( 𝔡 , f ) ∈ 𝔇 𝒜 q prin } , \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}}=\{(\rho(\mathfrak{d}),\rho(f))\colon(\mathfrak{d},f)\in\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}}\}, and furthermore, for each ( 𝔡 , f ) ∈ 𝔇 𝒜 q prin (\mathfrak{d},f)\in\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} , 𝔡 = ρ − 1 ​ ( ρ ​ ( 𝔡 ) ) \mathfrak{d}=\rho^{-1}(\rho(\mathfrak{d})) . We thus obtain the following (cf. Lemma 2.11 ): Lemma 4.5 . Suppose that ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) and ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) are compatible pairs such that Λ ⁡ ( m 1 , m 2 ) = Λ prin ​ ( ( m 1 , n 1 ) , ( m 2 , n 2 ) ) \Uplambda(m_{1},m_{2})=\Uplambda^{\prin}((m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2})) for all ( m 1 , n 1 ) , ( m 2 , n 2 ) ∈ M prin (m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2})\in M^{\prin} , so ρ \rho is well-defined as in Lemma 4.1 . Let S S be a seed, and let ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) be a compatible pair such that Λ prin | B 1 prin ​ ( ( N , 0 ) ) \Uplambda^{\prin}|_{B^{\prin}_{1}((N,0))} is given as in ( 49 ), so the map ξ \upxi of Lemma 4.1 is well-defined. For p ∈ N p\in N and generic 𝒬 ∈ N ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in N_{\mathbb{R}} , the theta function ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} defined with respect to 𝔇 𝒳 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}} is related to the theta function ϑ ξ ⁡ ( p ) , ξ ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) \vartheta_{\upxi(p),\upxi(\mathcal{Q})} defined with respect to 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} via ξ ⁡ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ) = ϑ ξ ⁡ ( p ) , ξ ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) . \displaystyle\upxi(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}})=\vartheta_{\upxi(p),\upxi(\mathcal{Q})}. Proof. By ( 61 ) these are exactly the broken lines with ends of M M -degree zero. ∎ We similarly find the following: Lemma 4.7 . Suppose Λ ⁡ ( ⋅ , B 1 ​ ( e i ) ) = e i \Uplambda(\cdot,B_{1}(e_{i}))=e_{i} for all i ∈ I i\in I so that Lemma 4.2 applies. Proposition 4.8 . The classical limits of the quantum theta functions in 𝒜 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} , 𝒜 ^ q prin , S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\prin,S}_{q} and 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} are the corresponding classical theta functions of [ GHKK18 ] . Proof. It is straightforward to check that applying lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} to 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} yields the corresponding classical scattering diagram 𝔇 in , s \mathfrak{D}_{\In,\textbf{s}} of [ GHKK18 ] . The claim thus follows for 𝒜 ^ q prin , S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} , since for these cases the [ GHKK18 ] theta functions are constructed directly from the broken lines corresponding to lim t → 1 𝔇 q 𝒜 t prin = 𝔇 in , s prin \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\mathfrak{D}_{q}^{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\prin}}=\mathfrak{D}_{\In,\textbf{s}^{\prin}} . For 𝒜 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} , [ GHKK18 , §7.2] constructs the theta functions by applying the classical analog of our map ρ \rho from Lemma 4.1 . 7 7 7 The reason that [ GHKK18 ] does not just directly construct the theta functions for the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -space using 𝔇 in , s \mathfrak{D}_{\In,\textbf{s}} is that they allow 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -spaces for which their “injectivity assumption” is not satisfied, i.e., for which there is no compatible pair Λ \Uplambda , hence no quantization. In such cases, there might not exist a strictly convex cone σ S , 𝒜 \upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}} containing the negative directions of the initial walls. Lemma 4.5 states that ρ \rho takes quantum theta functions to quantum theta functions, so the classical limits of the quantum theta functions in 𝒜 ^ q \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q} are indeed the classical theta functions of [ GHKK18 ] . In the classical limit, applying ξ − 1 \upxi^{-1} to the theta functions of M M -degree 0 0 is precisely the construction of the theta functions on the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -space in [ GHKK18 , Constr. 7.11] . So by Lemma 4.6 , the classical limits of our theta functions in 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S}_{q} are the classical theta functions of [ GHKK18 ] . ∎ 4.5. The classical version of Proposition 4.9 (1)–(3) follows from applying [ GHKK18 , Thm. 1.24] inductively, cf. [ GHKK18 , Construction 1.30] . The quantum analog of [ GHKK18 , Thm. 1.24] is Proposition A.1 , and Proposition 4.9 (1)–(3) similarly follows from applying this inductively. The classical version of Proposition 4.9 (4) is equivalent to [ GHKK18 , Thm. 4.4] , specifically, cf. the commutative diagram (4.6) of loc. cit in the case v = v ′ v=v^{\prime} there. The arguments of loc. cit. apply to yield the quantum version in the same way, i.e., as a consequence of Proposition A.1 . While deducing (1)–(3) from Proposition A.1 is fairly simple, the proof of (4) is more involved. 10 10 10 The fact that two seeds correspond to the same cluster only if they correspond to the same chamber of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} is actually not immediate from Proposition A.1 . This instead follows from (4). We therefore present a different argument which uses positivity to deduce Proposition 4.9 directly from its classical analog. The existence of the fan 𝒞 \mathcal{C} satisfying Properties (1) and (2) follows from the classical case [ GHKK18 , Construction 1.30] because positivity implies that the set of walls is unchanged when we take the t ↦ 1 t\mapsto 1 limit. For (4), let ȷ → = ( j 1 , … , j s ) \vec{\jmath}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{s}) , and suppose inductively that ψ ȷ → s ∘ ι ȷ → s = μ ȷ → s 𝒜 \psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}\circ\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}=\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}} . The sign ϵ = ± 1 \epsilon=\pm 1 is, on one hand, determined by the requirement that ϵ ​ v ∈ σ S , 𝒜 \epsilon v\in\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}} . On the other hand, by the definition of the wall-crossing automorphism ( 14 ), the exponent for Ad \Ad should be sgn ⁡ ( Λ ⁡ ( ψ ȷ → s − 1 ​ ( − ϵ ​ v ) , p ) ) \sign(\Uplambda(\psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}^{-1}(-\epsilon v),p)) for p p in the interior of 𝒞 ȷ → s \mathcal{C}_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}} . Now, we have ψ ȷ → ∘ ι ȷ → \displaystyle\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}} = ψ ȷ → ∘ Ad Ψ t ​ ( z ψ ȷ → − 1 ​ ( − ϵ ​ v ) ) ϵ ∘ ι ȷ → s \displaystyle=\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\Ad^{\epsilon}_{\Psi_{t}(z^{\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{-1}(-\epsilon v)})}\circ\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}} = ψ ȷ → ∘ Ad Ψ t ​ ( z ψ ȷ → − 1 ​ ( − ϵ ​ v ) ) ϵ ∘ ψ ȷ → s − 1 ∘ μ S , ȷ → s 𝒜 \displaystyle=\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\Ad^{\epsilon}_{\Psi_{t}(z^{\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{-1}(-\epsilon v)})}\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}^{-1}\circ\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}_{s}} = Ad Ψ t ​ ( z − ϵ ​ v ) ϵ ∘ ψ ȷ → ∘ ψ ȷ → s − 1 ∘ μ S , ȷ → s 𝒜 , \displaystyle=\Ad^{\epsilon}_{\Psi_{t}(z^{-\epsilon v})}\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}^{-1}\circ\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}_{s}}, where the second equality uses the inductive assumption and the last equality uses Lemma 4.10 . Since μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 = μ S ȷ → s , j s 𝒜 ∘ μ S , ȷ → s 𝒜 \upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}}=\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}},j_{s}}\circ\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}_{s}} , applying ( μ ȷ → s 𝒜 ) − 1 (\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}})^{-1} on the right and applying Ad Ψ t ​ ( z − ϵ ​ v ) − ϵ \Ad^{-\epsilon}_{\Psi_{t}(z^{-\epsilon v})} on the left reduces the desired result to ψ ȷ → ∘ ψ ȷ → s − 1 = Ad Ψ t ​ ( z − ϵ ​ v ) − ϵ ∘ Ad Ψ t ​ ( z v ) − 1 . \displaystyle\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}^{-1}=\Ad^{-\epsilon}_{\Psi_{t}(z^{-\epsilon v})}\circ\Ad^{-1}_{\Psi_{t}(z^{v})}. For ϵ = − 1 \epsilon=-1 , the right-hand side is the identity, and for ϵ = 1 \epsilon=1 , Lemma 4.11 tells us that the right-hand side is given by z p ↦ z p − Λ ⁡ ( v , p ) ​ v z^{p}\mapsto z^{p-\Uplambda(v,p)v} . So it suffices to check that ψ ȷ → ∘ ψ ȷ → s − 1 \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}^{-1} is determined by the appropriate linear map. Since the above arguments all apply in the classical limit in the same way, and we know the result holds in the classical limit, it follows that ψ ȷ → ∘ ψ ȷ → s − 1 \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}}^{-1} is indeed determined by this linear map, and the result follows. ∎ Remark 4.12 . [ Mou ] proves the analogous chamber decomposition for Hall algebra scattering diagrams (as in [ Bri17 ] ) associated to quivers with non-degenerate potential. It is not always the case that the associated quantum stability scattering diagram is equivalent to 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} (unless the potential is genteel as in Def. 7.3 ), but [ Mou , Thm. 1.1] together with Proposition 4.9 above imply that, if the potential is non-degenerate, then the two will at least agree on the support of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} . 4.6. Theta functions as elements of the quantum cluster algebras We now use Proposition 4.9 to deduce our results on the ordinary and middle quantum cluster algebras. Recall the linear maps ψ ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}} as in ( 68 ), as well as the induced automorphisms of the quantum torus algebras, also denoted ψ ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}} . Corollary 4.13 . Consider p ∈ M p\in M and generic 𝒬 , 𝒬 ȷ → \mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}} in 𝒞 S \mathcal{C}_{S} and 𝒞 S ȷ → \mathcal{C}_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} , respectively. Suppose ϑ p , 𝒬 ∈ 𝒜 q S \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}\in\mathcal{A}_{q}^{S} , i.e., ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is a Laurent polynomial, not just a formal Laurent series. Then ϑ p , 𝒬 ȷ → \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}} is also a Laurent polynomial. Furthermore, ψ ȷ → ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ȷ → ) = μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ) ∈ 𝒜 q S ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}})=\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}}(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}})\in\mathcal{A}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} , so ϑ p \vartheta_{p} determines an element of 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} . If p ∈ 𝒞 S ȷ → + ∩ M p\in\mathcal{C}_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+}\cap M and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is a generic point in the interior of 𝒞 S ȷ → + \mathcal{C}_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+} , then ϑ p , 𝒬 = z p \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}=z^{p} , and so ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is a quantum cluster monomial. On the other hand, all quantum cluster monomials are quantum theta functions. Proof. The statement that each ϑ p , 𝒬 ȷ → \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}} is a Laurent polynomial follows from its classical analog [ GHKK18 , Prop. 7.1] and the positivity of broken lines — since the classical limit has contributions from only finitely many broken lines, and applying lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} does not cause any broken lines to vanish, the quantum theta function must consist of contributions from only finitely many broken lines as well. The claim that ψ ȷ → ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ȷ → ) = μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ) ∈ 𝒜 q S ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}})=\upmu^{\mathcal{A}}_{S,\vec{\jmath}}(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}})\in\mathcal{A}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} then follows from Theorem 3.2 and Proposition 4.9 (4). For the statements on cluster monomials, [ GHKK18 , Prop. 3.6 and 3.8] together show that in the classical limit, the only broken line contributing to ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is the straight line with attached monomial z p z^{p} . By positivity, this is also the only broken line for the quantum analog (any others would have non-trivial classical limit), and since it has no kinks, the attached monomial must again be the initial monomial z p z^{p} . ∎ Let Θ 𝒜 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{A}}^{\midd} denote the set of p ∈ M p\in M such that ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} is a Laurent polynomial for some generic 𝒬 ∈ 𝒞 \mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{C} (hence for all generic 𝒬 ∈ 𝒞 \mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{C} by Corollary 4.13 ). I.e., Θ 𝒜 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{A}}^{\midd} is the set of p p such that ϑ p ∈ 𝒜 q up \vartheta_{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} rather than just 𝒜 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} . In particular, 𝒞 ∩ M ⊂ Θ 𝒜 mid , \mathcal{C}\cap M\subset\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}^{\midd}, because for p ∈ 𝒞 ∩ M p\in\mathcal{C}\cap M and 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} a generic point of M ℝ M_{\mathbb{R}} sharing a maximal cone of 𝒞 \mathcal{C} with p p , Corollary 4.13 tells us that ϑ p , 𝒬 = z p \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}=z^{p} , which in particular is a Laurent polynomial (this yields a new proof of the quantum Laurent phenomenon ( 57 )). In fact, using positivity again, we find that Θ 𝒜 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{A}}^{\midd} is the same in the quantum setup as in the classical setup, and furthermore, we find that all of [ GHKK18 , Theorem 7.5] carries over to the quantum setup (cf. Lemma 3.15 ). In particular, we have the following: Proposition 4.14 . Let 𝒜 q mid \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\midd} denote the 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -submodule of 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} spanned by { ϑ p } p ∈ Θ 𝒜 mid \{\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Theta_{\mathcal{A}}^{\midd}} . Then 𝒜 q mid \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\midd} is naturally a subalgebra of 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} , and we have inclusions of algebras 𝒜 q ord ⊂ 𝒜 q mid ⊂ 𝒜 q up ⊂ 𝒜 ^ q . \displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\midd}\subset\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}. Furthermore, the classical t ↦ 1 t\mapsto 1 limit of 𝒜 q mid \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\midd} is the classical middle cluster algebra, called mid ⁡ ( 𝒜 ) \midd(\mathcal{A}) in [ GHKK18 ] . For the quantum 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -algebras, recall from the proof of Proposition 4.8 that the theta functions in 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} can be obtained by applying ξ − 1 \upxi^{-1} to the M M -degree 0 0 theta functions of 𝒜 ^ q prin , S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} , for M M -degree as in ( 63 ). Let Θ 𝒳 mid ≔ Θ 𝒜 prin mid ∩ N ′ , \Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\Theta_{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}}^{\midd}\cap N^{\prime}, viewed as a subset of N N using ξ − 1 \upxi^{-1} . Here, Θ 𝒜 prin mid \Theta_{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}}^{\midd} is defined in the same way as Θ 𝒜 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{A}}^{\midd} , but for the seed S prin S^{\prin} in place of S S . By [ GHKK18 , Lem. 7.10(3)] , the t ↦ 1 t\mapsto 1 limit of any ϑ p \vartheta_{p} with p ∈ Θ 𝒳 mid p\in\Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{X}} is an element of 𝒳 up \mathcal{X}^{\up} (as opposed to just 𝒳 ^ S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}^{S} ). This together with positivity implies that ϑ p \vartheta_{p} must have been in 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} (as opposed to 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} ). In summary, we have the following: Proposition 4.15 . Let 𝒳 q mid \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\midd} denote the 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -submodule of 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} spanned by { ϑ p } p ∈ Θ 𝒳 mid \{\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{X}}} . Then 𝒳 q mid \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\midd} is naturally a subalgebra of 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q} , and we have the inclusions of algebras 𝒳 q ord ⊂ 𝒳 q mid ⊂ 𝒳 q up ⊂ 𝒳 ^ q . \displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{X}^{\midd}_{q}\subset\mathcal{X}^{\up}_{q}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}. Furthermore, the classical t ↦ 1 t\mapsto 1 limit of 𝒳 q mid \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\midd} is the algebra mid ⁡ ( 𝒳 ) \midd(\mathcal{X}) of [ GHKK18 ] . Here, the fact that 𝒳 q mid \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\midd} is closed under multiplication follows from the observation that it is the intersection of 𝒜 q prin , mid \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\midd} and ξ ⁡ ( 𝒳 ^ q ) \upxi(\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}) , and each of these is closed under multiplication. The fact that 𝒳 q ord ⊂ 𝒳 q mid \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{X}_{q}^{\midd} follows from noting that global monomials in 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} are just global monomials in 𝒜 q prin , up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\up} having degree 0 0 with respect to the M M -grading (precisely as in the classical analog, [ GHKK18 , Lem. 7.10(3)] ), and these are theta functions in 𝒜 q prin , mid \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\midd} . 4.7. Atlases We will now describe various atlases — i.e., sets of morphisms to (formal) quantum torus algebras — on our quantum cluster algebras. 4.7.1. Atlases for 𝒜 \mathcal{A} Let 𝒬 , 𝒬 0 ∈ M ℝ \mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}_{0}\in M_{\mathbb{R}} be generic with 𝒬 0 \mathcal{Q}_{0} in the interior of C S + C_{S}^{+} . Let γ \upgamma be a smooth path from 𝒬 0 \mathcal{Q}_{0} to 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} which is transverse to the walls of 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} . Then the isomorphism ι 𝒬 : 𝒜 ^ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝕜 σ S , 𝒜 , t Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}},t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket of ( 1 ) is given by the path-ordered product ι 𝒬 ≔ θ γ , 𝔇 𝒜 q . \displaystyle\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}}}. These charts ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} for generic 𝒬 ∈ M ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in M_{\mathbb{R}} form the scattering atlas for 𝒜 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{S}_{q} . The subset of charts ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} for generic 𝒬 ∈ 𝒞 \mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{C} is what we call the cluster atlas. For 𝒬 ∈ C S ȷ → + ⊂ 𝒞 \mathcal{Q}\in C_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+}\subset\mathcal{C} , denote the corresponding chart ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} by ι ȷ → \upiota_{\vec{\jmath}} . If 𝒬 ∈ C S ȷ → + \mathcal{Q}\in C_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+} , then Proposition 4.9 (4) implies that ψ ȷ → ∘ ι 𝒬 = μ S , ȷ → 𝒜 \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\circ\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}=\upmu_{S,\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}} on 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} . Since ψ ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}} is a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra automorphism of 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] \Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] , we may view the restriction of the cluster atlas to 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} as agreeing with the usual set of quantum clusters. 4.7.2. Atlases for 𝒳 \mathcal{X} To construct ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} for the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -spaces in terms of path-ordered products, we use the injections ξ : 𝒳 ^ q S ↪ 𝒜 ^ q prin , S \upxi\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} and ξ : N ℝ ↪ M ℝ prin \upxi\colon N_{\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prin} . Let 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} be a generic point in N ℝ N_{\mathbb{R}} , and let 𝒬 0 \mathcal{Q}_{0} be a generic point in the interior of C S prin + ⊂ M ℝ prin C_{S^{\prin}}^{+}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prin} . Let γ \upgamma be a smooth path from 𝒬 0 \mathcal{Q}_{0} to ξ ⁡ ( 𝒬 ) \upxi(\mathcal{Q}) in M ℝ prin M_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prin} , transverse to the walls of 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} . Then ι 𝒬 : 𝒳 ^ q S ⟶ ∼ 𝕜 σ S , 𝒳 , t B ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{X}},t}^{B}\llbracket M\rrbracket is given by ι 𝒬 ≔ ξ − 1 | ξ ⁡ ( 𝒳 ^ q S ) ∘ θ γ , 𝔇 𝒜 q prin ∘ ξ . \displaystyle\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}\coloneqq\upxi^{-1}|_{\upxi(\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S})}\circ\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}}}\circ\upxi. Here we use from the proof of Proposition 4.8 that scattering with respect to 𝔇 𝒜 q prin \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin}} preserves the M M -degree and that ξ ⁡ ( 𝒳 ^ q S ) \upxi(\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S}) can be identified with the subalgebra of degree 0 0 elements of 𝒜 ^ q prin , S \widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{\prin,S} . This gives the scattering atlas for 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} . In fact, we could more generally allow γ \upgamma to end at any generic 𝒬 ∈ M ℝ prin \mathcal{Q}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prin} , not just restricting to 𝒬 ∈ ξ ⁡ ( N ℝ ) \mathcal{Q}\in\upxi(N_{\mathbb{R}}) , giving what we call the principal coefficients scattering atlas. Theorems 3.9 and 3.16 still hold for 𝒳 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} when we allow these more general choices of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} , by the same arguments. Suppose 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} is in the cluster complex for 𝒜 q prin \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin} , say in the chamber corresponding to a sequence of mutations ȷ → \vec{\jmath} , and denote the corresponding ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} by ι ȷ → prin \upiota_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\prin} . Let ψ ȷ → prin \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\prin} denote the linear automorphism of M prin M^{\prin} defined as in ( 68 ) but using S prin S^{\prin} . Then ψ ȷ → prin ∘ ι ȷ → prin \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\prin}\circ\upiota^{\prin}_{\vec{\jmath}} agrees with μ S prin , ȷ → 𝒜 prin \upmu_{S^{\prin},\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}} on 𝒜 q prin , up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\prin,\up} , and by further restricting, they agree on 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} . Thus, as in the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -case, we can view the cluster atlas on 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} as agreeing with the usual set of quantum clusters, as considered in [ FG09 ] . 4.8. Enough global monomials Consider the subalgebras 𝒜 q can ⊂ 𝒜 ^ q S \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{S} and 𝒳 q can ⊂ 𝒳 ^ q S \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\can}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{X}}_{q}^{S} generated by the theta functions. By Proposition 3.1 , the theta functions form topological bases for these algebras. The theta functions will form an ordinary basis precisely when the multiplication rule is polynomial, i.e., if for each p 1 , p 2 p_{1},p_{2} , there are only finitely many p p such that the structure constant α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p) is nonzero. By strong positivity, nonzero structure constants never vanish when applying lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} , so this polynomiality property is equivalent to the analogous property in the classical limit (alternatively, this equivalence follows from Lemma 3.15 , with Θ \Theta and Θ ′ \Theta^{\prime} both equal to the full lattice). Thus, Proposition 4.16 . The theta functions form a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module basis for 𝒜 q can \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can} (resp. 𝒳 q can \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\can} ) if and only if their classical limits form a 𝕜 \Bbbk -module basis for the corresponding classical algebra 𝒜 can \mathcal{A}^{\can} (resp. 𝒳 can \mathcal{X}^{\can} ). Conditions implying polynomiality in the classical setup are explored in [ GHKK18 ] . In particular, [ GHKK18 , Thm. 0.12(1)] states that the multiplication of theta functions in 𝒜 can \mathcal{A}^{\can} (resp. 𝒳 can \mathcal{X}^{\can} ) is polynomial whenever 𝒜 can \mathcal{A}^{\can} (resp. 𝒳 can \mathcal{X}^{\can} ) has “Enough Global Monomials,” as we will now define. Consider a Laurent polynomial g = ∑ v ∈ L c v ​ z v g=\sum_{v\in L}c_{v}z^{v} in a quantum torus algebra 𝕜 t ω ​ [ L ] \Bbbk_{t}^{\omega}[L] or in the classical limit 𝕜 ⁡ [ L ] \Bbbk[L] . Let u ∈ L ∗ u\in L^{*} . Then g trop ​ ( u ) g^{\trop}(u) is defined by g trop ​ ( u ) ≔ min v ∈ L | c v ≠ 0 ⁡ ⟨ v , u ⟩ . \displaystyle g^{\trop}(u)\coloneqq\min_{v\in L|c_{v}\neq 0}\langle v,u\rangle. Geometrically (at least in the classical setup), this should be viewed as the valuation of g g along the toric boundary divisor corresponding to the direction u u . Definition 4.17 . One says that 𝒜 q \mathcal{A}_{q} (resp., 𝒳 q \mathcal{X}_{q} ) has enough global monomials (EGM) if for every u ∈ N S u\in N_{S} (resp., every u ∈ M S u\in M_{S} ), there exists a global monomial g ∈ 𝒜 q ord ⊂ 𝒜 q S = 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] g\in\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{A}_{q}^{S}=\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] (resp. g ∈ 𝒳 q ord ⊂ 𝒳 q S = 𝕜 t B ​ [ N ] g\in\mathcal{X}_{q}^{\ord}\subset\mathcal{X}_{q}^{S}=\Bbbk_{t}^{B}[N] ) such that g trop ​ ( u ) < 0 g^{\trop}(u)<0 . The analogous definition applies in the classical setup. Of course, having EGM in the quantum setup is equivalent to having EGM in the classical setup by our usual argument: positivity implies that no terms vanish when applying lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} . See [ GHKK18 , Prop. 0.14] for conditions implying that 𝒜 prin \mathcal{A}^{\prin} has EGM. Proposition 4.18 . Suppose 𝒜 q \mathcal{A}_{q} (resp. 𝒳 q \mathcal{X}_{q} ) has enough global monomials. Then 𝒜 q can \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can} (resp., 𝒳 q can \mathcal{X}^{\can}_{q} ) is finitely generated over 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} . Furthermore, each element of 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} (resp. 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} ) is a finite 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -linear combination of theta functions, so in particular we have 𝒜 q up ⊂ 𝒜 q can \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up}\subset\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can} (resp., 𝒳 q up ⊂ 𝒳 q can \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up}\subset\mathcal{X}_{q}^{\can} ). Proof. The classical analog of the first statement for 𝒜 prin \mathcal{A}^{\prin} and 𝒳 \mathcal{X} is [ GHKK18 , Cor. 8.21] , while the classical analog of the second statement for 𝒜 prin \mathcal{A}^{\prin} is [ GHKK18 , Prop. 8.22] . The same argument applies to prove the second statement for 𝒳 \mathcal{X} , and if the injectivity assumption holds, then the same arguments also apply to 𝒜 \mathcal{A} . The proof of the quantum version is the same. Indeed, the arguments of [ GHKK18 , §8.1-§8.3] are based entirely on properties of convex piecewise-linear functions and on the directions of broken lines/exponents of the associated monomials, and this data is identical in the classical and quantum setups. ∎ 4.9. Proofs of the main theorems Proof of Theorem 1.1 . We now put our results together to complete the proof of Theorem 1.1 . First, note that all broken lines, mutations, and actions of path-ordered products could be defined over ℤ t \mathbb{Z}_{t} rather than 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} , so we may work over ℤ t \mathbb{Z}_{t} as in § 1.1 . The statement that the theta functions form a topological basis for 𝒱 ^ q S \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} is part of Proposition 3.1 . The statement that the global monomials are elements of the theta basis is Corollary 4.13 in the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} case and the proof of Proposition 4.15 in the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} case. The fact that ι 𝒬 ​ ( ϑ p ) \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\vartheta_{p}) has coefficients in ℤ ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] {\mathbb{Z}}[t^{\pm 1}] instead of ℤ [ t ± 1 / D ] {\mathbb{Z}}[t^{\pm 1/D}] was already implicit in our definition of the theta functions in § 3.1 — the fundamental point here is the assumption that ω ⁡ ( L 0 , L ) \omega(L_{0},L) is contained in ℤ \mathbb{Z} instead of just ℚ \mathbb{Q} . That ϑ p \vartheta_{p} is p p -pointed is given by ( 40 ). Next we note that the functions on the walls of 𝔇 𝒱 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{V}_{q}} are bar-invariant and pre-Lefschetz by the corresponding statements in Theorem 2.15 . That ϑ p \vartheta_{p} is bar-invariant and pre-Lefschetz then follows as a special case of Proposition 3.12 . The claim regarding the parity of the coefficients is a special case of Proposition 3.11 . Next, positivity of the coefficients α ⁡ ( p 1 , p 2 , p ) \upalpha(p_{1},p_{2};p) is given by Theorem 3.10 . Finally, atomicity is a special case of Theorem 3.16 , with the extension to the principal coefficients scattering atlas following as in § 4.7.2 . ∎ Proof of Theorem 1.2 . (1) is just Proposition 4.8 . (2) follows from our usual positivity arguments — the cardinality of the set of broken lines with nonzero attached monomial is unchanged when we apply lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} . The statement that Θ 𝒱 mid = Θ 𝒱 , ℝ mid ∩ L 𝒱 \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd}=\Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}}\cap L_{\mathcal{V}} for Θ 𝒱 , ℝ mid \Theta^{\midd}_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} a convex cone then follows from [ GHKK18 , Thm. 0.3(4)] . That this remains a convex cone under the application of any T ȷ → 𝒱 T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}} is then a consequence of (6), which itself follows from Corollaries A.4 and A.5 . (3) is Proposition 4.14 for 𝒱 = 𝒜 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A} and Proposition 4.15 for 𝒱 = 𝒳 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{X} . (4) is the combination of Propositions 4.16 and 4.18 . The first statement of (5) is a special case of (2), and the second statement then follows from the last part of (4). (7) follows from Proposition 3.17 . Next, (8) follows from Lemmas 4.2 and 4.7 . The ∗ = up *=\up case is part of Lemma 4.2 , while the ∗ = can *=\can case follows from the fact that B 1 B_{1} takes theta functions to theta functions, which is the statement of Lemma 4.7 . Combining these, a theta function in 𝒳 q up \mathcal{X}_{q}^{\up} maps to a theta function and also to an element of 𝒜 q up \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\up} , so the ∗ = mid *=\midd case follows. The ∗ = ord *=\ord case follows from noting that B 1 B_{1} takes global monomials to global monomials. The fact that the image has degree 0 0 is immediate from the definition of the K 𝒜 K_{\mathcal{A}} –grading and from the homogeneity of the theta functions as in Proposition 3.17 . Finally, (9) is a special case of Proposition 3.18 . ∎ Proof of Theorem 1.5 . By definition, S S being acyclic means that ( B ⁡ ( e i , e j ) ) i , j ∈ I ∖ F (B(e_{i},e_{j}))_{i,j\in I\setminus F} is the signed adjacency matrix of an acyclic quiver. If there is a compatible Λ \Uplambda , then ( 4.1 ) implies that ( Λ ⁡ ( B 1 ​ ( e i ) , B 1 ​ ( e j ) ) ) i , j ∈ I ∖ F \left(\Uplambda(B_{1}(e_{i}),B_{1}(e_{j}))\right)_{i,j\in I\setminus F} is also the signed adjacency matrix of an acyclic quiver. Theorem 2.18 thus applies to by 𝔇 𝒳 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{X}_{q}} and 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} . Finally, the claim for the theta functions follows using Lemma 3.5 . ∎ Proof of Theorem 1.6 . It follows from Theorem 1.2 (2,3) for ∗ = mid , can *=\midd,\can , or more directly for ∗ = ord *=\ord , that the images of these restrictions are the corresponding classical analogs, so in these three cases they indeed give surjective homomorphisms lim t → 1 : 𝒱 q ∗ → 𝒱 ∗ \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*} . Now, since ⟨ t 1 / D − 1 ⟩ ​ 𝒱 ^ q S \langle t^{1/D}-1\rangle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} is the kernel of lim t → 1 : 𝒱 ^ q S → 𝒱 ^ S ≔ 𝕜 σ ​ ⟦ L ⟧ \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{S}\coloneqq\Bbbk_{\upsigma}\llbracket L\rrbracket , the injectivity problem amounts to showing that ( ⟨ t 1 / D − 1 ⟩ ​ 𝒱 ^ q S ) ∩ 𝒱 q ∗ ⊂ ⟨ t 1 / D − 1 ⟩ ​ 𝒱 q ∗ \displaystyle\left(\langle t^{1/D}-1\rangle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S}\right)\cap\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*}\subset\langle t^{1/D}-1\rangle\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*} (the reverse containment being obvious). Suppose ∗ = up *=\up . If f ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S ∖ 𝒱 q up f\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} , then there is some cluster where f f is an infinite Laurent series (not a Laurent polynomial), and then ( t 1 / D − 1 ) ​ f (t^{1/D}-1)f is also an infinite series in this cluster. So then ( t 1 / D − 1 ) ​ f ∉ 𝒱 q up (t^{1/D}-1)f\notin\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} . I.e., ( t 1 / D − 1 ) ​ f (t^{1/D}-1)f can be in 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} only if f ∈ 𝒱 q up f\in\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} . The desired containment follows. Now suppose ∗ = mid *=\midd or ∗ = can *=\can . Let f = ∑ p a p ​ ϑ p ∈ 𝒱 q ∗ f=\sum_{p}a_{p}\vartheta_{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{q}^{*} lie in the kernel of lim t → 1 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1} . Let ϑ ¯ p \overline{\vartheta}_{p} denote the classical theta function corresponding to p p as in [ GHKK18 ] , so ϑ ¯ p = lim t → 1 ( ϑ p ) \overline{\vartheta}_{p}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}(\vartheta_{p}) by Theorem 1.1 (4). Then lim t → 1 ( f ) = ∑ p lim t → 1 ( a p ) ​ ϑ ¯ p , \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}(f)=\sum_{p}\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}(a_{p})\overline{\vartheta}_{p}, and since the classical theta functions are linearly independent, this implies that lim t → 1 ( a p ) = 0 \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}(a_{p})=0 for each p p . Hence, each a p a_{p} is divisible by ( t 1 / D − 1 ) (t^{1/D}-1) , and so f f must be divisible by ( t 1 / D − 1 ) (t^{1/D}-1) in 𝒱 q ∗ \mathcal{V}_{q}^{*} , as desired. Finally, suppose that 𝒱 ord = 𝒱 mid \mathcal{V}^{\ord}=\mathcal{V}^{\midd} . Since we have proven the claim for ∗ = mid *=\midd , the ∗ = ord *=\ord case follows as long as 𝒱 q ord = 𝒱 q mid \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\ord}=\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\midd} . Let Θ \Theta denote the set of p ∈ L 𝒱 p\in L_{\mathcal{V}} for which ϑ p \vartheta_{p} is a global monomial (alternatively, a quantum cluster variable), and let Θ ′ = Θ 𝒱 mid \Theta^{\prime}=\Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd} . Then the claim follows from Lemma 3.15 , because 𝒱 ord = 𝒱 mid \mathcal{V}^{\ord}=\mathcal{V}^{\midd} exactly means that { lim t → 1 ϑ p } p ∈ Θ ′ \{\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Theta^{\prime}} spans A Θ = 𝒱 ord A_{\Theta}=\mathcal{V}^{\ord} as in the hypotheses there. ∎ 5. Reduction of quantum positivity to the two-wall cases Our goal for the remainder of the paper is to prove Theorem 2.15 . In this section, we will use the arguments of [ GHKK18 , §C.3] to reduce to the case of two initial walls, i.e., we will reduce to the case (71) 𝔇 in = { ( v 1 ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a 1 ​ z v 1 ) ) , ( v 2 ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a 2 ​ z v 2 ) ) } \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\In}=\{(v_{1}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-t^{a_{1}}z^{v_{1}})),(v_{2}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-t^{a_{2}}z^{v_{2}}))\} for a 1 , a 2 ∈ ℤ a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z} to prove the positivity statement in Theorem 2.15 , and the case (72) 𝔇 in = { ( v 1 ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − 𝒫 a 1 ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v 1 ) ) , ( v 2 ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − 𝒫 a 2 ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v 2 ) ) } \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\In}=\{(v_{1}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-\mskip-3.0mu\pl_{a_{1}}(t)z^{v_{1}})),(v_{2}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-\mskip-3.0mu\pl_{a_{2}}(t)z^{v_{2}}))\} to prove the statement regarding pL type polynomials. Furthermore, we will be able to assume that v 1 v_{1} and v 2 v_{2} form part of a basis for L L . From here, [ GHKK18 ] uses their “change of lattice trick” to further reduce to the case where | ω ⁡ ( v 1 , v 2 ) | = 1 |\omega(v_{1},v_{2})|=1 . However, this final trick does not work in the quantum setting! We will therefore use different methods, developed in § 6 and applied in § 7 , to tackle the cases of ( 71 ) and ( 72 ). 5.1. The change of monoid trick For any v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} , we say v v has order k k if v ∈ k ​ L 0 + ∖ ( k + 1 ) ​ L 0 + v\in kL_{0}^{+}\setminus(k+1)L_{0}^{+} . We review this here. Let u 1 , … , u r u_{1},\ldots,u_{r} be a basis for L L .
