The perturbation trick Firstly, using ( 25 ) and Example 2.8 (1), if 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} is a scattering diagram for which all the walls have the form given in ( 34 ), we may replace 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} with an equivalent scattering diagram for which all of the walls are of the form (73) ( v i ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v i ) ) \displaystyle(v_{i}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-t^{a}z^{v_{i}})) where the number of such walls appearing in the equivalent scattering diagram is the coefficient of t a t^{a} in p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) . Similarly, if all of the polynomials p i ​ ( t ) p_{i}(t) are of pL type, we may replace 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} with an equivalent diagram in which all walls are of the form (74) ( v i ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − 𝒫 j ⁡ ( t ) ​ z v i ) ) (v_{i}^{\omega\perp},\EE(-\mskip-3.0mu\pl_{j}(t)z^{v_{i}})) where the number of copies of this wall is given by λ j \lambda_{j} in the decomposition p i ​ ( t ) = ∑ j ∈ ℤ λ j ​ 𝒫 j ⁡ ( t ) . p_{i}(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{j}\pl_{j}(t). For any scattering diagram 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , the asymptotic scattering diagram 𝔇 as \mathfrak{D}_{\as} of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} is defined as follows: every wall ( v + 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 (v+\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D} , with 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} denoting a rational polyhedral cone (apex at the origin) and v ∈ L ℝ v\in L_{\mathbb{R}} translating this cone, is replaced by the wall ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) . Note that consistency of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} implies consistency of 𝔇 as \mathfrak{D}_{\as} . Indeed, for each k > 0 k>0 and each closed path γ : [ 0 , 1 ] → L ℝ \upgamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow L_{\mathbb{R}} crossing the walls of 𝔇 as \mathfrak{D}_{\as} transversely, we can find R ≫ 0 R\gg 0 such that θ γ , ( 𝔇 k ) as = θ R ​ γ , ( 𝔇 k ) as = θ R ​ γ , 𝔇 k = Id ∈ G k , \displaystyle\uptheta_{\upgamma,(\mathfrak{D}_{k})_{\as}}=\uptheta_{R\upgamma,(\mathfrak{D}_{k})_{\as}}=\uptheta_{R\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}_{k}}=\id\in G_{k}, where R ​ γ R\upgamma is the path mapping t t to R ⋅ γ ⁡ ( t ) R\cdot\upgamma(t) in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} . Since this holds for every k k , we deduce that θ γ , 𝔇 as = Id ∈ G \uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\as}}=\Id\in G . We deal with the positivity statement in Theorem 2.15 first. So assume that we have replaced 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} with an equivalent scattering diagram in which all walls are of the form ( 73 ). Now suppose that we deform 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} by translating each wall by some generic vector in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} , yielding a new scattering diagram 𝔇 in ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In} . Let 𝔇 ′ ≔ Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ′ ) \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}\coloneqq\scat(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In}) . Since 𝔇 as ′ = Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\as}=\scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) , it suffices to check that all scattering functions of 𝔇 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime} (up to equivalence) have the form 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v ) \EE(-t^{a}z^{v}) for various v ∈ L 0 + v\in L_{0}^{+} and a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} . Suppose inductively that every wall of any such 𝔇 k ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k} (up to equivalence) is of the form ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) for some f 𝔡 = 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v ) f_{\mathfrak{d}}=\EE(-t^{a}z^{v}) in G k G_{k} . Since ( 𝔇 in ′ ) 1 (\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In})_{1} is already consistent over 𝔤 1 \mathfrak{g}_{1} (because 𝔤 1 \mathfrak{g}_{1} is Abelian and all walls of 𝔇 in ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In} are full affine hyperplanes), all walls of 𝔇 ′ ∖ 𝔇 in ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In} have order > 1 >1 (the order of a non-trivial wall ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) is the smallest integer k k for which the projection of f 𝔡 f_{\mathfrak{d}} to G k G_{k} is non-trivial). Hence, 𝔇 1 ′ = ( 𝔇 in ′ ) 1 \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1}=(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In})_{1} (up to equivalence), and since all walls of 𝔇 in ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In} have the desired form, this yields the base case for the induction. Now consider 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} . Since this scattering diagram is finite, we can first apply the equivalences of Example 2.8 (1,2) to replace 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} with an equivalent scattering diagram for which any two walls intersect in codimension at least 2. Then we again use Example 2.8 (1) to replace 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} with an equivalent scattering diagram for which all functions on walls are of the form 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ ​ t a ​ z v ) \EE(\epsilon t^{a}z^{v}) for ϵ = ± 1 \epsilon=\pm 1 and various a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} and v ∈ L ⊕ v\in L^{\oplus} , and any two walls either have identical support, or intersect in codimension at least two, i.e., only along a joint. We pick this 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} to be minimal, in the following sense: if two walls ( 𝔡 1 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ 1 ​ t a 1 ​ z v 1 ) ) (\mathfrak{d}_{1},\EE(\epsilon_{1}t^{a_{1}}z^{v_{1}})) and ( 𝔡 2 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ 2 ​ t a 2 ​ z v 2 ) ) (\mathfrak{d}_{2},\EE(\epsilon_{2}t^{a_{2}}z^{v_{2}})) satisfy 𝔡 1 = 𝔡 2 \mathfrak{d}_{1}=\mathfrak{d}_{2} , a 1 = a 2 a_{1}=a_{2} and v 1 = v 2 v_{1}=v_{2} , then ϵ 1 = ϵ 2 \epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2} . Since ( 𝔇 k + 1 ′ ) k (\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1})_{k} is equivalent to 𝔇 k ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k} , it follows from the inductive assumption that if the order of v v is less than k + 1 k+1 , then any wall of the form ( 𝔡 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ ​ t a ​ z v ) ) (\mathfrak{d},\EE(\epsilon t^{a}z^{v})) in 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} has ϵ = − 1 \epsilon=-1 , as required. So to complete the inductive step we consider a wall ( 𝔡 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ ​ t a ​ z v ) ) (\mathfrak{d},\EE(\epsilon t^{a}z^{v})) of 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} in which v v has order k + 1 k+1 . If the wall is incoming, then ϵ = − 1 \epsilon=-1 by assumption. If it is not incoming, then 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} contains a joint 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} , i.e, a codimension 2 2 stratum of the affine polyhedral complex formed by 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} . Consider a point p ∈ 𝔧 p\in\mathfrak{j} that is not in any other joint of 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} (i.e., not in a codimension 3 3 stratum of this affine polyhedral complex). We define a new scattering diagram 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} by taking only the walls of 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} that contain p p and extending them to infinity. Precisely, for each wall ( 𝔡 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ ′ ​ t a ′ ​ z v ′ ) ) (\mathfrak{d},\EE(\epsilon^{\prime}t^{a^{\prime}}z^{v^{\prime}})) of 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} , with p ∈ 𝔡 p\in\mathfrak{d} , the scattering diagram 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} contains the wall ( { p + δ ( x − p ) ∈ L ℝ : x ∈ 𝔡 , δ ∈ ℝ ≥ 0 } , 𝔼 ( ϵ ′ t a ′ z v ′ ) ) . \left(\{p+\updelta(x-p)\in L_{\mathbb{R}}\colon x\in\mathfrak{d},\updelta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\},\EE(\epsilon^{\prime}t^{a^{\prime}}z^{v^{\prime}})\right). This scattering diagram is consistent, and contains as its unique joint the codimension 2 subspace of L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} containing 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} . Also, since walls of 𝔇 in ′ \mathfrak{D}_{\In}^{\prime} were generically translated, and since all walls of 𝔇 ′ ∖ 𝔇 in ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\In} have order > 1 >1 , 𝔇 k + 1 ′ \mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1} has at most two order 1 1 walls containing 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} , and so 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} has at most two order 1 1 walls. Let us label the walls of 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} as { ( 𝔡 j , 𝔼 ( ϵ j t a j z v j ) : j = 1 , … , s } \{(\mathfrak{d}_{j},\EE(\epsilon_{j}t^{a_{j}}z^{v_{j}})\colon j=1,\ldots,s\} . We may assume that any order 1 1 walls have j = 1 j=1 or 2 2 . We claim that, up to equivalence, we can assume that every incoming wall ( 𝔡 , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ϵ ​ t a ​ z v ) ) \left(\mathfrak{d},\EE(\epsilon t^{a}z^{v})\right) of 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} has ϵ = − 1 \epsilon=-1 . If the order of v v is less than k + 1 k+1 , this follows from the inductive hypothesis. If the order of v v equals k + 1 k+1 , since 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v ) \EE(-t^{a}z^{v}) is central in G k + 1 G_{k+1} , we can add the wall ( v ω ⟂ + p , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v ) ) (v^{\omega\perp}+p,\EE(-t^{a}z^{v})) to 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} without changing its equivalence class, and then by Examples 2.8 (1,2) we can remove any incoming wall for which ϵ = 1 \epsilon=1 . Now we apply the change of monoid trick to 𝔇 k + 1 , p \mathfrak{D}_{k+1,p} . Let 𝔇 ~ k + 1 , p , in \widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{k+1,p,\In} denote the lifted scattering diagram in L ~ ℝ \widetilde{L}_{\mathbb{R}} as in § 5.1 , and consider a wall ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 ~ k + 1 , p , in ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\scat(\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{k+1,p,\In}) whose projection to L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} has order k + 1 k+1 . Since the order of v ~ j \widetilde{v}_{j} is less than the order of v j v_{j} for j ≥ 3 j\geq 3 , the order of ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) is ≤ k \leq k in L ~ 0 + \widetilde{L}_{0}^{+} unless the wall is in Scat ( { ( 𝔡 ~ j , 𝔼 ( − t a j z v ~ j ) ) : j = 1 , 2 } ) \scat(\{(\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{j},\EE(-t^{a_{j}}z^{\widetilde{v}_{j}}))\colon j=1,2\}) . If the order is ≤ k \leq k , then the inductive assumption applies to show that ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) has the desired form. Thus, it suffices to check the claim for Scat ( { ( 𝔡 ~ j , 𝔼 ( − t a j z v ~ j ) ) : j = 1 , 2 } ) \scat(\{(\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{j},\EE(-t^{a_{j}}z^{\widetilde{v}_{j}}))\colon j=1,2\}) . We have thus reduced the positivity statement in Theorem 2.15 to the case of two incoming walls. Relabelling and applying the change of monoid trick, we can assume that our initial scattering diagram is (75) 𝔇 in = { ( v 1 ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a 1 ​ z v 1 ) ) , ( v 2 ω ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t a 2 ​ z v 2 ) ) } \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\In}=\{(v_{1}^{\omega^{\perp}},\EE(-t^{a_{1}}z^{v_{1}})),(v_{2}^{\omega^{\perp}},\EE(-t^{a_{2}}z^{v_{2}}))\} for v 1 , v 2 v_{1},v_{2} forming a basis for L ⊕ L^{\oplus} . This is exactly the special case of the positivity statement of Theorem 2.15 that is considered in Corollary 7.8 . Exactly the same argument, starting with replacing 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} by a scattering diagram in which all walls are of the form given in ( 74 ), and continuing to replace instances of t a t^{a} for various a a in the proof by 𝒫 a ⁡ ( t ) \pl_{a}(t) , reduces the statement regarding pL type polynomials in Theorem 2.15 to the case of two walls as in ( 72 ). 6. Positivity for Donaldson–Thomas invariants After a reminder of some notation and constructions regarding quiver representations in § 6.1 , in § 6.2 we prove Theorem 6.15 , our main positivity result for the refined DT invariants of the category of right modules for the path algebra ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q of a quiver Q Q . This generalizes a positivity result in [ MR19 ] which applied only to the case of acyclic quivers. The proof of this theorem is within the framework of cohomological Donaldson–Thomas theory. The reader who is more interested in cluster algebras than Donaldson–Thomas theory is advised to focus on this positivity theorem, as opposed to the one that follows it in § 6.3 , since it is sufficient to prove the positivity of quantum theta functions and involves fewer unfamiliar concepts, so it should be easier to digest for the uninitiated. On the other hand, since with a little work, the original positivity result of Meinhardt and Reineke [ MR19 ] , written in the language of refined DT theory, can be used to prove the pL preservation statement of Theorem 2.15 , we present this version of the theory in § 6.3 . Since the pL property is stronger than positivity, this provides an alternative proof of the (strong) positivity of quantum theta functions. 6.1. Quiver representations We introduce the background notation for talking about either flavour of Donaldson–Thomas theory for quivers. We will work with right modules throughout, and so our sign conventions will agree with [ Dav18 ] and differ slightly from [ DM20 ] . A quiver Q Q is determined by two sets Q 1 Q_{1} and Q 0 Q_{0} , the arrows and vertices respectively, along with a pair of maps s , t : Q 1 → Q 0 s,t\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0} taking an arrow to its source and target, respectively. We will always assume that Q 0 Q_{0} and Q 1 Q_{1} are finite. In contrast with the usual situation in cluster algebras, we allow Q Q to contain oriented cyclic paths of length 1 1 and 2 2 . We denote by K = ℤ ≥ 0 Q 0 K=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q_{0}} the semigroup of dimension vectors for Q Q . For i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} we denote by 1 i ∈ K 1_{i}\in K the generator corresponding to i i . We denote by ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q the free path algebra of Q Q over ℂ \mathbb{C} , and for each i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} we denote by e ˙ i \dot{e}_{i} the “lazy path” of length 0 0 beginning and ending at i i . Let v ∈ K v\in K . A v v -dimensional right ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho is determined by a Q 0 Q_{0} -tuple of complex vector spaces { ρ i ≔ ρ ⋅ e ˙ i } i ∈ Q 0 \{\rho_{i}\coloneqq\rho\cdot\dot{e}_{i}\}_{i\in Q_{0}} with dim ℂ ( ρ i ) = v i \dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\rho_{i})=v_{i} and a linear map ρ ⁡ ( a ) : ρ t ⁡ ( a ) → ρ s ⁡ ( a ) \rho(a)\colon\rho_{t(a)}\rightarrow\rho_{s(a)} for each a ∈ Q 1 a\in Q_{1} . We denote by (76) 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) ≔ ∏ a ∈ Q 1 Hom ⁡ ( ℂ v t ⁡ ( a ) , ℂ v s ⁡ ( a ) ) / GL v \mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q)\coloneqq\prod_{a\in Q_{1}}\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{v_{t(a)}},\mathbb{C}^{v_{s(a)}})/\GL_{v} the stack of v v -dimensional right ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules, where GL v ≔ ∏ i ∈ Q 0 GL v i \GL_{v}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\GL_{v_{i}} is the gauge group, acting by change of basis on each of the factors ℂ v i \mathbb{C}^{v_{i}} . This stack is smooth, and so all open substacks of it are also smooth. We denote by dim ¯ ( ρ ) = ( dim ( ρ i ) ) i ∈ Q 0 ∈ K \udim(\rho)=(\dim(\rho_{i}))_{i\in Q_{0}}\in K the dimension vector of a ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho . Then dim ( ρ ) = ∑ i ∈ Q 0 dim ¯ ( ρ ) i . \dim(\rho)=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\udim(\rho)_{i}. We define the Euler pairing on dimension vectors via χ Q : \displaystyle\upchi_{Q}\mskip-3.0mu\colon ℤ Q 0 × ℤ Q 0 → ℤ \displaystyle\,\,\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z} ( γ ′ , γ ′′ ) ↦ ∑ i ∈ Q 0 γ i ′ ​ γ i ′′ − ∑ a ∈ Q 1 γ t ⁡ ( a ) ′ ​ γ s ⁡ ( a ) ′′ , \displaystyle(\upgamma^{\prime},\upgamma^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\upgamma^{\prime}_{i}\upgamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}-\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}\upgamma^{\prime}_{t(a)}\upgamma^{\prime\prime}_{s(a)}, and we define (77) B Q ​ ( γ ′ , γ ′′ ) = χ Q ​ ( γ ′′ , γ ′ ) − χ Q ​ ( γ ′ , γ ′′ ) . \displaystyle B_{Q}(\upgamma^{\prime},\upgamma^{\prime\prime})=\upchi_{Q}(\upgamma^{\prime\prime},\upgamma^{\prime})-\upchi_{Q}(\upgamma^{\prime},\upgamma^{\prime\prime}). Where there is no possibility of confusion we will omit the superscript Q Q . Note that dim 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) = − χ Q ​ ( v , v ) \dim\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q)=-\upchi_{Q}(v,v) . We denote by ℬ Q \mathscr{B}_{Q} the ℤ ⁡ ( ( t ) ) \mathbb{Z}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu) -algebra freely generated as a ℤ ⁡ ( ( t ) ) \mathbb{Z}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu) -module by symbols x v x^{v} , for v ∈ K v\in K . For i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} we write x i ≔ x 1 i x_{i}\coloneqq x^{1_{i}} . The quantum tropical vertex group G Q qtr G^{\qtrop}_{Q} is defined to be the closure of the subgroup of the group of units ℬ ^ Q × \hat{\mathscr{B}}_{Q}^{\times} generated by elements 𝔼 ⁡ ( t n ​ x v ) \EE(t^{n}x^{v}) for v ∈ K ∖ { 0 } v\in K\setminus\{0\} and n ∈ ℤ n\in\mathbb{Z} . As in § 2.2.3 , this group is obtained by exponentiation from a Lie subalgebra (defined over ℤ \mathbb{Z} ) 𝔤 Q qtr \mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\qtrop} of a Lie algebra 𝔤 ^ Q \hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q} . The algebra 𝔤 ^ Q \hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q} is in turn obtained by completing a Lie algebra 𝔤 Q \mathfrak{g}_{Q} with respect to the sequence of Lie ideals 𝔤 Q ∩ I Q n \mathfrak{g}_{Q}\cap I_{Q}^{n} . The Lie algebra 𝔤 Q \mathfrak{g}_{Q} here is the Lie subalgebra of the commutator Lie algebra of ℬ Q \mathscr{B}_{Q} generated over 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} by the elements x ^ v ≔ x v / ( | v | ) t \hat{x}^{v}\coloneqq x^{v}/(\lvert v\lvert)_{t} for 0 ≠ v ∈ K 0\neq v\in K , where | v | \lvert v\lvert is the index of v v , i.e. the largest positive integer such that v / | v | ∈ K v/\lvert v\lvert\in K . Then 𝔤 Q qtr \mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\qtrop} is the closure of the ℤ \mathbb{Z} -span of the quantum dilogarithms − Li ⁡ ( − t a ​ z v , t ) -\Li(-t^{a}z^{v};t) . 6.1.1. Stability conditions Let ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} be a stability condition . We define the slope of a non-trivial ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho by μ ζ ​ ( ρ ) = ζ ⋅ dim ¯ ( ρ ) dim ( ρ ) . \upmu_{\upzeta}(\rho)=\frac{\upzeta\cdot\udim(\rho)}{\dim(\rho)}. We will omit the subscript ζ \upzeta when the choice of stability condition is clear. We denote by S θ ζ ⊂ ℤ ≥ 0 Q 0 S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{Q_{0}} the submonoid of dimension vectors of slope θ \uptheta with respect to ζ \upzeta . We say that a stability condition ζ \upzeta is θ \uptheta - generic if B ⁡ ( v , v ′ ) = 0 B(v,v^{\prime})=0 for all v , v ′ ∈ S θ ζ v,v^{\prime}\in S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta} . We say a stability condition is simply generic if it is θ \uptheta -generic for every θ \uptheta . A ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module is called ζ \upzeta - semistable if for all non-trivial proper submodules ρ ′ ⊂ ρ \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho we have the inequality of slopes μ ζ ​ ( ρ ′ ) ≤ μ ζ ​ ( ρ ) \upmu_{\upzeta}(\rho^{\prime})\leq\upmu_{\upzeta}(\rho) , and ρ \rho is called ζ \upzeta -stable if this inequality is strict for all non-trivial proper submodules. We denote by 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) ⊂ 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q)\subset\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q) the open substack of v v -dimensional ζ \upzeta -semistable ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules. We denote by ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) the coarse moduli space of ζ \upzeta -semistable v v -dimensional ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules defined by King [ Kin91 ] , and by ℳ v ζ ​ -st ​ ( Q ) ⊂ ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\stable}_{v}(Q)\subset\mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) the smooth open subvariety corresponding to ζ \upzeta -stable modules. 11 11 11 Strictly speaking, King defines these spaces as GIT quotients under the assumption that ζ ∈ ℚ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}} . So we define these moduli spaces by taking a perturbation ζ v ′ ∈ ℚ Q 0 \upzeta^{\prime}_{v}\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}} of ζ \upzeta , which will depend on v v in general, such that ζ \upzeta -(semi)stability for v v -dimensional ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules is equivalent to ζ ′ \upzeta^{\prime} -(semi)stability. As it is constructed as a GIT quotient, the map from ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) to its affinization is projective. On the other hand, by [ LP90 , Thm. 1] the functions on the affinization are generated by taking traces of evaluations of modules on oriented cycles in Q Q . It follows that ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) is projective if and only if the support of v v does not contain an oriented cycle in Q Q , and ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) is projective for all v ∈ K v\in K if Q Q is acyclic. Let ρ \rho be a non-trivial ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module. Then ρ \rho admits a unique Harder–Narasimhan filtration 0 = ρ ( 0 ) ⊂ ρ ( 1 ) ⊂ … ​ ρ ( r ) = ρ 0=\rho_{(0)}\subset\rho_{(1)}\subset\ldots\rho_{(r)}=\rho for some r ∈ ℤ ≥ 1 r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1} , such that (1) Each subquotient ρ ( i ) / ρ ( i − 1 ) \rho_{(i)}/\rho_{(i-1)} for 1 ≤ i ≤ r 1\leq i\leq r is a non-trivial ζ \upzeta -semistable ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module. (2) There is a strict inequality of slopes μ ζ ​ ( ρ ( i ) / ρ ( i − 1 ) ) > μ ζ ​ ( ρ ( i + 1 ) / ρ ( i ) ) \upmu_{\upzeta}(\rho_{(i)}/\rho_{(i-1)})>\upmu_{\upzeta}(\rho_{(i+1)}/\rho_{(i)}) for 1 ≤ i ≤ r − 1 1\leq i\leq r-1 . Let I ⊂ ( − ∞ , ∞ ) I\subset(-\infty,\infty) be an interval. We denote by 𝔐 I ζ ​ ( Q ) ⊂ 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta}_{I}(Q)\subset\mathfrak{M}(Q) the open substack, the points of which correspond to modules ρ \rho satisfying the condition that each subquotient ρ ( i ) / ρ ( i − 1 ) \rho_{(i)}/\rho_{(i-1)} appearing in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho has slope contained in I I . We define 𝔐 I , v ζ ​ ( Q ) = 𝔐 I ζ ​ ( Q ) ∩ 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}_{I,v}^{\upzeta}(Q)=\mathfrak{M}_{I}^{\upzeta}(Q)\cap\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q) . 6.2. Cohomological DT theory We denote by Vect + \Vect^{+} the Abelian category of cohomologically graded vector spaces V V such that H i ⁡ ( V ) = 0 \HO^{i}(V)=0 for i ≪ 0 i\ll 0 , and for which dim ( H i ⁡ ( V ) ) < ∞ \dim(\HO^{i}(V))<\infty for all i ∈ ℤ i\in\mathbb{Z} . We denote by Vect K + \Vect^{+}_{K} the category of formal direct sums ⨁ v ∈ K V v \bigoplus_{v\in K}V_{v} with V v ∈ Vect + V_{v}\in\Vect^{+} . We denote by Vect θ ζ , + \Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} the full subcategory of Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} containing those V V such that V v = 0 V_{v}=0 for v ∉ S θ ζ v\notin S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta} . We give Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} the twisted monoidal structure, defined by (78) ( V ′ ⊗ tw V ′′ ) v = ⨁ v ′ + v ′′ = v V v ′ ′ ⊗ V v ′′ ′′ ​ [ B ⁡ ( v ′′ , v ′ ) ] . (V^{\prime}\otimes^{\tw}V^{\prime\prime})_{v}=\bigoplus_{v^{\prime}+v^{\prime\prime}=v}V^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}_{v^{\prime\prime}}[B(v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime})]. The associator for this monoidal product is as defined in [ DM20 , Sec 3.2] . For ζ \upzeta a θ \uptheta -generic stability condition, the shift by B ⁡ ( v ′′ , v ′ ) B(v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}) is trivial on Vect θ ζ , + \Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} and ( 78 ) is part of the usual symmetric monoidal structure on graded vector spaces, incorporating the Koszul sign rule with respect to the cohomological degree. The monoidal product ( 78 ) induces a (noncommutative) ring structure on K_0 ⁡ ( Vect K + ) \KK(\Vect^{+}_{K}) . The characteristic function (79) χ K , t : K_0 ⁡ ( Vect K + ) → \displaystyle\upchi_{K,t}\colon\KK(\Vect^{+}_{K})\rightarrow ℬ ^ Q \displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{B}}_{Q} [ V ] ↦ \displaystyle[V]\mapsto ∑ i ∈ ℤ ∑ v ∈ K dim ( H i ⁡ ( V v ) ) ​ t i ​ x v \displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{v\in K}\dim(\HO^{i}(V_{v}))t^{i}x^{v} is an isomorphism satisfying χ K , t ​ ( V ′ ⊗ tw V ′′ ) = χ K , t ​ ( V ′ ) ​ χ K , t ​ ( V ′′ ) . \upchi_{K,t}(V^{\prime}\otimes^{\tw}V^{\prime\prime})=\upchi_{K,t}(V^{\prime})\upchi_{K,t}(V^{\prime\prime}). Furthermore, ( 79 ) is an isomorphism of ℤ ⁡ ( ( t ) ) \mathbb{Z}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu) -algebras, where t t acts on K_0 ⁡ ( Vect K + ) \KK(\Vect^{+}_{K}) by increasing cohomological degree by one. For v ∈ K v\in K we define 𝒜 v ζ ​ ( Q ) ≔ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir \mathcal{A}_{v}^{\upzeta}(Q)\coloneqq\HO\left(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q}\right)_{\vir} . Let ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} , and let θ ∈ ( − ∞ , ∞ ) \uptheta\in(-\infty,\infty) be a slope. We define 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) ≔ ⨁ v ∈ S θ ζ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∈ ob ⁡ ( Vect θ ζ , + ) . \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{v\in S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}}\HO\left(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q}\right)_{\vir}\in\ob(\Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta}). Here and elsewhere, if X X is a disjoint union of connected irreducible stacks X = ∐ i ∈ I X i X=\coprod_{i\in I}X_{i} we set H ⁡ ( X , ℚ ) vir ≔ ⨁ i ∈ I H ⁡ ( X i , ℚ ) ​ [ dim ( X i ) ] . \HO(X,\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in I}\HO\left(X_{i},\mathbb{Q}\right)[\dim(X_{i})]. Via the inclusion of categories Vect θ ζ , + ⊂ Vect K + \Vect_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta,+}\subset\Vect_{K}^{+} we can consider 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q) as an object in Vect K + \Vect_{K}^{+} . 6.2.1. Cohomological DT invariants As in [ MR19 ] we define (80) BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ≔ { IC ⁡ ( ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) ​ [ 1 − χ Q ​ ( v , v ) ] if ​ ℳ v ζ ​ -st ​ ( Q ) ≠ ∅ 0 otherwise , \BPS^{\upzeta}_{v}(Q)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\IC\left(\mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sst}(Q),\mathbb{Q}\right)[1-\upchi_{Q}(v,v)]&\textrm{if }\mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\stable}_{v}(Q)\neq\emptyset\\ 0&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases} where in the first line we have taken the total hypercohomology of the intersection complex on the (possibly singular) irreducible variety ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) — i.e. the usual intersection cohomology of ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) . The vector space BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) \BPS_{v}^{\upzeta}(Q) is a finite-dimensional cohomologically graded vector space, and so is in particular an element of Vect + \Vect^{+} . Let ℂ ∗ ⊂ GL v \mathbb{C}^{*}\subset\GL_{v} denote the subgroup containing Q 0 Q_{0} -tuples of the form ( λ ⋅ Id v i × v i ) i ∈ Q 0 (\lambda\cdot\Id_{v_{i}\times v_{i}})_{i\in Q_{0}} for λ ∈ ℂ ∗ \lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*} . Assuming that ℳ v ζ ​ -st ​ ( Q ) ≠ 0 \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\stable}_{v}(Q)\neq 0 , it is the quotient of a free action of GL v / ℂ ∗ \GL_{v}/\mathbb{C}^{*} on an open subspace of ∏ a ∈ Q 1 Hom ⁡ ( ℂ v t ⁡ ( a ) , ℂ v s ⁡ ( a ) ) \prod_{a\in Q_{1}}\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{v_{t(a)}},\mathbb{C}^{v_{s(a)}}) . Moreover, ℳ v ζ ​ -st ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\stable}(Q) is a dense open subvariety of ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sst}(Q) . It follows that (81) dim ( ℳ v ζ ​ -st ​ ( Q ) ) = dim ( ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) ) = 1 − χ Q ​ ( v , v ) . \dim(\mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\stable}(Q))=\dim(\mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sst}(Q))=1-\upchi_{Q}(v,v). Remark 6.1 . Assume that the support of v v contains no oriented cycles in Q Q , so that ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) is projective. Since the cohomological shift in the definition of BPS v ζ \BPS^{\upzeta}_{v} is equal to the complex dimension of ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q) , it follows from Poincaré duality for intersection cohomology that χ t ​ ( BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) \upchi_{t}(\BPS^{\upzeta}_{v}(Q)) is bar-invariant. By the hard Lefschetz theorem for intersection cohomology [ BBD82 , Chap. 6] , we deduce moreover that 𝑂𝑃𝐸𝑁 χ t ​ ( BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) ) \upchi_{t}(\BPS^{\upzeta}_{v}(Q))) is of Lefschetz type. Lemma 6.2 . For a quiver Q Q , dimension vector v v and stability condition ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} , BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) \BPS^{\upzeta}_{v}(Q) is nonzero if and only if there exists a ζ \upzeta -stable v v -dimensional ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -representation, and so in particular 1 − χ Q ​ ( v , v ) ≥ 0 1-\upchi_{Q}(v,v)\geq 0 . If there exists such a stable module, there are equalities dim ( H j ⁡ ( BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) ) = { 1 if ​ j = χ Q ​ ( v , v ) − 1 0 if | j | > 1 − χ Q ( v , v ) . \dim(\HO^{j}(\BPS^{\upzeta}_{v}(Q)))=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if }j=\upchi_{Q}(v,v)-1\\ 0&\textrm{if }\lvert j\lvert>1-\upchi_{Q}(v,v).\end{cases} Proof. First we claim that for any quasiprojective variety X X the degree zero intersection cohomology IC 0 ⁡ ( X , ℚ ) \IC^{0}(X,\mathbb{Q}) has a natural basis indexed by the irreducible components of X X , which we label X p X_{p} for p p in an indexing set P X P_{X} . This follows from definitions and foundational results in intersection homology, see [ KW06 , Chapter 4] . In a little more detail: Firstly, the morphism from the normalization X ~ → X \widetilde{X}\rightarrow X is small, so IC ( X , ℚ ) ≅ IC ( X ~ , ℚ ) ≅ ⊕ p ∈ P X ~ IC ( X p ~ , ℚ ) \IC(X,\mathbb{Q})\cong\IC(\widetilde{X},\mathbb{Q})\cong\oplus_{p\in P_{\tilde{X}}}\IC(\widetilde{X_{p}},\mathbb{Q}) , and so it is sufficient to prove the claim under the assumption that X X is irreducible. By results of Whitney [ Whi65 ] , Łojasiewicz [ Ło64 ] and Goresky [ Gor78 ] , there is a Whitney stratification of X X by complex subvarieties, along with a triangulation of X X respecting the stratification. The sum of the top degree simplices of this triangulation gives a spanning top degree class in intersection homology, i.e. a degree zero class in intersection cohomology, by Poincaré duality. This proves the claim. Then the first equality follows from the fact that ℳ v ζ ​ -st ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\stable}(Q) is a dense open subvariety of ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sstable}(Q) , and is irreducible, as it is a free quotient of an irreducible variety. The second equality follows from the fact that the degree of IC ⁡ ( ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) \IC\left(\mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sst}(Q),\mathbb{Q}\right) is bounded above by − 2 ​ ( χ Q ​ ( v , v ) − 1 ) -2(\upchi_{Q}(v,v)-1) , since by ( 81 ) this is the real dimension of ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sstable}(Q) . ∎ 6.2.2. Cohomological wall crossing The following “categorification” of the wall crossing formula from Donaldson–Thomas theory is a very special case of [ DM20 , Thm B] (see also [ FR18 ] and the proof of [ Dav18 , Thm 3.21] ). Theorem 6.3 . Let ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} be a stability condition (not necessarily generic). Then there is an isomorphism in Vect K + \Vect^{+}_{K} : (82) H ⁡ ( 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ≅ ⨂ ∞ → θ − ∞ tw 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) . \HO(\mathfrak{M}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\cong\bigotimes^{\tw}_{\infty\xrightarrow{\uptheta}-\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q). Since the monoidal product is not symmetric, it is important to note the order in which we take it (i.e., moving from left to right as the slope decreases). Also, in order to define the above infinite product, note that for each v ∈ K v\in K there are only finitely many ways of decomposing v = v 1 + … + v l v=v_{1}+\ldots+v_{l} into nonzero dimension vectors in K K . In addition, the v = 0 v=0 graded piece of every 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q) is equal to ℚ \mathbb{Q} , the one dimensional vector space concentrated in cohomological degree zero. This is the monoidal unit in Vect + \Vect^{+} . So we may write the v v -graded piece of the right-hand side of ( 82 ) as ⨁ v 1 , … ​ v l ≠ 0 v 1 + … + v l = v μ ζ ​ ( v 1 ) ≥ … ≥ μ ζ ​ ( v l ) 𝒜 v 1 ζ ( Q ) ⊗ tw ⋯ ⊗ tw 𝒜 v l ζ ( Q ) , \bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1},\ldots v_{l}\neq 0\\ v_{1}+\ldots+v_{l}=v\\ \upmu_{\upzeta}(v_{1})\geq\ldots\geq\upmu_{\upzeta}(v_{l})\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}^{\upzeta}_{v_{1}}(Q)\otimes^{\tw}\cdots\otimes^{\tw}\mathcal{A}^{\upzeta}_{v_{l}}(Q), which is, in particular, a cohomologically graded vector space which is finite-dimensional in every cohomological degree, as required. Applying χ K , t \upchi_{K,t} to both sides of ( 82 ), we deduce that there is an equality (83) χ K , t ​ ( H ⁡ ( 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ) = ∏ ∞ → θ − ∞ χ K , t ​ ( 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) ) ∈ ℬ ^ Q . \upchi_{K,t}\left(\HO(\mathfrak{M}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\right)=\prod_{\infty\xrightarrow{\uptheta}-\infty}\upchi_{K,t}(\mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q))\in\hat{\mathscr{B}}_{Q}. Corollary 6.4 . The cohomologically graded vector space 𝒜 v ζ \mathcal{A}_{v}^{\upzeta} is entirely concentrated in even or odd degrees, depending on whether χ Q ​ ( v , v ) \upchi_{Q}(v,v) is even or odd, respectively. Proof. Taking the monoidal unit in every term in the infinite tensor product in ( 82 ) except the term corresponding to θ \uptheta , there is an inclusion 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) ⊂ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir , \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q)\subset\HO(\mathfrak{M}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}, and so, since ( 82 ) respects the K K -grading, an inclusion 𝒜 v ζ ​ ( Q ) ⊂ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir \mathcal{A}_{v}^{\upzeta}(Q)\subset\HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir} for v ∈ S θ ζ v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta} . Now the result follows from the fact that H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) ≅ H ⁡ ( pt / GL v , ℚ ) \HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})\cong\HO(\pt/\GL_{v},\mathbb{Q}) is entirely concentrated in even degrees, and the dimension of 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q) is − χ Q ​ ( v , v ) -\upchi_{Q}(v,v) . ∎ Remark 6.5 . A more elementary proof of the vanishing of even cohomology of H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) \HO(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sst}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q}) avoiding e.g. the use of the decomposition theorem in [ DM20 ] has more recently been provided in [ FR18 , Thm 5.1] . 6.2.3. Integrality theorem The second main ingredient we use from cohomological DT theory is a version of the integrality conjecture. The conditions on ζ \upzeta for this theorem are slightly stronger than in Theorem 6.3 . Recall that if ζ \upzeta is θ \uptheta -generic, the twist in the twisted monoidal product becomes trivial on Vect θ ζ , + \Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} and the monoidal product on Vect θ ζ , + \Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} can be made symmetric, with symmetrizing morphism incorporating the Koszul sign rule with respect to the cohomological degree. In particular, under this genericity assumption, given V ∈ Vect θ ζ , + V\in\Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} we may form (84) 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ) = ⨁ n ≥ 0 𝕊 ​ ym n ⁡ ( V ) , \Sym(V)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Sym^{n}(V), the underlying graded object of the free super commutative algebra generated by V V . Recall that this is isomorphic to Sym ⁡ ( H even ⁡ ( V ) ) ⊗ ⋀ H odd ⁡ ( V ) , \CSym\left(\HO^{\textrm{even}}(V)\right)\otimes\bigwedge\HO^{\textrm{odd}}(V), the tensor product of the free commutative algebra generated by H even ⁡ ( V ) \HO^{\textrm{even}}(V) and the free exterior algebra generated by H odd ⁡ ( V ) \HO^{\textrm{odd}}(V) . We ignore the extra grading on this vector space determined by the decomposition on the right hand side of ( 84 ). The object 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( V ) \Sym(V) will again be an object of Vect θ ζ , + \Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} if V 0 = 0 V_{0}=0 . Theorem 6.6 . [ DM20 , Thm. A] Let ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} be a θ \uptheta -generic stability condition. There is an isomorphism in Vect θ ζ , + \Vect^{\upzeta,+}_{\uptheta} (85) 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) ≅ 𝕊 ​ ym ⁡ ( ⨁ 0 ≠ v ∈ S θ ζ BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ⊗ H ⁡ ( pt / ℂ ∗ , ℚ ) vir ) . \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q)\cong\Sym\left(\bigoplus_{0\neq v\in S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}}\BPS_{v}^{\upzeta}(Q)\otimes\HO(\pt/\mathbb{C}^{*},\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\right). Theorem 6.6 , along with ( 33 ), implies that χ K , t ​ ( 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) ) ∈ G Q qtr \upchi_{K,t}(\mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q))\in G^{\qtrop}_{Q} . Combining with ( 83 ) we deduce moreover that χ K , t ​ ( H ⁡ ( 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ) ∈ G Q qtr \upchi_{K,t}\left(\HO(\mathfrak{M}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\right)\in G^{\qtrop}_{Q} . Remark 6.7 . If Q Q is symmetric, in the sense that between any two distinct vertices i i and j j there are as many arrows from i i to j j as from j j to i i , then the degenerate stability condition ζ = ( 0 , … , 0 ) \upzeta=(0,\ldots,0) is generic, and there is an equality 𝒜 θ = 0 ζ ​ ( Q ) = H ⁡ ( 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir . \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta=0}^{\upzeta}(Q)=\HO(\mathfrak{M}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}. Theorem 6.6 gives a precise definition of V prim V_{\mathrm{prim}} in this case. 6.2.4. Examples We let J ( r ) \moLQ^{(r)} denote the quiver with 1 1 vertex and r r loops. The quiver J ( 1 ) \moLQ^{(1)} is known as the Jordan quiver. Whichever stability condition ζ ∈ ℝ \upzeta\in\mathbb{R} we choose, all ℂ ​ J ( r ) \mathbb{C}\mskip-3.0mu\moLQ^{(r)} -modules are semistable, and they are stable if and only if they are simple. Thus ζ \upzeta is not relevant, and we drop it from the notation when considering the quivers J ( r ) \moLQ^{(r)} . Example 6.8 . The variety ℳ 1 ​ ( J ( 0 ) ) = pt \mathcal{M}_{1}(\moLQ^{(0)})=\pt is smooth, and so IC ⁡ ( ℳ 1 ​ ( J ( 0 ) ) , ℚ ) = \displaystyle\IC(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\moLQ^{(0)}),\mathbb{Q})= H ⁡ ( ℳ 1 ​ ( J ( 0 ) ) , ℚ ) \displaystyle\HO\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\moLQ^{(0)}),\mathbb{Q}\right) = \displaystyle= ℚ . \displaystyle\mathbb{Q}. It will be a key ingredient in proving preservation of positivity in Corollary 7.8 . It generalizes the positivity statement of [ MR19 , Cor. 1.2] , which applies to the case in which Q Q is acyclic. The statement that the refined DT invariant is of Lefschetz type, or even bar-invariant, on the other hand, fails outside of the non-acyclic case. This is a result of the fact that the moduli spaces we consider outside of the acyclic case are not proper, and so their intersection cohomology does not satisfy Poincaré duality or the hard Lefschetz theorem. Proof. Combine ( 86 ) and Lemmas 6.2 and 6.13 . ∎ Combining Proposition 6.14 with ( 83 ), we deduce positivity for refined Donaldson–Thomas invariants. Theorem 6.15 (Positivity of DT invariants) . Refined Donaldson–Thomas theory In this section we recall some results from refined Donaldson–Thomas theory, culminating in the Meinhardt–Reineke result [ MR19 ] on the positivity and Lefschetz type property of refined DT invariants of acyclic quivers. As per the warnings at the beginning of § 6 , we will need to recall some extra definitions first. 6.3.1. Graded mixed Hodge structures Recall that a (rational) mixed Hodge structure is given by the data of • a finite-dimensional vector space V V over ℚ \mathbb{Q} • an ascending filtration W ∙ W_{\bullet} of V V • a descending filtration F ∙ F^{\bullet} of V ⊗ ℚ ℂ V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C} such that the filtration induced by F ∙ F^{\bullet} on W n ⊗ ℚ ℂ / W n − 1 ⊗ ℚ ℂ W_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/W_{n-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C} determines a pure Hodge structure of weight n n . A cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structure is a possibly infinite formal direct sum V = ⨁ i ∈ ℤ V i ​ [ − i ] V=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{i}[-i] with each V i V^{i} a rational mixed Hodge structure, i.e. a cohomologically graded object with V i V^{i} in cohomological degree i i . We then write H i ⁡ ( V ) = V i \HO^{i}(V)=V^{i} . We denote by MHS \MHS the category of rational mixed Hodge structures. We denote by MHS + \MHS^{+} the category of partially bounded cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structures, i.e. those satisfying • if n ≪ 0 n\ll 0 then Gr n W ⁡ ( H i ⁡ ( V ) ) = 0 \operatorname{Gr}^{W}_{n}(\HO^{i}(V))=0 for all i i • for all n ∈ ℤ n\in\mathbb{Z} the underlying vector space of ⨁ i ∈ ℤ Gr n W ⁡ ( H i ⁡ ( V ) ) \bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Gr}^{W}_{n}(\HO^{i}(V)) is finite-dimensional, and so it is naturally a pure weight n n Hodge structure. The category MHS + \MHS^{+} is a symmetric monoidal category, where the symmetrizing morphism incorporates the Koszul sign rule. We call an object of MHS + \MHS^{+} pure if Gr i W ⁡ ( H j ⁡ ( V ) ) = 0 \operatorname{Gr}^{W}_{i}(\HO^{j}(V))=0 for all i ≠ j i\neq j . We denote by 𝕃 \mathbb{L} the pure object of MHS + \MHS^{+} given by H c ⁡ ( 𝔸 1 , ℚ ) \HO_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathbb{Q}) . It is a one dimensional pure weight 2 2 Hodge structure concentrated in cohomological degree 2 2 . Given an element V ∈ MHS + V\in\MHS^{+} we define χ wt ​ ( V ) = ∑ i , n ∈ ℤ ( − 1 ) i ​ dim ℚ ( Gr n W ⁡ ( H i ⁡ ( V ) ) ) ​ ( − t ) n ∈ ℤ ⁡ ( ( t ) ) . \upchi_{\Wt}(V)=\sum_{i,n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{i}\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(\operatorname{Gr}^{W}_{n}(\HO^{i}(V)))(-t)^{n}\in\mathbb{Z}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu). The category MHS \MHS is a full subcategory of the category MMHS \MMHS of monodromic mixed Hodge structures defined in [ KS11 , Sec.7] , which we refer to for the facts about the extension of χ wt \upchi_{\Wt} recalled below. We define the category of partially bounded monodromic mixed Hodge structures MMHS + \MMHS^{+} via the same conditions as for MHS + \MHS^{+} . We make almost no use of this larger category, except for the following fact: there is a tensor square root 𝕃 1 / 2 \mathbb{L}^{1/2} for 𝕃 \mathbb{L} inside MMHS + \MMHS^{+} , and we denote by MHS ¯ + \overline{\MHS}^{+} the smallest full tensor subcategory of MMHS + \MMHS^{+} containing MHS + \MHS^{+} and this tensor square root. Since 𝕃 \mathbb{L} is concentrated in cohomological degree 2 2 , the object 𝕃 1 / 2 \mathbb{L}^{1/2} is concentrated in degree 1 1 . Alternatively, as in [ KS11 , Sec 3.4] , one can adjoin a formal tensor square root of 𝕃 \mathbb{L} to MHS + \MHS^{+} in order to define MHS ¯ + \overline{\MHS}^{+} . For the purposes of this paper, the only important thing to note is that the relation χ wt ​ ( V ⊗ V ′ ) = χ wt ​ ( V ) ​ χ wt ​ ( V ′ ) \upchi_{\Wt}(V\otimes V^{\prime})=\upchi_{\Wt}(V)\upchi_{\Wt}(V^{\prime}) extends to the Grothendieck ring of objects in MHS ¯ + \overline{\MHS}^{+} , and we have χ wt ​ ( 𝕃 1 / 2 ) = t . \upchi_{\Wt}(\mathbb{L}^{1/2})=t. Furthermore, if V ∈ MHS ¯ + V\in\overline{\MHS}^{+} is pure then χ wt ​ ( V ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ ( ( t ) ) \upchi_{\Wt}(V)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu) , and if V V is pure and carries a Lefschetz operator then χ wt ​ ( V ) \upchi_{\Wt}(V) is a Lefschetz type Laurent polynomial. We will slightly abuse notation and refer to objects in MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} as cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structures, despite the fact that MHS + \MHS^{+} is only a full subcategry of MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} . We say an object of MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} is of Tate type if each cohomologically graded piece is a direct sum of tensor powers of 𝕃 1 / 2 ​ [ 1 ] \mathbb{L}^{1/2}[1] . We denote by MHS ¯ K + \mMHS_{K}^{+} the category of formal K K -graded direct sums in MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} . We make this into a monoidal category by setting ( V ′ ⊗ tw V ′′ ) v = ⨁ v ′ + v ′′ = v V v ′ ′ ⊗ V v ′′ ′′ ⊗ 𝕃 B ⁡ ( v ′ , v ′′ ) / 2 . (V^{\prime}\otimes^{\tw}V^{\prime\prime})_{v}=\bigoplus_{v^{\prime}+v^{\prime\prime}=v}V^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\otimes V^{\prime\prime}_{v^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{L}^{B(v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})/2}. For V ∈ ob ⁡ ( MHS ¯ K + ) V\in\ob(\mMHS_{K}^{+}) we define χ wt , K ​ ( V ) = ∑ v ∈ K χ wt ​ ( V v ) ​ x v ∈ ℬ ^ Q . \upchi_{\Wt,K}(V)=\sum_{v\in K}\upchi_{\Wt}(V_{v})x^{v}\in\hat{\mathscr{B}}_{Q}. Then χ wt , K ​ ( V ′ ⊗ tw V ′′ ) = χ wt , K ​ ( V ′ ) ​ χ wt , K ​ ( V ′′ ) \upchi_{\Wt,K}(V^{\prime}\otimes^{\tw}V^{\prime\prime})=\upchi_{\Wt,K}(V^{\prime})\upchi_{\Wt,K}(V^{\prime\prime}) . For X X an irreducible finite type global quotient Artin stack, we define the cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structures H ⁡ ( X , ℚ ) vir = \displaystyle\HO(X,\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}= H ( X , ℚ ) ⊗ 𝕃 − dim ( X ) / 2 \displaystyle\HO(X,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathbb{L}^{-\dim(X)/2} H c ⁡ ( X , ℚ ) vir = \displaystyle\HO_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}= H c ( X , ℚ ) ⊗ 𝕃 − dim ( X ) / 2 . \displaystyle\HO_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathbb{L}^{-\dim(X)/2}. The latter is a cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structure which may have nontrivial pieces of weight n n for n ≪ 0 n\ll 0 , and so it is not an object of MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} . As such we will only consider its (cohomologically graded) dual H c ⁡ ( X , ℚ ) vir ∗ \HO_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}^{*} in the category of cohomologically graded mixed Hodge structures, which is an object of MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} . 6.3.2. Factorization and integrality We recall the following result of Reineke [ Rei03 , Sec. 6] . Theorem 6.16 . There is an equality of generating series χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ v ∈ K H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ ) = ∏ ∞ → θ − ∞ χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ v ∈ S θ ζ H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ ) . \upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})^{*}_{\vir}\right)=\prod_{\infty\xrightarrow{\uptheta}-\infty}\upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta}}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sstable}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}^{*}\right). Reineke’s original result was written in terms of compactly supported cohomology (not its dual), and so one should make the substitution t ↦ t − 1 t\mapsto t^{-1} to translate between his result and its statement above. Note that since all of the stacks 𝔐 \mathfrak{M} in the above expression are smooth, by Poincaré duality we have H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 , ℚ ) vir ∗ ≅ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 , ℚ ) vir , \HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{Q})^{*}_{\vir}\cong\HO(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}, and so we can rewrite Theorem 6.16 as χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ v ∈ K H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ) = ∏ ∞ → θ − ∞ χ wt , K ​ ( 𝒜 θ , Hdg ζ ​ ( Q ) ) , \upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\right)=\prod_{\infty\xrightarrow{\uptheta}-\infty}\upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta,\Hodge}(Q)\right), where 𝒜 θ , Hdg ζ ​ ( Q ) ≔ ⨁ v ∈ S θ ζ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir \mathcal{A}^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta,\Hodge}(Q)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta}}\HO(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sstable}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir} is the Hodge-theoretic upgrade of 𝒜 θ ζ ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}(Q) from § 6.2 . Later we will use a slight variant of this result. The proof is the same, following essentially from the existence of the Harder–Narasimhan stratification of 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}(Q) and the long exact sequence in compactly supported cohomology. Proposition 6.17 . Let I = [ a , b ] ⊂ ( − ∞ , ∞ ) I=[a,b]\subset(-\infty,\infty) be an interval, and denote by 𝔐 I , v ζ ​ ( Q ) ⊂ 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta}_{I,v}(Q)\subset\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q) the open substack of modules ρ \rho such that the slope of every subquotient in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho has slope contained in I I . Integrality The main result of [ MR19 ] establishes that if the stability condition is generic, each term in the right hand side of ( 88 ) is the plethystic exponential of a more manageable power series. Denote by x ¯ \overline{x} the set { x 1 i } i ∈ Q 0 \{x^{1_{i}}\}_{i\in Q_{0}} of degree one monomials in ℬ ^ Q \hat{\mathscr{B}}_{Q} . To state their result in something close to its original form, for ζ \upzeta a θ \uptheta -generic stability condition, we use the isomorphism ( 24 ) to define elements Ω Q , v ζ ∈ ℤ ⁡ ( ( t ) ) \Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta}\in\mathbb{Z}(\mskip-3.0mu(t)\mskip-3.0mu) via ∑ 0 ≠ v ∈ S θ ζ Ω Q , v ζ ​ x v = ( t − 1 − t ) ​ Log − t , x ¯ ⁡ ( χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ v ∈ S θ ζ H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ ) ) \sum_{0\neq v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta}}\Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta}x^{v}=(t^{-1}-t)\Log_{-t,\overline{x}}\left(\upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta}}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sstable}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}^{*}\right)\right) so that we have the equation (89) χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ v ∈ S θ ζ H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ ) = 𝔼 ⁡ ( ∑ 0 ≠ v ∈ S θ ζ Ω Q , v ζ ​ x v ) . \upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta}}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sstable}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}^{*}\right)=\EE\left(\sum_{0\neq v\in S^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta}}\Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta}x^{v}\right). These Ω Q , v ζ \Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta} are, by definition, the refined DT invariants. We define BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ≔ { IC ⁡ ( ℳ v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ¯ sm ) ⊗ 𝕃 ( χ Q ​ ( v , v ) − 1 ) / 2 if ​ ℳ v ζ ​ -st ≠ ∅ 0 otherwise , \BPS^{\upzeta}_{v,\Hodge}(Q)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\IC(\mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sst}(Q),\underline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\textrm{sm}})\otimes\mathbb{L}^{(\upchi_{Q}(v,v)-1)/2}&\textrm{if }\mathcal{M}^{\upzeta\stable}_{v}\neq\emptyset\\ 0&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases} where on the right we have taken the total hypercohomology of the intersection complex for the constant (shifted) mixed Hodge module ℚ ¯ sm \underline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\textrm{sm}} on the smooth locus of ℳ v ζ ​ -sst \mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sstable} . [ MR19 ] Assume that ζ \upzeta is θ \uptheta -generic. Then there is an equality Ω Q , v ζ = χ wt ​ ( BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) . \Omega^{\upzeta}_{Q,v}=\upchi_{\Wt}(\BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q)). In particular, Ω Q , v ζ ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] \Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta}\in\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}] . As in the statement of Theorem 6.16 we are working with the graded duals of the cohomology groups considered by Meinhardt and Reineke. In addition, they consider the compactly supported cohomology with coefficients in the IC complex to define BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) \BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q) , whereas we have taken cohomology. These two differences cancel out, by Poincaré duality for intersection cohomology groups. Example 6.19 . For all of the quivers Q Q considered in Examples 6.8 , 6.9 and 6.10 from § 6.2 , it is easy to verify directly that BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) \BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q) is pure, so that χ wt ​ ( BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) = χ t ​ ( BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) \upchi_{\Wt}(\BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q))=\upchi_{t}(\BPS_{v}^{\upzeta}(Q)) . The following corollary of Theorem 6.18 is [ MR19 , Cor 1.2] . It follows from purity and the existence of a Lefschetz operator on IC ⁡ ( X , ℚ ¯ sm ) \IC(X,\underline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\textrm{sm}}) for X X a projective variety. The part of the statement concerning parity is a consequence of the fact that Ω Q , v ζ \Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta} arises from a formal series in the class [ 𝕃 1 / 2 ] [\mathbb{L}^{1/2}] in the Grothendieck ring of mixed Hodge structures, but on the other hand IC ⁡ ( ℳ v ζ ​ -sst , ℚ ¯ sm ) ∈ MHS + \IC(\mathcal{M}_{v}^{\upzeta\sstable},\underline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\textrm{sm}})\in\MHS^{+} , a category in which 𝕃 \mathbb{L} has no square root. Corollary 6.20 . [ MR19 ] Let Q Q be acyclic, and let ζ \upzeta be θ \uptheta -generic, where μ ⁡ ( v ) = θ \upmu(v)=\uptheta . Then Ω Q , v ζ \Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta} is of Lefschetz type and is concentrated in even or odd degrees, depending on whether χ Q ​ ( v , v ) \upchi_{Q}(v,v) is odd or even, respectively. In particular, Ω Q , v ζ \Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta} has positive integer coefficients. 6.3.4. Purity In this section we explain how it comes about that both versions of Donaldson–Thomas theory that we have presented can produce the same generating functions. This coincidence is a byproduct of purity . Note that for pure objects of MHS ¯ + \mMHS^{+} there is an equality χ wt ​ ( V ) = χ t ​ ( V ) . \upchi_{\Wt}(V)=\upchi_{t}(V). Purity for cohomological DT invariants of quivers (possibly containing oriented cycles) without potential is in fact a direct consequence of the main theorems of [ DM20 ] ; we briefly explain how. Proposition 6.21 . The object 𝒜 θ , Hdg ζ ​ ( Q ) \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q) is pure of Tate type, and is moreover concentrated entirely in even or odd degrees, depending on whether χ Q ​ ( v , v ) \upchi_{Q}(v,v) is even or odd, respectively. Proof. Theorem B in [ DM20 ] gives the following Hodge theoretic upgrade of ( 82 ): ⨁ v ∈ K H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ≅ ⨂ ∞ → θ − ∞ tw 𝒜 θ , Hdg ζ ​ ( Q ) . \bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\cong\bigotimes^{\tw}_{\infty\xrightarrow{\uptheta}-\infty}\mathcal{A}^{\upzeta}_{\uptheta,\Hodge}(Q). In particular there is an embedding 𝒜 θ , Hdg ζ ​ ( Q ) ⊂ ⨁ v ∈ S θ ζ H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) ⊗ 𝕃 χ Q ​ ( v , v ) / 2 . \mathcal{A}_{\uptheta,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q)\subset\bigoplus_{v\in S_{\uptheta}^{\upzeta}}\HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})\otimes\mathbb{L}^{\upchi_{Q}(v,v)/2}. Now the result follows from the fact that H ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) ≅ H ⁡ ( pt / GL v , ℚ ) \HO(\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})\cong\HO(\pt/\GL_{v},\mathbb{Q}) is pure, of Tate type, and concentrated in even degrees. ∎ Proposition 6.22 . Let ζ \upzeta be a θ \uptheta -generic stability condition. Then the mixed Hodge structure BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) \BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q) is pure, of Tate type, and concentrated entirely in even or odd degrees depending on whether χ Q ​ ( v , v ) \upchi_{Q}(v,v) is odd or even, respectively. Proof. Theorem A in [ DM20 ] upgrades ( 85 ) to the category of mixed Hodge structures. In particular, there is an inclusion BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ⊗ 𝕃 1 / 2 ⊂ 𝒜 v , Hdg ζ ​ ( Q ) . \BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q)\otimes\mathbb{L}^{1/2}\subset\mathcal{A}_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q). A sub mixed Hodge structure of a pure Tate type Hodge structure is pure of Tate type, and the result follows from Proposition 6.21 . ∎ In particular, it follows that for any Q Q , and for ζ \upzeta a θ \uptheta -generic stability condition with μ ⁡ ( v ) = θ \upmu(v)=\uptheta , there are equalities. χ t ​ ( BPS v ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) = χ wt ​ ( BPS v , Hdg ζ ⁡ ( Q ) ) = Ω Q , v ζ . \upchi_{t}(\BPS_{v}^{\upzeta}(Q))=\upchi_{\Wt}(\BPS_{v,\Hodge}^{\upzeta}(Q))=\Omega_{Q,v}^{\upzeta}. 7. Stability scattering diagrams and the basic two-wall cases 7.1. Stability scattering diagrams In this section we recall the stability scattering diagrams of [ Bri17 ] . Since that paper is written in terms of Q Q -representations, or equivalently left ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules, when passing from our right ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules to Bridgeland’s representations, one should replace Q Q with Q op Q^{\opp} . Given ζ ∈ ℝ Q 0 \upzeta\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} , a ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho is said to be ζ \upzeta King-semistable if ζ ⋅ dim ¯ ( ρ ) = 0 \upzeta\cdot\udim(\rho)=0 , and for every proper nonzero submodule ρ ′ ⊂ ρ \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho we have ζ ⋅ dim ¯ ( ρ ′ ) ≤ 0 \upzeta\cdot\udim(\rho^{\prime})\leq 0 . We let 𝔐 v ζ ​ -Kss ​ ( Q ) ⊂ 𝔐 v ​ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\kss}_{v}(Q)\subset\mathfrak{M}_{v}(Q) denote the open substack of ζ \upzeta King-semistable ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules. Following the alternative scattering diagram convention mentioned in Footnote 4 , it is shown in [ Bri17 ] that there is a consistent scattering diagram 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} in ℝ Q 0 \mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} over a Lie algebra 𝔤 Hall \mathfrak{g}_{\Hall} such that the support of 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} is precisely the set of ζ \upzeta for which there exists a ζ \upzeta King-semistable ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module, and functions at general points ζ \upzeta of the walls are elements 1 ss ​ ( ζ ) 1_{\mathrm{ss}}(\upzeta) which lie in the pro-unipotent algebraic group G ^ Hall \hat{G}_{\Hall} . This group is defined to be a subgroup of the group of units of the (completed) motivic Hall algebra of the category of ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -modules. We refer the reader to [ Bri17 , §5–6] for the original treatment of 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} and for the definition of G ^ Hall \hat{G}_{\Hall} . Denoting B 1 : ℝ Q 0 → ( ℝ Q 0 ) ∗ = ℝ Q 0 B_{1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}})^{*}=\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} (identifying ( ℝ Q 0 ) ∗ (\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}})^{*} with ℝ Q 0 \mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} using the dot product) for B 1 ​ ( u ) ≔ B ⁡ ( u , ⋅ ) B_{1}(u)\coloneqq B(u,\cdot) with B B as in ( 77 ), we view 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} as a scattering diagram under our conventions by applying B 1 − 1 B_{1}^{-1} to the support of each wall — i.e., for us, the scattering function of 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} at a general point u ∈ ℝ Q 0 u\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} is 1 ss ​ ( B 1 ​ ( u ) ) 1_{\mathrm{ss}}(B_{1}(u)) . Here we must assume that B ≠ 0 B\neq 0 since otherwise 1 ss ​ ( B 1 ​ ( u ) ) = 𝔐 ⁡ ( Q ) 1_{\mathrm{ss}}(B_{1}(u))=\mathfrak{M}(Q) for all u ∈ ℝ Q 0 u\in\mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} . For B ≠ 0 B\neq 0 , we have codim ⁡ ( ker ⁡ ( B ) ) ≥ 2 \codim(\ker(B))\geq 2 , and then no general points will lie in ker ⁡ ( B ) \ker(B) , so we get a well-defined scattering diagram. We forego recalling the details of the definition of G ^ Hall \hat{G}_{\Hall} ourselves, instead remarking that by [ Joy07 , Thm 6.1] there is a group homomorphism ∫ : G ^ Hall → ℬ ^ Q × \int\colon\hat{G}_{\Hall}\rightarrow\hat{\mathscr{B}}_{Q}^{\times} where, by definition, there is an identity (94) ∫ 1 ss ​ ( ζ ) = χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ v ∈ K H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -Kss ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ ) . \displaystyle\int 1_{\mathrm{ss}}(\upzeta)=\upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\kss}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}^{*}\right). By the definition of King semistability, there is an equality ⨁ v ∈ K H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -Kss ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir = ⨁ v ∈ S 0 ζ H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -sst ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir , \bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\kss}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}=\bigoplus_{v\in S_{0}^{\upzeta}}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\sstable}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}, i.e. the notion of King semistability is the same as the one used throughout the paper, but with the added requirement that the slope is zero. Let log ∫ : 𝔤 ^ Hall → 𝔤 ^ Q \log\int\colon\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Hall}\rightarrow\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{Q} denote the morphism of Lie algebras associated to ∫ \int . We define the stability scattering diagram to be the scattering diagram 𝔇 Stab \mathfrak{D}_{\Stab} in ℝ Q 0 \mathbb{R}^{Q_{0}} over 𝔤 Q \mathfrak{g}_{Q} given by 𝔇 Stab ≔ [ ( log ∫ ) ∗ 𝔇 Hall ] − \displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\Stab}\coloneqq\left[\left(\log\int\right)_{*}\mathfrak{D}_{\Hall}\right]^{-} where ( log ∫ ) ∗ (\log\int)_{*} is defined as at the start of § 2.3.3 , and ( ∙ ) − (\bullet)^{-} is defined as in Corollary 2.10 . Remark 7.1 . As noted above, in translating between this paper and [ Bri17 ] one should replace Q Q with Q op Q^{\opp} . The result is that the skew-symmetric form ⟨ ⋅ , ⋅ ⟩ \langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle of [ Bri17 ] and the skew-symmetric form B Q ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) B_{Q}(\cdot,\cdot) of this paper differ by a sign. For generic ζ \upzeta satisfying ζ ⋅ 1 i = 0 \upzeta\cdot 1_{i}=0 , we have ⨁ v ∈ K H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -Kss ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ = ⨁ n ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 n ​ 1 i ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ . \bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\kss}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})^{*}_{\vir}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}_{n1_{i}}(Q),\mathbb{Q})^{*}_{\vir}. In particular, for each i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} there is an incoming wall (95) ( 1 i B ⟂ , F i ) ; F i = χ wt , K ​ ( ⨁ n ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 n ​ 1 i ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ∗ ) − 1 (1_{i}^{B\perp},F_{i});\quad\quad F_{i}=\upchi_{\Wt,K}\left(\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}_{n1_{i}}(Q),\mathbb{Q})^{*}_{\vir}\right)^{-1} in 𝔇 Stab \mathfrak{D}_{\Stab} (assuming 1 i ∉ ker ⁡ ( B ) 1_{i}\notin\ker(B) — otherwise our version of 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} has no wall associated to 1 i 1_{i} ). Example 7.2 . Let i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} satisfy 1 i ∉ ker ⁡ ( B 1 ) 1_{i}\notin\ker(B_{1}) . If the vertex i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} does not support a loop, then by Example 6.8 or ( 90 ), the corresponding incoming wall of 𝔇 Stab \mathfrak{D}_{\Stab} is ( 1 i B ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − x i ) ) . \displaystyle(1_{i}^{B\perp},\EE(-x_{i})). Similarly, if there is a single loop based at i i , then by Example 6.9 or ( 91 ), the corresponding incoming wall is ( 1 i B ⟂ , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t − 1 ​ x i ) ) . \displaystyle(1_{i}^{B\perp},\EE(-t^{-1}x_{i})). The reason we consider quivers with loops is precisely because they allow us to consider scattering functions 𝔼 ⁡ ( − p ⁡ ( t ) ​ x ) \EE(-p(t)x) where p ⁡ ( t ) p(t) has odd parity. Definition 7.3 . A quiver Q Q is called genteel if, up to equivalence, the only incoming walls of 𝔇 Stab \mathfrak{D}_{\Stab} are those of ( 95 ) for i ∈ Q 0 i\in Q_{0} . A ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho is called self-(semi)stable if it is King-(semi)stable for the stability condition B ⁡ ( dim ¯ ( ρ ) , ⋅ ) B(\udim(\rho),\cdot) . In [ Bri17 , Def. 11.3] , a quiver (with potential) is called genteel if the only self-stable modules are supported at a single vertex. As pointed out to us by Lang Mou and recently acknowledged in [ Bri17 , arXiv v4] , it is not clear that this implies the condition of Definition 7.3 . On the other hand, if every self- semi stable object is supported at a single vertex, then the condition of Definition 7.3 in fact follows easily. Unfortunately, this self-semistable version of the definition turns out to be too strong, e.g., Lemma 7.5 would fail (as we shall see in the lemma’s proof). Hence our decision to modify the definition as above. Example 7.4 . Each of the quivers J ( r ) \moLQ^{(r)} are genteel. Indeed, J ( r ) \moLQ^{(r)} has only one vertex, so the genteel condition is trivially satisfied. In fact we can generalise this: Lemma 7.5 . Let Q Q be a quiver. Then Q Q is genteel if and only if every cycle in Q Q is composed of loops. The proof is partially motivated by that of [ Bri17 , Lem 11.5] , which covers one of the implications in the case in which Q Q is acyclic, but for the self-stable version of genteelness. Also cf. [ Mou , Cor. 1.2(i)] and [ Qin , Thm. 1.2.2] for genteelness results in the quantum and classical cases, respectively, for quivers Q Q without loops and with a green-to-red sequence and non-degenerate potential. Proof. Let a n ⋯ a 1 a_{n}\cdots a_{1} be a cycle in Q Q , and let P ⊂ Q 0 P\subset Q_{0} be the set of vertices occurring as s ⁡ ( a l ) s(a_{l}) or t ⁡ ( a l ) t(a_{l}) for 1 ≤ l ≤ n 1\leq l\leq n . Set N = | P | N=\lvert P\lvert . Then consider any N N -dimensional ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho , with dimension vector v v satisfying v i = { 1 if ​ i ∈ P 0 otherwise v_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if }i\in P\\ 0&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases} and for which the action of each a 1 , … , a n a_{1},\ldots,a_{n} is an isomorphism. Clearly this module is simple, and so in particular it is King-stable for any stability condition in dim ¯ ( ρ ) ⟂ \udim(\rho)^{\perp} . Let ζ ∈ dim ¯ ( ρ ) ⟂ \upzeta\in\udim(\rho)^{\perp} be a general stability condition. Then ρ \rho is ζ \upzeta -stable, and so 𝔐 v ζ ​ -Kss ​ ( Q ) \mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\kss}_{v}(Q) is nonempty. The top degree compactly supported cohomology of this stack has, as a basis, the set of top-dimensional irreducible components; since the stack is irreducible, this cohomology is one-dimensional, and in particular nontrivial. It follows that ⨁ v ∈ K H c ⁡ ( 𝔐 v ζ ​ -Kss ​ ( Q ) , ℚ ) vir ≠ 0 \bigoplus_{v\in K}\HO_{c}(\mathfrak{M}^{\upzeta\kss}_{v}(Q),\mathbb{Q})_{\vir}\neq 0 and so by Proposition 6.21 ∫ 1 ss ​ ( ζ ) ≠ 0 . \int 1_{\mathrm{ss}}(\upzeta)\neq 0. It follows that dim ¯ ( ρ ) ⟂ \udim(\rho)^{\perp} is covered by walls of 𝔇 Stab \mathfrak{D}_{\Stab} , and in particular contains an incoming wall. So if there exists a cycle as above with N > 1 N>1 , then Q Q is not genteel. On the other hand, assume that Q Q contains no cycles that are not entirely composed of loops. Equivalently, we can label the vertices Q 0 Q_{0} with numbers { 1 , … , r } \{1,\ldots,r\} in such a way that for i > j i>j there are no arrows from i i to j j . Given a ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module ρ \rho , let Q 0 ρ Q_{0}^{\rho} denote the vertices of Q Q which lie in the support of ρ \rho , and let Q ρ Q^{\rho} be the subquiver of Q Q consisting of these vertices and all arrows between them. Given any connected component Q ′ Q^{\prime} of Q ρ Q^{\rho} , let α ∈ { 1 , … , r } \upalpha\in\{1,\ldots,r\} be the minimal number for which ρ \rho has non-trivial support ρ α \rho_{\upalpha} at the vertex α \upalpha . We extend ρ α \rho_{\upalpha} by zero to obtain a submodule ρ ′ ⊂ ρ \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho . Then B ⁡ ( dim ¯ ( ρ ) , dim ¯ ( ρ ′ ) ) ≥ 0 B(\udim(\rho),\udim(\rho^{\prime}))\geq 0 , with equality if and only if α \upalpha is the only vertex of Q ′ Q^{\prime} . Thus, ρ \rho cannot be self-semistable unless every component of Q ρ Q^{\rho} has only a single vertex (possibly with loops). So if ρ \rho is self-semistable, it must be a direct sum of representations which are supported on disjoint vertices. Suppose ρ \rho is semistable for some other stability condition ζ \upzeta . Then each summand ρ α \rho_{\upalpha} of ρ \rho must be ζ \upzeta -semistable, thus forcing ζ \upzeta to be in dim ¯ ( ρ α ) ⟂ \udim(\rho_{\upalpha})^{\perp} for each ρ α \rho_{\upalpha} . So the locus of such ζ \upzeta lives in codimension at least two (and thus does not form a wall) unless ρ \rho is supported at a single vertex, as desired. ∎ One may consider a possibly stronger notion of genteelness which requires that the only incoming walls of 𝔇 Hall \mathfrak{D}_{\Hall} (as opposed to 𝔇 Stab \mathfrak{D}_{\Stab} ) are ( 1 i B ⟂ , F i ) (1_{i}^{B\perp},F_{i}) with F i − 1 = 1 ss ​ ( B 1 ​ ( 1 i ) ) , F_{i}^{-1}=1_{\mathrm{ss}}(B_{1}(1_{i})), i.e., without application of χ wt , K \upchi_{\Wt,K} . We note that Lemma 7.5 holds for this stronger notion as well by the same proof. 7.2. Positivity and parity proofs As usual, let L 0 L_{0} be a lattice with a skew-symmetric integer valued form ω ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \omega(\cdot,\cdot) , let σ \upsigma be a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone in L 0 , ℝ L_{0,\mathbb{R}} , and denote L 0 ⊕ ≔ L 0 ∩ σ L_{0}^{\oplus}\coloneqq L_{0}\cap\upsigma and L 0 + ≔ L 0 ⊕ ∖ { 0 } L_{0}^{+}\coloneqq L_{0}^{\oplus}\setminus\{0\} . Let ( V , P ) (V,P) be a pair consisting of a tuple of elements V = ( v 1 , … , v s ) V=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}) of L 0 + L_{0}^{+} , and nonzero Laurent polynomials P = ( p 1 ​ ( t ) , … , p s ​ ( t ) ) P=(p_{1}(t),\ldots,p_{s}(t)) with each p i ​ ( t ) ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 ​ [ t ± 1 ] p_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[t^{\pm 1}] . We will assume that there is some 1 ≤ s ′ ≤ s 1\leq s^{\prime}\leq s such that p 1 ​ ( t ) , … , p s ′ ​ ( t ) p_{1}(t),\ldots,p_{s^{\prime}}(t) are even, and p s ′ + 1 ​ ( t ) , … , p s ​ ( t ) p_{s^{\prime}+1}(t),\ldots,p_{s}(t) are odd. We define a quiver Q = Q ⁡ ( V , P ) Q=Q(V,P) depending on this data as follows. First define Q 0 = { ( i , α ) : i ∈ { 1 , … , s } ​ and ​ α ∈ { 1 , … , p i ​ ( 1 ) } } . Q_{0}=\{(i,\upalpha)\colon i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}\textrm{ and }\upalpha\in\{1,\ldots,p_{i}(1)\}\}. For each pair of vertices ( i , α ) , ( j , β ) ∈ Q 0 (i,\upalpha),(j,\upbeta)\in Q_{0} with ( i , α ) ≠ ( j , β ) (i,\upalpha)\neq(j,\upbeta) , we add max ⁡ ( 0 , − ω ⁡ ( v i , v j ) ) \max(0,-\omega(v_{i},v_{j})) arrows with source ( i , α ) (i,\upalpha) and target ( j , β ) (j,\upbeta) . Finally, we add a loop to each ( i , α ) (i,\upalpha) with i > s ′ i>s^{\prime} . The following is a consequence of Lemma 7.5 . Corollary 7.6 . Let Q ⁡ ( V ) Q(V) be the quiver with vertices Q ​ ( V ) 0 = V = { v 1 , … , v s } Q(V)_{0}=V=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\} and max ⁡ ( 0 , − ω ⁡ ( v i , v j ) ) \max(0,-\omega(v_{i},v_{j})) arrows from v i v_{i} to v j v_{j} for all i , j ≤ s i,j\leq s . Then Q ⁡ ( V , P ) \moBQ(V,P) is genteel if and only if Q ⁡ ( V ) Q(V) is acyclic. In particular, if s = 2 s=2 then Q ⁡ ( V , P ) \moBQ(V,P) is genteel. Proof. Note that Q ⁡ ( V ) Q(V) is isomorphic to Q ⁡ ( V , ( 1 , … , 1 ) ) Q(V,(1,\ldots,1)) . It is straightforward to see that Q ⁡ ( V ) Q(V) being acyclic is equivalent to the condition that the only oriented cycles of Q ⁡ ( V , P ) Q(V,P) are composed of loops. The claim then follows from Lemma 7.5 . ∎ Proposition 7.7 . Example 7.10 (Density of walls for Kronecker quivers) . We now consider a situation like that of [ GHKK18 , Ex. 1.15] . I.e., consider the two-dimensional scattering diagram Scat ⁡ ( 𝔇 in ) \scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\In}) for 𝔇 in = { ( v i Λ ⟂ , 𝔼 ( − z v i ) ) : i = 1 , 2 } \mathfrak{D}_{\In}=\{(v_{i}^{\Uplambda\perp},\EE(-z^{v_{i}}))\colon i=1,2\} with Λ ⁡ ( v 1 , v 2 ) = n \Uplambda(v_{1},v_{2})=n , i.e., the scattering diagram associated to the n n -Kronecker quiver Kr ( n ) \moKr^{(n)} . For convenience, take v 1 = ( 0 , 1 ) v_{1}=(0,1) , v 2 = ( − 1 , 0 ) v_{2}=(-1,0) . It follows from the mutation invariance of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} (Proposition A.1 ) that 𝔇 ∖ 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\In} is preserved by the action of μ ≔ ( 0 − 1 1 n ) \upmu\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}0&-1\\ 1&n\end{matrix}\right) . For n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , it follows that the rays of the cluster complex accumulate along the eigenrays of μ \upmu generated by ( 1 , − λ ) (1,-\lambda) for λ = n ± n 2 − 4 2 \lambda=\frac{n\pm\sqrt{n^{2}-4}}{2} . Let σ bad \upsigma_{\bad} denote the interior of the cone generated by these two rays. It has often been suggested (e.g., in [ GPS10 , Ex. 1.6] , [ GP10 , Ex. 1.4] and [ GHKK18 , Ex. 1.15] ) that every ray of rational slope in σ bad \upsigma_{\bad} appears to support a nontrivial wall of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} . Using Example 7.9 , we can see that this is indeed true. 𝔼 ⁡ ( − z v 2 ) \EE(-z^{v_{2}}) 𝔼 ⁡ ( − z v 1 ) \EE(-z^{v_{1}}) σ bad \upsigma_{\bad} Figure 7.2. Sketch of the scattering diagram for the n n -Kronecker quiver Kr ( n ) \moKr^{(n)} , n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 . It suffices to check this for the classical version of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} . In this case, we can apply the change of lattice trick of [ GHKK18 , §C.3, Step IV] to replace our initial walls with the pair of walls lim t → 1 𝔼 ⁡ ( − nz u 1 ) \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\EE(-nz^{u_{1}}) and lim t → 1 𝔼 ⁡ ( − nz u 2 ) \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\EE(-nz^{u_{2}}) with Λ ⁡ ( u 1 , u 2 ) = 1 \Uplambda(u_{1},u_{2})=1 . Then the interaction of the 𝔡 i ​ 1 \mathfrak{d}_{i1} ’s produces a limiting ray with function lim t → 1 𝔼 ⁡ ( − ( t + t − 1 ) ​ z u 1 + u 2 ) \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\EE(-(t+t^{-1})z^{u_{1}+u_{2}}) (this follows from, say, Example 6.10 ). When this interacts with lim t → 1 𝔼 ⁡ ( − z u i ) \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\EE(-z^{u_{i}}) , Example 7.9 implies that the resulting outgoing rays include every ray of rational slope in the cone spanned by u 1 + u 2 u_{1}+u_{2} and u 1 + u 2 + u i u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{i} . This shows that every ray of rational slope in the cone C ≔ ℝ ≥ 0 ​ ⟨ ( n − 1 , − 1 ) , ( 1 , 1 − n ) ⟩ C\coloneqq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle(n-1,-1),(1,1-n)\rangle has non-trivial attached scattering function. Finally, noting that μ ⁡ ( ( , , , ) ) = ( 1 , 1 − n ) \upmu((1,-1))=(1,1-n) , and so C C overlaps μ ⁡ ( C ) \upmu(C) , we see that the cones μ k ​ ( C ) \upmu^{k}(C) for k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z} completely cover the interior of σ bad \upsigma_{\bad} , and the claim follows. In fact, we can see a bit more than density of the walls — we see that z v z^{v} has a nonzero-coefficient along ℝ ≥ 0 ​ ( − v ) \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(-v) for every nonzero v ∈ − σ bad v\in-\upsigma_{\bad} . Indeed, using the above arguments, the analogous statement about z ( α , β ) z^{(\upalpha,\upbeta)} having nonzero coefficients for all 0 < α < β 0<\upalpha<\upbeta in Example 7.9 implies the claim for − v ∈ ℝ > 0 ​ ⟨ ( 1 , − 1 ) , ( 1 , 1 − n ) ⟩ -v\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\langle(1,-1),(1,1-n)\rangle . We extend this to include the ray ℝ > 0 ​ ( 1 , − 1 ) \mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-1) using Reineke’s description of this ray [ Rei11 , Thm. 6.4] (cf. [ GHKK18 , Ex. 1.15] ). Then applying μ k \upmu^{k} for all k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z} as before yields the claim for the full region. In terms of quiver moduli, this can be viewed as saying that there exist stable representations of Kr ( n ) \moKr^{(n)} ( n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 ) of dimension vector v v for all v ∈ ℝ > 0 ​ ⟨ ( 1 , n − n 2 − 4 2 ) , ( 1 , n + n 2 − 4 2 ) ⟩ ∩ ℤ ≥ 0 2 v\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\langle(1,\frac{n-\sqrt{n^{2}-4}}{2}),(1,\frac{n+\sqrt{n^{2}-4}}{2})\rangle\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2} . Remark 7.11 . Via Theorem 6.18 and Lemma 6.2 , the statement above regarding nonvanishing of the coefficients for z ( α , β ) z^{(\upalpha,\upbeta)} is equivalent to the existence of stable modules of dimension vector ( α , β ) (\upalpha,\upbeta) . So rather than using the above example to deduce the existence of such stable modules, one could hope to directly show the existence of the stable modules, and then deduce the nonvanishing of the coefficients from this. Indeed, as pointed out to us by Pierrick Bousseau, there is an argument taking as its inputs only results from the quiver literature [ Kac80 ] and [ Sch90 ] which demonstrates the existence of such stable modules, without invoking any DT theory or scattering diagram techniques. Putting the above examples together, we see that if | ω ( v 1 , v 2 ) | ≠ 0 \lvert\omega(v_{1},v_{2})\lvert\neq 0 in Corollary 7.8 , the only two choices of ( n , m 1 , m 2 ) (n,m_{1},m_{2}) that do not give rise to regions in L ℝ L_{\mathbb{R}} of nonzero measure in which walls of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} are dense are ( 1 , 0 , 0 ) (1,0,0) and ( 2 , 0 , 0 ) (2,0,0) . 7.2.3. Let ρ \rho be a ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module, with dim ( ρ ) ≤ k \dim(\rho)\leq k , satisfying the condition that each of the subquotients appearing in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho with respect to the stability condition ζ \upzeta has slope contained in I ≔ [ − δ , δ ] I\coloneqq[-\updelta,\updelta] . Then in particular, ρ \rho has slope contained in [ − δ , δ ] [-\updelta,\updelta] . It follows that if Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} is isomorphic to the Kronecker quiver, the restriction of the dimension vector of ρ \rho to Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} must be ( a , a ) (a,a) for some a ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . Then the contribution of Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} to the slope of ρ \rho is zero, whichever quiver Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} is, and so if Q ( 1 ) Q^{(1)} is isomorphic to the Kronecker quiver, the restriction of the dimension vector of ρ \rho to Q ( 1 ) Q^{(1)} must be of the form ( b , b ) (b,b) for some b ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} . Let ρ \rho be a ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module with dim ( ρ ) ≤ k \dim(\rho)\leq k , and with the dimension vector of ρ \rho constant after restriction to Q ( 1 ) Q^{(1)} , and also constant after restriction to Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} . We claim that the following are equivalent (1) All of the subquotients appearing in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho with respect to ζ ⁡ ( − ) \upzeta(-) have slope in I I (2) The restrictions ρ ( i ) \rho^{(i)} of ρ \rho to Q ( i ) Q^{(i)} are ζ ⁡ ( + ) \upzeta(+) semistable for i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 (3) All of the subquotients appearing in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho with respect to ζ ⁡ ( + ) \upzeta(+) have slope in I I . ( 1 ) ⇔ ( 2 ) (1)\Leftrightarrow(2) The equivalence is trivial if one of ρ ( 1 ) \rho^{(1)} or ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} is trivial, so assume that they are both nontrivial. So ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} is a proper nontrivial submodule of ρ \rho , and destabilizes it (for the stability condition ζ ⁡ ( − ) \upzeta(-) ). In particular, picking ρ ′ ⊂ ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho^{(2)} to be the maximal submodule to attain the maximal slope of a submodule of ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} , ρ ′ \rho^{\prime} is the maximal destabilizing submodule of ρ \rho and it has slope in I I if and only if ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} is semistable (with respect to both stability conditions). Similarly, the maximal destabilizing quotient of ρ \rho with respect to ζ ⁡ ( − ) \upzeta(-) is a quotient of ρ ( 1 ) \rho^{(1)} , and it has slope in I I if and only if ρ ( 1 ) \rho^{(1)} is semistable with respect to both stability conditions. ( 3 ) ⇒ ( 2 ) (3)\Rightarrow(2) If ρ ′ ⊂ ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho^{(2)} was a destabilizing submodule with respect to ζ ⁡ ( + ) \upzeta(+) , it would also be a submodule of ρ \rho with ζ ⁡ ( + ) \upzeta(+) -slope greater than δ \updelta , which is impossible by the condition on ρ \rho . Similarly, if ρ ′ ⊂ ρ ( 1 ) \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho^{(1)} was destabilizing, then ρ ′ ⊕ ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{\prime}\oplus\rho^{(2)} , with its natural ℂ ​ Q \mathbb{C}Q -module structure, would have slope greater than δ \updelta . So we deduce that both ρ ( 1 ) \rho^{(1)} and ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} are semistable. ( 2 ) ⇒ ( 3 ) (2)\Rightarrow(3) Say that ρ ( 1 ) \rho^{(1)} and ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} are semistable. Let ρ ′ ⊂ ρ \rho^{\prime}\subset\rho be the maximal destabilizing submodule with respect to the stability condition ζ ⁡ ( + ) \upzeta(+) , i.e. the first non-trivial term in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho . If Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} is isomorphic to the Kronecker quiver, let ( a , b ) (a,b) be the dimension vector of ρ ′ \rho^{\prime} restricted to Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} . Since this restriction is a submodule of ρ ( 2 ) \rho^{(2)} we must have a ≤ b a\leq b . On the other hand, since ρ ′ \rho^{\prime} destabilizes ρ \rho we must have a ≥ b a\geq b . We deduce that a = b a=b , and so whichever quiver Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} is, the contribution of Q ( 2 ) Q^{(2)} to the slope of ρ ′ \rho^{\prime} is zero. Now assume that Q ( 1 ) Q^{(1)} is isomorphic to the Kronecker quiver. By the same argument, the dimension vector of ρ ′ \rho^{\prime} , restricted to Q ( 1 ) Q^{(1)} , must be constant. We deduce that the slope of ρ ′ \rho^{\prime} lies within I I . Applying the same argument to ρ / ρ ′ \rho/\rho^{\prime} , we see that the slope of all the subquotients in the Harder–Narasimhan filtration of ρ \rho belong to I I . Denote by L ′ L^{\prime} the lattice spanned by v 1 v_{1} and v 2 v_{2} .
