Exponentiating, we obtain an isomorphism of pro-unipotent algebraic groups Φ : G ^ ∘ → ≅ G ^ L ′ . \Phi\colon\hat{G}^{\circ}\xrightarrow{\cong}\hat{G}_{L^{\prime}}. Finally, we use the identity ( 104 ) to construct 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} . Let V = v 1 ω ⟂ ∩ v 2 ω ⟂ V=v_{1}^{\omega\perp}\cap v_{2}^{\omega\perp} . From ω ⁡ ( v 1 , v 2 ) ≠ 0 \omega(v_{1},v_{2})\neq 0 and v i ∈ v i ω ⟂ v_{i}\in v_{i}^{\omega\perp} it follows that V V has codimension 2. We define 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} to be the scattering diagram containing 𝔇 in \mathfrak{D}_{\In} as well as a wall (105) ( V − ℝ ≥ 0 ⋅ ( α ​ v 1 + β ​ v 2 ) , 𝔼 ⁡ ( − χ wt ​ ( BPS α ​ e + β ​ f lim ⁡ ( Q ) ) ​ z α ​ v 1 + β ​ v 2 ) ) \displaystyle(V-\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot(\upalpha v_{1}+\upbeta v_{2}),\EE(-\upchi_{\Wt}(\BPS^{\lim}_{\upalpha e+\upbeta f}(Q))z^{\upalpha v_{1}+\upbeta v_{2}})) for each α , β ∈ ℤ > 0 \upalpha,\upbeta\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} . Then V V is the only joint in 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , and consistency around this joint amounts to the equality (106) 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ m 1 + 1 ] t ​ z v 1 ) \displaystyle\EE(-[m_{1}+1]_{t}z^{v_{1}}) 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ m 2 + 1 ] t ​ z v 2 ) \displaystyle\EE(-[m_{2}+1]_{t}z^{v_{2}}) = \displaystyle= 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ m 2 + 1 ] t ​ z v 2 ) ​ ∏ ∞ → β / α 0 α ≠ 0 , β ≠ 0 𝔼 ⁡ ( − χ wt ​ ( BPS α ​ e + β ​ f lim ⁡ ( Q ) ) ​ z α ​ v 1 + β ​ v 2 ) ​ 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ m 1 + 1 ] t ​ z v 1 ) , \displaystyle\EE(-[m_{2}+1]_{t}z^{v_{2}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\infty\xrightarrow{\upbeta/\upalpha}0\\ \upalpha\neq 0,\upbeta\neq 0\end{subarray}}\EE(-\upchi_{\Wt}(\BPS^{\lim}_{\upalpha e+\upbeta f}(Q))z^{\upalpha v_{1}+\upbeta v_{2}})\EE(-[m_{1}+1]_{t}z^{v_{1}}), cf. Figure 7.4 where this corresponds to the equality of θ γ 1 , 𝔇 \uptheta_{\upgamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}} and θ γ 2 , 𝔇 \uptheta_{\upgamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}} . Finally, ( 106 ) follows from applying Φ \Phi to ( 104 ) and inverting each side. 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t m 1 ​ z v 1 ) \EE(-t^{m_{1}}z^{v_{1}}) 𝔼 ⁡ ( − t m 2 ​ z v 2 ) \EE(-t^{m_{2}}z^{v_{2}}) ⋅ \cdot ⋅ \cdot γ 1 \upgamma_{1} γ 2 \upgamma_{2} Figure 7.4. Sketch of 𝔇 \mathfrak{D} , with outgoing walls as in ( 105 ) living in the grey region. ∎ The substitution we used in the above proof in order to assume that m 1 , m 2 ∈ { 0 , 1 } m_{1},m_{2}\in\{0,1\} preserves the pL property but not the Lefschetz property, explaining why this argument does not yield preservation of Lefschetz type. To prove Conjecture 2.17 , it would be sufficient to have a version of Proposition 7.12 stating that if the two incoming walls carry elements of the form 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ r 1 ] t ​ z v 1 ) \EE(-[r_{1}]_{t}z^{v_{1}}) and 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ r 2 ] t ​ z v 2 ) \EE(-[r_{2}]_{t}z^{v_{2}}) for r 1 , r 2 ∈ ℤ > 0 r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} , then all outgoing walls can be chosen to carry elements of the form 𝔼 ⁡ ( − [ r ] t ​ z α ​ v 1 + β ​ v 2 ) \EE(-[r]_{t}z^{\upalpha v_{1}+\upbeta v_{2}}) for r , α , β ∈ ℤ > 0 r,\upalpha,\upbeta\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} . The reason we cannot easily adapt the proof of Proposition 7.12 to prove this statement is that for r r -Kronecker quivers with r > 2 r>2 there are non-primitive DT invariants, i.e. we can have Ω Kr ( r ) , ( n , n ) ( 1 , − 1 ) ≠ 0 \Omega_{\moKr^{(r)},(n,n)}^{(1,-1)}\neq 0 for n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 (see Examples 6.10 and 6.11 ). Appendix A Mutation invariance Here we prove the mutation invariance properties of the scattering diagrams, broken lines, and theta functions. The arguments are based on those of [ GHKK18 ] in the classical setting. A.1. Mutation invariance of the scattering diagram Let ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) be a compatible pair. Recall the map B 1 : N → M B_{1}\colon N\rightarrow M sending e ↦ B S ​ ( e , ⋅ ) e\mapsto B_{S}(e,\cdot) . Throughout the appendix we denote v k ≔ B 1 ​ ( e k ) v_{k}\coloneqq B_{1}(e_{k}) for k ∈ I k\in I . For k ∈ I ∖ F k\in I\setminus F , recall the piecewise-linear maps T k : M ℝ → M ℝ T_{k}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}} from ( 64 ), i.e., T k ​ ( m ) ≔ { m + ⟨ e k , m ⟩ ​ v k if ⟨ e k , m ⟩ ≥ 0 m otherwise. \displaystyle T_{k}(m)\coloneqq\begin{cases}m+\langle e_{k},m\rangle v_{k}&\mbox{if $\langle e_{k},m\rangle\geq 0$}\\ m&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases} Let ℋ k , + ≔ { m ∈ M ℝ : ⟨ e k , m ⟩ ≥ 0 } and ℋ k , − ≔ { m ∈ M ℝ : ⟨ e k , m ⟩ ≤ 0 } , \displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k,+}\coloneqq\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\langle e_{k},m\rangle\geq 0\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{H}_{k,-}\coloneqq\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\langle e_{k},m\rangle\leq 0\}, i.e., ℋ k , ± \mathcal{H}_{k,\pm} are the domains of linearity for T k T_{k} . Let T k , + T_{k,+} and T k , − T_{k,-} denote the integral linear functions on M ℝ M_{\mathbb{R}} obtained by extending T k | ℋ k , + T_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}_{k,+}} and T k | ℋ k , − T_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}_{k,-}} , respectively. In particular, T k , − = Id M ℝ T_{k,-}=\Id_{M_{\mathbb{R}}} . Note that each of T k , ± T_{k,\pm} induces a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module automorphism of Π ⁡ ( M ) ≔ ∏ m ∈ M 𝕜 ⁡ ( t 1 / D ) \Pi(M)\coloneqq\prod_{m\in M}\Bbbk(t^{1/D}) by taking the m m -factor to the T k , ± ​ ( m ) T_{k,\pm}(m) -factor. Each M M -graded algebra, Lie algebra, or pro-unipotent algebraic group we consider naturally embeds as an M M -graded 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module, 𝕜 ⁡ [ t ± 1 ] \Bbbk[t^{\pm 1}] -module, or set, respectively, into ∏ m ∈ M 𝕜 ⁡ ( t 1 / D ) \prod_{m\in M}\Bbbk(t^{1/D}) via ∑ m ∈ M a m ​ z m ↦ ( a m ) m \sum_{m\in M}a_{m}z^{m}\mapsto(a_{m})_{m} , i.e., the coefficient of z m z^{m} gives the m m -factor of ∏ m ∈ M 𝕜 ⁡ ( t 1 / D ) \prod_{m\in M}\Bbbk(t^{1/D}) . We can then define T k , ± ​ ( ∑ m ∈ M a m ​ z m ) ≔ ∑ m ∈ M a m ​ z T k , ± ​ ( m ) T_{k,\pm}(\sum_{m\in M}a_{m}z^{m})\coloneqq\sum_{m\in M}a_{m}z^{T_{k,\pm}(m)} , where the right-hand side is viewed as the element ( a m ) T k , ± ​ ( m ) ∈ ∏ m ∈ M 𝕜 ⁡ ( t 1 / D ) (a_{m})_{T_{k,\pm}(m)}\in\prod_{m\in M}\Bbbk(t^{1/D}) . In particular, since T k , ± T_{k,\pm} both respect Λ \Uplambda , they induce 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra isomorphisms T k , ± : 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] → 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] T_{k,\pm}\colon\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M]\rightarrow\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] . Let 𝔡 k \mathfrak{d}_{k} denote the wall ( e k ⟂ , Ψ t ​ ( z v k ) ) (e_{k}^{\perp},\Psi_{t}(z^{v_{k}})) in M ℝ M_{\mathbb{R}} . Let 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} denote the consistent scattering diagram 𝔇 𝒜 q \mathfrak{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{q}} defined in § 4.3 for the compatible pair ( S , Λ ) (S,\Uplambda) , and similarly, let 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} denote the analogous scattering diagram for the compatible pair ( μ k ​ ( S ) , Λ ) (\upmu_{k}(S),\Uplambda) . Note that while both scattering diagrams are in M ℝ M_{\mathbb{R}} , 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} is defined over the Lie algebra 𝔤 M S , 0 + , Λ \mathfrak{g}_{M_{S,0}^{+},\Uplambda} for M S , 0 + ≔ ( B 1 ​ ( N uf ) ∩ σ ) ∖ { 0 } M_{S,0}^{+}\coloneqq(B_{1}(N_{\uf})\cap\upsigma)\setminus\{0\} , where σ ⊂ M ℝ \upsigma\subset M_{\mathbb{R}} is the cone spanned by { v i = B 1 ( e i ) } i ∈ I ∖ F \{v_{i}=B_{1}(e_{i})\}_{i\in I\setminus F} , while 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} is defined over 𝔤 M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + , Λ \mathfrak{g}_{M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+},\Uplambda} for M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + ≔ ( B 1 ​ ( N uf ) ∩ μ k ​ ( σ ) ) ∖ { 0 } M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+}\coloneqq(B_{1}(N_{\uf})\cap\upmu_{k}(\upsigma))\setminus\{0\} , where μ k ​ ( σ ) ⊂ M ℝ \upmu_{k}(\upsigma)\subset M_{\mathbb{R}} is the cone spanned by { B 1 ​ ( μ k ​ ( e i ) ) } i ∈ I ∖ F \{B_{1}(\upmu_{k}(e_{i}))\}_{i\in I\setminus F} for μ k \upmu_{k} as in ( 4.2 ). The following is the quantum analog of [ GHKK18 , Thm. 1.24] . Proposition A.1 . Up to equivalence, the walls of 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} can be given as follows. For each 𝔡 ∈ 𝔇 S ∖ { 𝔡 k } \mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{S}\setminus\{\mathfrak{d}_{k}\} , we have one or two walls, given as ( T k ​ ( 𝔡 ∩ ℋ k , − ) , T k , − ​ ( f 𝔡 ) ) , ( T k ​ ( 𝔡 ∩ ℋ k , + ) , T k , + ​ ( f 𝔡 ) ) , \displaystyle(T_{k}(\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,-}),T_{k,-}(f_{\mathfrak{d}})),\hskip 18.06749pt(T_{k}(\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,+}),T_{k,+}(f_{\mathfrak{d}})), throwing out one of these if the support is of codimension at least 2 2 . The only other wall is 𝔡 k ′ ≔ ( e k ⟂ , Ψ t ​ ( z − v k ) ) . \displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{k}\coloneqq(e_{k}^{\perp},\Psi_{t}(z^{-v_{k}})). Here, if f 𝔡 = 1 + ∑ r = 1 ∞ a r ​ z r ​ v 𝔡 ∈ G ^ S ≔ exp ⁡ ( 𝔤 ^ M S , 0 + , Λ ) ⊂ ∏ m ∈ M 𝕜 ⁡ ( t 1 / D ) f_{\mathfrak{d}}=1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}a_{r}z^{rv_{\mathfrak{d}}}\in\hat{G}_{S}\coloneqq\exp(\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{M_{S,0}^{+},\Uplambda})\subset\prod_{m\in M}\Bbbk(t^{1/D}) , then by definition, T k , ± ​ ( f 𝔡 ) = 1 + ∑ r = 1 ∞ a r ​ z T k , ± ​ ( r ​ v 𝔡 ) ∈ ∏ m ∈ M 𝕜 ⁡ ( t 1 / D ) T_{k,\pm}(f_{\mathfrak{d}})=1+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}a_{r}z^{T_{k,\pm}(rv_{\mathfrak{d}})}\in\prod_{m\in M}\Bbbk(t^{1/D}) . The fact that this latter sum is actually contained in G ^ μ k ​ ( S ) ≔ exp ⁡ ( 𝔤 ^ M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + , Λ ) \hat{G}_{\upmu_{k}(S)}\coloneqq\exp(\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+},\Uplambda}) is not at all obvious. This technical issue is dealt with in [ GHKK18 ] using their Theorem 1.28. Fortunately, we can circumvent the need to generalize this theorem to the quantum setup, instead using positivity to recover the following directly from its classical analog: Lemma A.2 . The walls described in Proposition A.1 are well-defined and form a scattering diagram T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) in M ℝ M_{\mathbb{R}} over 𝔤 M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + , Λ \mathfrak{g}_{M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+},\Uplambda} . Proof. By Theorem 2.15 , all walls of 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} specialize to non-trivial walls of the corresponding classical scattering diagram with no cancellation being possible. Furthermore, this classical specialization clearly preserves the z z -exponents of all terms of the scattering functions. Thus, the fact that the classical specialization of T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) is a scattering diagram over lim t → 1 ( 𝔤 M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + , Λ ) \lim_{t\rightarrow 1}(\mathfrak{g}_{M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+},\Uplambda}) [ GHKK18 , Thm 1.24] implies the desired statement for the quantum scattering diagram. ∎ Proof of Proposition A.1 . We know from Lemma A.2 that T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) is a scattering diagram over 𝔤 M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + , Λ \mathfrak{g}_{M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+},\Uplambda} . We will now show that the incoming walls of T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) are the same as the incoming walls of 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} , and we will then show that T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) is consistent. Proposition A.1 then follows from the uniqueness statement of Theorem 2.13 . Step 1, incoming walls coincide: Using the equivalence of Example 2.8 (2), let us split up all walls ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) under consideration into their intersections ( 𝔡 ∩ ℋ k , + , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,+},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) and ( 𝔡 ∩ ℋ k , − , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,-},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) , throwing away any empty intersections (and keeping only one copy of 𝔡 k = 𝔡 k ∩ ℋ k , ± \mathfrak{d}_{k}=\mathfrak{d}_{k}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm} ). Now since T k , ± T_{k,\pm} are linear and injective, it is clear that a wall of the form ( 𝔡 ∩ ℋ k , ± , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) contains its direction − v 𝔡 -v_{\mathfrak{d}} if and only if T k ​ ( 𝔡 ∩ ℋ k , ± ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{d}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm}) contains its direction T k , ± ​ ( − v 𝔡 ) T_{k,\pm}(-v_{\mathfrak{d}}) . That is, T k T_{k} maps incoming walls to incoming walls and maps outgoing walls to outgoing walls. Next we check that the functions on the incoming walls of T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) match those on the incoming walls of 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} . Consider an incoming wall ( 𝔡 i ∩ ℋ k , ± , f 𝔡 i ) (\mathfrak{d}_{i}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm},f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}) of the modified 𝔇 S , in \mathfrak{D}_{S,\In} , where 𝔡 i = e i ⟂ \mathfrak{d}_{i}=e_{i}^{\perp} and f 𝔡 i = Ψ t ​ ( z v i ) f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=\Psi_{t}(z^{v_{i}}) . The corresponding wall in T k ​ ( 𝔇 S , in ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S,\In}) is ( T k ​ ( 𝔡 i ∩ ℋ k , ± ) , Ψ t ​ ( z T k ​ ( v i ) ) ) (T_{k}(\mathfrak{d}_{i}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm}),\Psi_{t}(z^{T_{k}(v_{i})})) if i ≠ k i\neq k , or ( 𝔡 k , Ψ t ​ ( z − v i ) ) (\mathfrak{d}_{k},\Psi_{t}(z^{-v_{i}})) if i = k i=k . In any case, ( 65 ) implies that this is the same as ( T k ​ ( 𝔡 i ∩ ℋ k , ± ) , Ψ t ​ ( z B 1 ​ ( μ k ​ ( e i ) ) ) ) (T_{k}(\mathfrak{d}_{i}\cap\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm}),\Psi_{t}(z^{B_{1}(\upmu_{k}(e_{i}))})) , which is the corresponding initial wall of 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) , in \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S),\In} . We have thus shown that the incoming walls of T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) and 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} coincide. Step 2, T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) is consistent: Here we follow the strategy of [ GHKK18 , Proof of Thm. 1.24, Step II] . It suffices to check consistency of T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) around joints contained in e k ⟂ e_{k}^{\perp} because this is the only locus where T k T_{k} fails to be linear. For non-initial walls ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) ∈ 𝔇 S ∖ 𝔇 S , in (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\in\mathfrak{D}_{S}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{S,\In} , the vector v 𝔡 ∈ σ v_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\upsigma is not contained in an extremal ray of σ \upsigma , i.e., is not equal to a multiple of any e i e_{i} . So 𝔡 = 𝔡 k \mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{d}_{k} is the only wall with v 𝔡 Λ ⟂ = e k ⟂ v_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\Uplambda\perp}=e_{k}^{\perp} . I.e., 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} has no walls contained in e k ⟂ e_{k}^{\perp} other than 𝔡 k \mathfrak{d}_{k} . Now, let 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} be one such joint in e k ⟂ e_{k}^{\perp} . Let γ \upgamma denote a small closed path around 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} based in ℋ k , − \mathcal{H}_{k,-} . To any finite order, we can consider a path γ = γ 4 ∘ γ 3 ∘ γ 2 ∘ γ 1 \upgamma=\upgamma_{4}\circ\upgamma_{3}\circ\upgamma_{2}\circ\upgamma_{1} such that: • γ 1 \upgamma_{1} is a path starting in ℋ k , − \mathcal{H}_{k,-} and immediately crossing 𝔡 k \mathfrak{d}_{k} , crossing no other walls, • γ 2 \upgamma_{2} crosses every wall containing 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} with interior in ℋ k , + \mathcal{H}_{k,+} , but crosses no other walls, • γ 3 \upgamma_{3} is a path starting in ℋ k , + \mathcal{H}_{k,+} and immediately crossing 𝔡 k \mathfrak{d}_{k} , crossing no other walls, and • γ 4 \upgamma_{4} crosses every wall containing 𝔧 \mathfrak{j} with interior in ℋ k , − \mathcal{H}_{k,-} , returning to the initial point of γ 1 \upgamma_{1} . It is sufficient to check that (107) θ γ , T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) = θ γ , 𝔇 S , \displaystyle\uptheta_{\upgamma,T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S})}=\uptheta_{\upgamma,\mathfrak{D}_{S}}, since we know the latter is the identity because 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} is consistent. We have θ γ 1 , T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) \displaystyle{\uptheta_{\upgamma_{1},T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S})}} = θ γ 1 , 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \displaystyle={\uptheta_{\upgamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)}}} θ γ 2 , T k , + ​ ( 𝔇 S ) \displaystyle{\uptheta_{\upgamma_{2},T_{k,+}(\mathfrak{D}_{S})}} = T k , + ​ ( θ γ 2 , 𝔇 S ) \displaystyle=T_{k,+}(\uptheta_{\upgamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}_{S}}) θ γ 3 , T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) \displaystyle\uptheta_{\upgamma_{3},T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S})} = θ γ 3 , 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \displaystyle=\uptheta_{\upgamma_{3},\mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)}} θ γ 4 , T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) \displaystyle\uptheta_{\upgamma_{4},T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S})} = θ γ 4 , 𝔇 S . \displaystyle=\uptheta_{\upgamma_{4},\mathfrak{D}_{S}}. Recall that the existence of a compatible Λ \Uplambda implies that ker ⁡ ( B S , 1 ) ∩ M S , 0 + = ∅ \ker(B_{S,1})\cap M_{S,0}^{+}=\emptyset , and similarly ker ⁡ ( B μ k ​ ( S ) , 1 ) ∩ M μ k ​ ( S ) , 0 + = ∅ \ker(B_{\upmu_{k}(S),1})\cap M_{\upmu_{k}(S),0}^{+}=\emptyset . It follows that elements of G ^ S \hat{G}_{S} and G ^ μ k ​ ( S ) \hat{G}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} are determined by their conjugation actions on 𝕜 t , σ Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \Bbbk_{t,\upsigma}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket and 𝕜 t , μ k ​ ( σ ) Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \Bbbk_{t,\upmu_{k}(\upsigma)}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket , respectively, and in fact, these actions are already determined by their restrictions to monomials z m z^{m} with m ∈ ℋ k , − m\in\mathcal{H}_{k,-} . Suppose f ∈ 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] f\in\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] . Let θ 𝔡 k ≔ θ γ 1 , 𝔇 S = Ψ t ​ ( z v k ) \uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}_{k}}\coloneqq\uptheta_{\upgamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{S}}=\Psi_{t}(z^{v_{k}}) , and let θ 𝔡 k ′ ≔ θ γ 1 , 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) = Ψ t ​ ( z − v k ) − 1 \uptheta_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{k}}\coloneqq\uptheta_{\upgamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)}}=\Psi_{t}(z^{-v_{k}})^{-1} . Mutation invariance of broken lines We continue to work with the map T k : M ℝ → M ℝ T_{k}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}} and the induced maps T k , ± : 𝕜 t ​ [ M ] → 𝕜 t ​ [ M ] T_{k,\pm}\colon\Bbbk_{t}[M]\rightarrow\Bbbk_{t}[M] as in § A.1 . We write ϑ p , 𝒬 S \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}^{S} to indicate the theta function ϑ p , 𝒬 \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}} associated to the consistent scattering diagram 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} defined at the start of § A.1 , and similarly we write ϑ p , 𝒬 μ k ​ ( S ) \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}^{\upmu_{k}(S)} for the theta function defined with respect to 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} . Proposition A.3 . For any p ∈ M p\in M and generic 𝒬 ∈ M ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in M_{\mathbb{R}} , T k T_{k} defines a bijection between broken lines with respect to 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) and broken lines with respect to 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} with ends ( T k ​ ( p ) , T k ​ ( 𝒬 ) ) (T_{k}(p),T_{k}(\mathcal{Q})) . If z m z^{m} is the final monomial of one such broken line for 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} , then the final monomial of the corresponding broken line for 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} is z T k , + ​ ( m ) z^{T_{k,+}(m)} if 𝒬 ∈ ℋ k , + \mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{k,+} and z T k , − ​ ( m ) z^{T_{k,-}(m)} if 𝒬 ∈ ℋ k , − \mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{k,-} . Hence, (109) T k , ± ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 S ) = ϑ T k ​ ( p ) , T k ​ ( 𝒬 ) μ k ​ ( S ) , \displaystyle T_{k,\pm}(\vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}}^{S})=\vartheta_{T_{k}(p),T_{k}(\mathcal{Q})}^{\upmu_{k}(S)}, where the sign in the subscript of T k , ± T_{k,\pm} on the left-hand side is determined by 𝒬 ∈ ℋ k , ± \mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm} . We follow the proof of the analogous classical result, [ GHKK18 , Prop. 3.6] . Proof. Given a broken line γ \upgamma with ends ( p , 𝒬 ) (p,\mathcal{Q}) whose underlying map is γ : ( − ∞ , 0 ] → M ℝ \upgamma\colon(-\infty,0]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}} , the underlying map for T k ​ ( γ ) T_{k}(\upgamma) is of course taken to be T k ∘ γ T_{k}\circ\upgamma . Subdivide the domains of linearity of γ \upgamma so that each lies entirely in either ℋ k , + \mathcal{H}_{k,+} or ℋ k , − \mathcal{H}_{k,-} . For a domain of linearity L ⊂ ℋ k , ± L\subset\mathcal{H}_{k,\pm} , T k T_{k} takes the attached monomial c L ​ z m L c_{L}z^{m_{L}} to T k , ± ​ ( c L ​ z m L ) T_{k,\pm}(c_{L}z^{m_{L}}) . We wish to check that this gives a broken line. Let us assume that the representative of 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} is chosen so that each scattering function is of the form Ψ t ​ ( t 2 ​ a ​ z v ) \Psi_{t}(t^{2a}z^{v}) or Ψ t 2 r ​ ( t 2 r ​ ( 2 ​ a + 1 ) ​ z 2 r ​ v ) \Psi_{t^{2^{r}}}(t^{2^{r}(2a+1)}z^{2^{r}v}) for various a ∈ ℤ a\in\mathbb{Z} , r ∈ ℤ ≥ 0 r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} , and v ∈ M S , 0 + v\in M_{S,0}^{+} , as in Theorem 2.15 . Let G ^ S ∘ \hat{G}_{S}^{\circ} denote the subgroup of G ^ S \hat{G}_{S} generated by the scattering functions of 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} , so Lemma A.2 ensures that T k , ± T_{k,\pm} take G ^ S ∘ \hat{G}_{S}^{\circ} into G ^ μ k ​ ( S ) \hat{G}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} . For ( 𝔡 , f 𝔡 ) (\mathfrak{d},f_{\mathfrak{d}}) a wall of 𝔇 S \mathfrak{D}_{S} , p ∈ M p\in M , and ϵ = sgn ⁡ ( Λ ⁡ ( v 𝔡 , m ) ) \epsilon=\sign(\Uplambda(v_{\mathfrak{d}},m)) , we see that (110) T k , ± ​ ( Ad f 𝔡 ϵ ⁡ ( z p ) ) = Ad T k , ± ​ ( f 𝔡 ) ϵ ⁡ ( T k , ± ​ ( z p ) ) . \displaystyle T_{k,\pm}(\Ad_{f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\epsilon}}(z^{p}))=\Ad_{T_{k,\pm}(f_{\mathfrak{d}})^{\epsilon}}(T_{k,\pm}(z^{p})). Indeed, by ( 23 ), Ad f 𝔡 ϵ ⁡ ( z p ) \Ad_{f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\epsilon}}(z^{p}) and Ad T k , ± ​ ( f 𝔡 ) ϵ ⁡ ( z p ) \Ad_{T_{k,\pm}(f_{\mathfrak{d}})^{\epsilon}}(z^{p}) are both contained in 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] \Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] , so the equality follows from the fact that, since T k , ± T_{k,\pm} respects Λ \Uplambda , the maps T k , ± : G ^ S ∘ → G ^ μ k ​ ( S ) T_{k,\pm}\colon\hat{G}_{S}^{\circ}\rightarrow\hat{G}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} and T k , ± : 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] → 𝕜 t Λ ​ [ M ] T_{k,\pm}\colon\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M]\rightarrow\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M] are group and algebra homomorphisms which intertwine the actions of the groups on the algebras (cf. the proof of Lemma 4.10 ). As a result, to show that T k ​ ( γ ) T_{k}(\upgamma) is a broken line for 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)} , it remains to check what happens when γ \upgamma crosses e k ⟂ e_{k}^{\perp} . Let L 1 , L 2 L_{1},L_{2} be consecutive refined domains of linearity of γ \upgamma which lie on opposite sides of e k ⟂ e_{k}^{\perp} , and let c i ​ z m i c_{i}z^{m_{i}} denote the monomial attached to L i L_{i} . For L 1 ⊂ ℋ k , − L_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{k,-} , we have Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) > 0 \Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})>0 , and so c 2 ​ z m 2 c_{2}z^{m_{2}} is any term in (111) Ad Ψ t ​ ( z v k ) ⁡ ( c 1 ​ z m 1 ) = c 1 ​ z m 1 ​ ∏ n = 1 | Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) | ( 1 + t sgn ⁡ ( Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ) ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ z v k ) \displaystyle\Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{v_{k}})}(c_{1}z^{m_{1}})=c_{1}z^{m_{1}}\prod_{n=1}^{|\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})|}\left(1+t^{\sign(\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1}))(2n-1)}z^{v_{k}}\right) where the equality is a special case of ( 20 ), with ϵ = 1 \epsilon=1 . Applying T k , + T_{k,+} , we have that T k , + ​ ( c 2 ​ z m 2 ) T_{k,+}(c_{2}z^{m_{2}}) is a term in c 1 ​ z m 1 − Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ​ v k ​ ∏ n = 1 | Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) | ( 1 + t sgn ⁡ ( Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ) ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ z v k ) . \displaystyle c_{1}z^{m_{1}-\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})v_{k}}\prod_{n=1}^{|\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})|}\left(1+t^{\sign(\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1}))(2n-1)}z^{v_{k}}\right). Applying the same manipulations which lead up to ( 108 ), we find that this is equal to c 1 ​ z m 1 ​ ∏ n = 1 | Λ ⁡ ( − v k , m 1 ) | ( t sgn ⁡ ( Λ ⁡ ( − v k , m 1 ) ) ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ z − v k + 1 ) = Ad Ψ t ​ ( z − v k ) ⁡ ( c 1 ​ z m 1 ) . \displaystyle c_{1}z^{m_{1}}\prod_{n=1}^{|\Uplambda(-v_{k},m_{1})|}\left(t^{\sign(\Uplambda(-v_{k},m_{1}))(2n-1)}z^{-v_{k}}+1\right)=\Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{-v_{k}})}(c_{1}z^{m_{1}}). Since the right-hand side is the corresponding wall-crossing for 𝔇 μ k ​ ( S ) = T k ​ ( 𝔇 S ) \mathfrak{D}_{\upmu_{k}(S)}=T_{k}(\mathfrak{D}_{S}) , it follows that T k ​ ( γ ) T_{k}(\upgamma) does in fact satisfy the correct bending rule. Similarly, if we instead had L 1 ⊂ ℋ k , + L_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{k,+} , then T k , − ​ ( c 2 ​ z m 2 ) = c 2 ​ z m 2 T_{k,-}(c_{2}z^{m_{2}})=c_{2}z^{m_{2}} could be any term in the right-hand side of ( 111 ) (although the sign of Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) \Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1}) is now − 1 -1 ). On the other hand, if we apply T k T_{k} before the wall-crossing, we would have to apply T k , + T_{k,+} to c 1 ​ z m 1 c_{1}z^{m_{1}} , yielding c 1 ​ z m 1 − Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ​ v k c_{1}z^{m_{1}-\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})v_{k}} . One then computes that Ad Ψ t ​ ( z − v k ) ⁡ ( c 1 ​ z m 1 − Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ​ v k ) = c 1 ​ z m 1 − Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ​ v k ​ ∏ k = 1 | Λ ⁡ ( − v k , m ) | ( 1 + t sgn ⁡ ( Λ ⁡ ( − v k , m ) ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ z − v k ) , \displaystyle\Ad_{\Psi_{t}(z^{-v_{k}})}(c_{1}z^{m_{1}-\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})v_{k}})=c_{1}z^{m_{1}-\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})v_{k}}\prod_{k=1}^{|\Uplambda(-v_{k},m)|}\left(1+t^{\sign(\Uplambda(-v_{k},m))(2k-1)}z^{-v_{k}}\right), and applying similar manipulations as before, noting that now Λ ⁡ ( v k , m ) < 0 \Uplambda(v_{k},m)<0 so z − Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) ​ v k = z | Λ ⁡ ( v k , m 1 ) | ​ v k z^{-\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})v_{k}}=z^{|\Uplambda(v_{k},m_{1})|v_{k}} , we find that this indeed equals the right-hand side of ( 111 ), as desired. We have thus shown that T k T_{k} takes broken lines to broken lines. That this is a bijection follows from noting that T k − 1 : M ℝ → M ℝ T_{k}^{-1}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}} induces the inverse map on broken lines. ∎ A.3. Mutation invariance of theta functions Recall the linear maps ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 \psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}} (depending on generic 𝒬 ∈ M ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in M_{\mathbb{R}} ) and ψ ȷ → ≔ ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}\coloneqq\psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}} as defined in ( 67 ) and ( 68 ), respectively. From now on, we will denote these by ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒜 \psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{A}} and ψ ȷ → 𝒜 \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}} . Applying this to S prin S^{\prin} , we obtain ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒜 prin \psi^{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}}_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}} and ψ ȷ → 𝒜 prin \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}} mapping M ℝ prin → M ℝ prin M^{\prin}_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M^{\prin}_{\mathbb{R}} (in § 4.7.2 , ψ ȷ → 𝒜 prin \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}} was denoted ψ ȷ → prin \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\prin} ). By restriction to N ℝ ′ = ξ ⁡ ( N ℝ ) ⊂ M ℝ prin N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}=\upxi(N_{\mathbb{R}})\subset M^{\prin}_{\mathbb{R}} , we obtain linear automorphisms ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒳 \psi^{\mathcal{X}}_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}} and ψ ȷ → 𝒳 : N ℝ → N ℝ \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{X}}\colon N_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow N_{\mathbb{R}} which induce maps on Π ⁡ ( N ) \Pi(N) and 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra automorphisms of 𝕜 t B ​ [ N ] \Bbbk_{t}^{B}[N] . More directly, let T j S , 𝒳 ​ ( n ) ≔ { n + B ⁡ ( n , e j ) ​ e j if B ⁡ ( n , e j ) ≥ 0 , n otherwise , \displaystyle T_{j}^{S,\mathcal{X}}(n)\coloneqq\begin{cases}n+B(n,e_{j})e_{j}&\mbox{if $B(n,e_{j})\geq 0$},\\ n&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases} and then let (112) T ȷ → 𝒳 = T S ȷ → s , 𝒳 j s ∘ ⋯ ∘ T S ȷ → 1 , 𝒳 j 1 : N ℝ → N ℝ . \displaystyle T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{X}}=T^{S_{\vec{\jmath}_{s}},\mathcal{X}}_{j_{s}}\circ\cdots\circ T^{S_{\vec{\jmath}_{1}},\mathcal{X}}_{j_{1}}\colon N_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow N_{\mathbb{R}}. Then ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒳 \psi^{\mathcal{X}}_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}} is obtained by restricting T ȷ → 𝒳 T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{X}} to a neighborhood of 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} and extending linearly. We note that the definition as the restriction of ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒜 prin \psi^{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}}_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}} has the advantage of easily applying to generic 𝒬 ∈ M ℝ prin \mathcal{Q}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prin} , not just generic 𝒬 ∈ N ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in N_{\mathbb{R}} . Corollary A.4 . Let 𝒱 \mathcal{V} denote 𝒜 \mathcal{A} or 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . Let S S be a seed, and if 𝒱 = 𝒜 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A} , pick a compatible Λ \Uplambda . Then for any tuple ȷ → ∈ ( I ∖ F ) s \vec{\jmath}\in(I\setminus F)^{s} and any generic 𝒬 ∈ L 𝒱 , ℝ \mathcal{Q}\in L_{\mathcal{V},\mathbb{R}} , ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒱 \psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{V}} induces a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra isomorphism ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒱 : ι 𝒬 ​ ( 𝒱 q can , S ) ⟶ ∼ ι T ȷ → 𝒱 ​ ( 𝒬 ) ​ ( 𝒱 q can , S ȷ → ) \displaystyle\psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{V}}\colon\upiota_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can,S})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\upiota_{T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{Q})}(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can,S_{\vec{\jmath}}}) with ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒱 ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 S ) = ϑ T ȷ → 𝒱 ​ ( p ) , T ȷ → 𝒱 ​ ( 𝒬 ) S ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\vartheta^{S}_{p,\mathcal{Q}})=\vartheta^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}_{T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}(p),T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{Q})} . In particular, ψ ȷ → 𝒱 ​ ( ϑ p ) ≔ ϑ T ȷ → 𝒱 ​ ( p ) \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\vartheta_{p})\coloneqq\vartheta_{T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}(p)} defines a 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -algebra isomorphism ψ ȷ → 𝒱 : 𝒱 q can , S ⟶ ∼ 𝒱 q can , S ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}\colon\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\longrightarrow$}}\limits^{\sim}}\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\can,S_{\vec{\jmath}}} . Similarly if we replace each instance of can \can with mid \midd . Proof. Take 𝒱 = 𝒜 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A} . The claim that ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒜 ​ ( ϑ m , 𝒬 ) \psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\vartheta_{m,\mathcal{Q}}) and ϑ T ȷ → 𝒜 ​ ( m ) , T ȷ → 𝒜 ​ ( 𝒬 ) \vartheta_{T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}}(m),T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{Q})} are equal in ∏ n ∈ N 𝕜 t \prod_{n\in N}\Bbbk_{t} follows from applying ( 109 ) inductively. The fact that the isomorphism respects the structure constants follows from the mutation invariance of broken lines in Proposition A.3 because, by Proposition 3.3 , the broken lines directly determine the structure constants. However, the topological structures of 𝒜 q can , S \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S} and 𝒜 q can , S ȷ → \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S_{\vec{\jmath}}} are different, so a bijection between the formal bases respecting the structure constants is not sufficient to conclude that this gives an isomorphism. To prove this, it suffices to consider the case ȷ → = ( j ) \vec{\jmath}=(j) since the isomorphisms can then be applied inductively. We have σ S , 𝒜 = \displaystyle\upsigma_{S,\mathcal{A}}= ℝ ≥ 0 ⟨ B 1 ( e i ) : i ∈ I ∖ F ⟩ ⊂ M ℝ \displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle B_{1}(e_{i})\colon i\in I\setminus F\rangle\subset M_{\mathbb{R}} σ S j , 𝒜 = \displaystyle\upsigma_{S_{j},\mathcal{A}}= ℝ ≥ 0 ⟨ B 1 ( μ j ( e i ) ) : i ∈ I ∖ F ⟩ ⊂ M ℝ . \displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle B_{1}(\upmu_{j}(e_{i}))\colon i\in I\setminus F\rangle\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}. Note that both cones are contained in the convex (but not strongly convex) cone σ S , S j ≔ ℝ ≥ 0 ⟨ B 1 ( e i ) : i ∈ I ∖ ( F ∪ { j } ) ⟩ + ℝ B 1 ( e j ) . \upsigma_{S,S_{j}}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle B_{1}(e_{i})\colon i\in I\setminus(F\cup\{j\})\rangle+\mathbb{R}B_{1}(e_{j}). We can define 𝕜 σ S , S j , t Λ ⟦ M ⟧ ≔ 𝕜 t Λ [ M ] ⊗ 𝕜 t Λ [ M ⊕ S , S j ] 𝕜 t Λ ⟦ M ⊕ S , S j ⟧ \Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,S_{j}},t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket\coloneqq\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M]\otimes_{\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M^{\oplus_{S,S_{j}}}]}\Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M^{\oplus_{{}_{S,S_{j}}}}\rrbracket , where M ⊕ S , S j ≔ M ∩ σ S , S j M^{\oplus_{S,S_{j}}}\coloneqq M\cap\upsigma_{S,S_{j}} . Here, 𝕜 t Λ ⟦ M ⊕ S , S j ⟧ \Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M^{\oplus_{{}_{S,S_{j}}}}\rrbracket is the completion of 𝕜 t Λ [ M ⊕ S , S j ] \Bbbk_{t}^{\Uplambda}[M^{\oplus_{S,S_{j}}}] with respect to the maximal monomial ideal generated by z m z^{m} with m ∈ M + S , S j ≔ M ⊕ S , S j ∖ M × S , S j m\in M^{+_{S,S_{j}}}\coloneqq M^{\oplus_{S,S_{j}}}\setminus M^{\times_{S,S_{j}}} , where M × S , S j M^{\times_{S,S_{j}}} is the monoid of invertible elements of M ⊕ S , S j M^{\oplus_{S,S_{j}}} . We see that 𝒜 q can , S \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S} and 𝒜 q can , S j \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S_{j}} are both naturally contained in 𝕜 σ S , S j , t Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,S_{j}},t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket — between any two instances of a broken line γ \upgamma crossing e j ⟂ e_{j}^{\perp} , there must be an instance of γ \upgamma crossing a wall 𝔡 \mathfrak{d} with v 𝔡 ∈ M + S , S j v_{\mathfrak{d}}\in M^{+_{S,S_{j}}} , so the completion in the ± e j \pm e_{j} direction is not necessary. Furthermore, since T j T_{j} preserves M + S , S j M^{+_{S,S_{j}}} , it acts via homeomorphism and automorphism on 𝕜 σ S , S j , t Λ ​ ⟦ M ⟧ \Bbbk_{\upsigma_{S,S_{j}},t}^{\Uplambda}\llbracket M\rrbracket viewed as a topological 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -module. Since this action takes ι 𝒬 j ​ ( 𝒜 q can , S ) \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}_{j}}(\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S}) to ι T j ​ ( 𝒬 j ) ​ ( 𝒜 q can , S j ) \upiota_{T_{j}(\mathcal{Q}_{j})}(\mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S_{j}}) (for 𝒬 j ∈ 𝒞 j \mathcal{Q}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{j} ), it identifies 𝒜 q can , S \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S} and 𝒜 q can , S j \mathcal{A}_{q}^{\can,S_{j}} as 𝕜 t \Bbbk_{t} -modules which now have the same topology. The claim follows. For the 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -version, we first consider the compatible pair ( S prin , Λ prin ) (S^{\prin},\Uplambda^{\prin}) for Λ prin \Uplambda^{\prin} as in ( 49 ) and recall the map ξ \upxi of Lemma 4.1 . By construction, ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒜 prin ∘ ξ = ξ ∘ ψ ȷ → , 𝒬 𝒳 \psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{A}^{\prin}}\circ\upxi=\upxi\circ\psi_{\vec{\jmath},\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathcal{X}} . The 𝒳 \mathcal{X} -version of the result thus follows from the 𝒜 prin \mathcal{A}^{\prin} -version, and this is a special case of the 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -version above. For the final statement, we just have to check that Θ 𝒱 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd} is preserved by T ȷ → 𝒱 T_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}} . This again follows from Proposition A.3 since Θ 𝒱 mid \Theta_{\mathcal{V}}^{\midd} is defined in terms of the finiteness of certain sets of broken lines. ∎ A.3.1. The formal quantum upper cluster algebra Let 𝒱 \mathcal{V} denote 𝒜 \mathcal{A} or 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . For 𝒬 ∈ C S ȷ → + ⊂ 𝒞 \mathcal{Q}\in C_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+}\subset\mathcal{C} (in M ℝ M_{\mathbb{R}} for 𝒱 = 𝒜 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A} or M ℝ prin M_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prin} for 𝒱 = 𝒳 \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{X} ), denote ι 𝒬 \upiota_{\mathcal{Q}} by ι ȷ → \upiota_{\vec{\jmath}} . Recall that ψ ȷ → 𝒱 ∘ ι ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}\circ\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}} agrees with μ ȷ → 𝒱 \upmu^{\mathcal{V}}_{\vec{\jmath}} on 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} . Motivated by this, we say that an element f ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S f\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} satisfies the formal Laurent phenomenon if ψ ȷ → 𝒱 ∘ ι ȷ → ​ ( f ) ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S ȷ → \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}\circ\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}}(f)\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} for each ȷ → \vec{\jmath} . The algebra of such f ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S f\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} is what we call the formal quantum upper cluster algebra , denoted 𝒱 ^ q up \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{\up} . Note that the cluster atlas { ι ȷ → } ȷ → \{\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}}\}_{\vec{\jmath}} can naturally be viewed as an atlas on 𝒱 ^ q up \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{\up} . Since ( ψ ȷ → 𝒱 ) − 1 ​ ( 𝒱 q S ȷ → ) = 𝒱 q S ȷ → (\psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}})^{-1}(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}})=\mathcal{V}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} , we can view 𝒱 q up \mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up} as 𝒱 q up = ⋂ ȷ → ι ȷ → − 1 ​ ( 𝒱 q S ȷ → ) ⊂ 𝒱 ^ q S . \displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up}=\bigcap_{\vec{\jmath}}\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}}^{-1}(\mathcal{V}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}})\subset\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S}. Thus, 𝒱 q up ⊂ 𝒱 ^ q up . \displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{q}^{\up}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{\up}. Let p ∈ L p\in L be arbitrary. For 𝒬 0 ∈ C S + \mathcal{Q}_{0}\in C_{S}^{+} , we have ϑ p , 𝒬 0 S ∈ 𝒱 ^ q S \vartheta^{S}_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S} by construction. If 𝒬 ȷ → \mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}} is instead in some other C S ȷ → + C_{S_{\vec{\jmath}}}^{+} , then it follows from Corollary A.4 that ψ ȷ → 𝒱 ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 ȷ → S ) \psi_{\vec{\jmath}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\vartheta^{S}_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}}) is in 𝒱 ^ q S ȷ → \widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{S_{\vec{\jmath}}} . Since ϑ p , 𝒬 ȷ → S = ι ȷ → ​ ( ϑ p , 𝒬 0 S ) \vartheta^{S}_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{\vec{\jmath}}}=\upiota_{\vec{\jmath}}(\vartheta^{S}_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{0}}) , it follows that ϑ p , 𝒬 0 ∈ 𝒱 ^ q up \vartheta_{p,\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{q}^{\up} . I.e., while the Laurent phenomenon holds for all cluster monomials, this formal version of the Laurent phenomenon holds for all theta functions.
